# Village builder: game design snapshot v0.4

Nov 3, 2026 · @Kim

## Status of this snapshot

Version 0.4 begins on 3 November 2026, when `docs/architecture/target-v2.md` became the architecture reference and Kim chose to grow the game
in vertical slices (`docs/slices.md`). It is a skeleton: every point of version 0.3 is carried over with its status, in a new order of
sections, and Kim's decisions of 3 November are added. Points those decisions change are marked **Under revision**, with the slice whose
plan task rewrites them; until then they stand as written. Sections marked "to be written" are filled in by that slice's plan. It is a
reference for later work, not the truth: where it and the game differ, either may be wrong, and any point can be reopened.

Each version is its own document and is left unchanged once a newer one exists: version 0.3 is `docs/design-v0.3.md`.

Implementation details (rendering, code structure, performance) are left out: the architecture is target-v2, and the budget and the rules
for cost are in `docs/cost.md` and `AGENTS.md`. Numbers are design targets or starting values to tune, not fixed values.

| Label | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Decided | Stated by Kim |
| Agreed | Proposed in planning and accepted, or adopted without objection |
| Proposed | Suggested, not yet accepted |
| Open | Not decided |
| Under revision | Decided or Agreed, but changed by a later decision; it stands as written until the named slice's plan rewrites it |

**Where version 0.3's sections went.**

New in v0.4: section 9 (Exchange, goodwill and obligations), 12 (Institutions and the world beyond), 13 (Balance loops and targets), and
the subsections "to be written" in 2, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10 and 11.

## 1. Vision and scope

A village builder on a generated hex archipelago, played at the level of individual pawns with family histories, for about 1,000 living pawns on a medium map.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Genre | Village builder and colony simulation; pawns plan their own work | Decided |
| Population | About 1,000 living pawns on a medium map is the reference for the late game and its budget; a large map may reach about 2,000, at half the speed. Every pawn is individually represented; balance loops, not a cap, hold the number (see Balance loops and targets) | Decided, 3 Nov (was: up to about 1,000 pawns) |
| Setting | Medieval Nordic peasant society, with inspiration from medieval Sweden. Swedish and continental customs may be mixed: history is the source of systems and of their plausibility, and where the two part, engaging play decides. The founders scenario is a frontier settlement, growing faster than a settled medieval society would; other starting scenarios begin elsewhere | Decided; mixing, and the frontier, Decided 9 Nov (Kim, gameplay review) |
| Look | Naturalistic, painterly hex terrain; medieval Swedish farmsteads | Decided |
| Society | Families, inheritance and customs that shift over generations | Decided |
| Pace | A game about incremental progress; one run lasts 40–80 hours | Decided |
| Start | Several starting scenarios; by default the founders arrive to almost unprepared land and spend the first year making fields and meadows | Decided |
| Player role | The player inhabits one pawn as its source of commands (Commands, direct control) and lives a life the game does not prescribe: works and marries, inherits or leaves, takes service, clears land, argues at the meeting with the pawn's standing, and later learns a craft, trades at the fair, joins the Church or moves to a town. The pawn keeps its needs and the rules every pawn has, and the world goes on without the player. The player is the line, not the person or the farm (Kim, 17 and 18 Nov, VB-1121): when its pawn dies, and only then, the player goes on as the pawn's successor by its village's inheritance customs (Which pawn); with none the game ends. There is no fixed score: the player picks ambitions (hold three farms, change a custom, found a village, become a master household, leave service and hold land), and the chronicle shows their progress; the line's standing over time is one of them. The player should be able to influence the world a lot, through all the systems. The player steers what its pawn does, and through the pawn's position whatever that position commands: as head of a household it directs the household itself (where the next field is made, what the season's work is, whom its children marry), as any holder does but by the player's choice (see The player and what they see). Slice 2 (backlog VB-079) plans it | Decided, 3 Nov (replaces the family line of 30 Oct); its ambitions postponed, 7 Nov (see Ambitions); steering by position, 9 Nov |
| Which pawn | At the start, any living pawn, a child too, chosen on the game itself once the land is raised (Starting a game, in The player, commands and views; was: from the pawn panel); each scenario suggests one (a holder in founders and heirs). One rule of who may be played, wherever the player takes a pawn. In life nothing changes whom the player plays: a pawn whose farm passes on in its lifetime is played on, retired. At its death the player goes on as its successor: the first of its line by its village's inheritance customs (who can inherit, which child, a daughter's place), worked out beforehand and shown on the played pawn's sheet, not chosen from a list; for any pawn, landed or not. The line, not the holding: the widow's right to the farm does not make her the successor, and a successor's own farm, its refusal of the inheritance or its being passed over for the holding do not unmake it. A child successor is played as a child. The line reaches as far as a farm's ranking does (descendants to four generations, siblings and theirs, the parents' siblings and theirs); with no one of it alive the game ends, the break is recorded and the end-game summary is shown | Decided, 17 and 18 Nov (Kim; VB-1121; was, 3 Nov: the player chose among kin, household and close ties, the heir first, else any adult) |
| Ambitions | Read from the state and the record of events; they grant nothing. Postponed (Kim, 7 Nov): the idea came from an agent and is tentative; it is designed late, since nothing else depends on it, and slice 2 builds none | Decided, 3 Nov; postponed, 7 Nov |
| Scale of society | A society at five scales: the pawn, the household, the settlement (village, market place, town), the region of the archipelago, and the world beyond it, which stays abstract (ships, the Church, the Crown, migrants, news) until its people arrive and become pawns. Towns, institutions and markets grow out of the villages along the phases of the cities review (`docs/review-cities.md`) | Decided, 3 Nov |
| What a pawn knows | All is known to all for now, fathers included. Those who decide read through an actor view, a seam that filters nothing until stories arrive (slice 7); knowledge with a source is Open (see Open questions) | Decided, 30 Oct; kept 3 Nov |

### The game, in Kim's terms (3 November 2026)

Kim's description of the game in the architecture conversation of 3 November (`docs/architecture/target-v2.md`); Decided as aims. The
slices of `docs/slices.md` build towards them, and the sections below say how far each is designed.

- **Society and economy.** Several strata: an agricultural base supports town life and a specialised economy, with a land-owning elite on
  top. Many systems balance themselves, and tuning the game means tuning their balance points (peasant fertility held in check by taxation and
  by higher death rates in crowded towns). Social hierarchy grows from a few founding homesteads; homesteads, families and households are
  distinct. An alderman can gather wealth and standing, collect a debt in land, pay for a mill, and become the first level of landed elite.
- **Exchange.** Goods are exchanged for goods and decaying goodwill; elites and merchants use currency once it exists. Markets arise between
  villages and can grow into towns; a region holds a town or city and its villages, about two cities a map; alehouses mix villages.
- **Culture.** Values are held by individuals, by institutions (the Church) and by social bodies (a village, a city, a court). Inheritance
  (patri-, matri- or mixed) and marriage rules differ between villages.
- **World and work.** An island world, where terrain and water separate cultures; boats are few early (fishermen) and more common later
  (merchants). Late in the game there is limited contact with an abstract outside world: a port draws merchant ships that sell exotic goods
  and buy in bulk; diplomats and a few immigrants arrive. Resources come in subtypes (granite and marble are both stone; cow's and sheep's
  milk), extracted and refined through work; pawns plan simply, and specialised roles such as miller and baker emerge. Pawns track relations,
  stats and skills.
- **Inspiration.** The acoup blog (Bret Devereaux), especially "Bread, How Did They Make It?", "Clothing, How Did They Make It?" and "Life,
  Work, Death and the Peasant".

### Principles for decisions (Kim, 12 and 14 November 2026)

Decided, in a discussion of the open questions and a revision two days later. They say how a question is answered when the sections
below do not; `AGENTS.md`, Deciding without Kim, sets the escalation and reporting procedure.

- **Nothing teleports.** The one hard rule. Goods, animals and people change place only by being carried, carted, led, shipped or
  walked, by someone who makes the journey, and nothing appears or vanishes but by a process that makes or uses it: water is fetched
  from the well, hay from the meadow. A farmstead is one place; a pawn working away from it carries its meal. Rights and knowledge are
  exempt, since following every fact through the world is not computable: ownership, obligations, goodwill and news move at once, so a
  gift, a share or a loan changes owner where it lies and waits to be fetched. The world a scenario starts with is placed, not
  carried. Where the sections below still move goods or animals at once (help in hunger, shares, handing on, a new household's start),
  the rule stands over them. No task adds a teleport, however small or temporary; one found in the code is recorded under the
  collection procedure in `AGENTS.md`, Surfacing.
- **Hunger presses, the feast binds.** Shortage drives help, loans and land passing, and through them the landed class; help still
  books goodwill. What drives the goodwill economy is plenty: at a feast a household turns its surplus into goodwill and standing. A
  household may fail; the design does not aim for every household to be fed.
- **The island grows without the player.** The island as a whole must grow reliably with nobody steering it: at least 1% a year in the
  median seed (annualized over the run). At least three quarters of seeds end above their start and at most one eighth below half. A shrinking median is a collapse. Apply these targets to populous while land is free. See Balance loops and targets, Growth.
- **Parameters, not patches.** What balance depends on is a named parameter with a meaning in the physical world or in a gameplay
  system. The first value is seldom the best: a task may experiment a little to find a good parameterisation, of new parameters and
  existing ones. The game as a whole is tuned in runs Kim starts, over many parameters together, for gameplay balance. A collapse may be tuned away.
- **Mechanisms, not prescriptions.** History is preferred, within reason, and the game must be fun; where history and play part, play
  decides. A historical pattern is reached by modelling what produced it, never by setting it: women's age at marriage held
  population in check, so the design models what makes a couple wait (a holding to marry onto, years in service, a portion to save),
  not the age allowed. Hard limits are for physical facts.
- **Customs may prescribe.** Customs and their defaults are grounded in history. A custom may set a rule (a culture's least age to
  marry): it is a rule the pawns hold, which differs between villages and can change, not a rule of the simulation. Customs may be
  added freely for now; a later review will look for a smaller set with the same effects.
- **Place matters.** The game favours settlements that specialise where the land suits it (a mining village, a farming one, a fishing
  one) and trade between them: over land, by river, and late in the game by sea. The cost of carrying is what makes a route worth
  having. It is enough that the mechanics reward this; an island with no player is not held to a target for it.
- **Detail where it is a decision.** Quantities and logistics are grounded in history, and the kinds modelled are those that give the
  head or the player a decision: key kinds, not every kind, as with the crops (Crops and rotations).
- **The future is someone's estimate** (Kim, 15 November). What the player is shown of what will happen (when a task ends, whether a
  store will last, what help a neighbour wants) is the estimate of a pawn, by that pawn's judgement, never the simulation's own
  knowledge; what has happened is shown as it was (section 7, The estimate and The timetable).
- **Like is shown alike, and pictures before words** (Kim, 16 November). Every thing of one kind (a level, a store, a trait, a
  person's row, a link) has one presentation, used wherever it appears, with any exception justified in the task. Design for desktop first. Presentation favours
  clear, indicative icons, and images and graphs where they apply, before text; a level is shown on a meter, the same meter
  everywhere (its scales are under review with Kim: VB-665). Traits are not hidden information: they are shown as icons.
- **Practice before institution** (Kim, 16 November). A venue, an office or a good the design names before the institution that
  keeps it exists is built as the practice that produced it, which the institution later takes over: the ale evening before the
  town's alehouse, the rite before the Church's service.
- **A wait is the thing waited for** (Kim, 16 November). No rule holds a pawn back by a chance or a count of years alone: what
  makes it wait is modelled (a holding to marry onto, a portion to save), and the wait is what that takes.
- **A label is read, never a switch** (Kim, 16 November). A place's or a person's name for what it is (market place, town, smith,
  landed) is read from what people do and feeds no rule; a label gets a cut point only where a measure falls into two groups, else
  the measure is shown and no word is put on it.
- **What does not wear needs a way out** (Kim, 16 November). A good that does not wear leaves play by a process: given, buried,
  lost with its keeper's knowledge.
- **A made good is graded, a built thing is not** (Kim, 16 November; with the grades of workmanship, section 8, The metals and
  the stones).
- **An elite is what the rules leave over, not a quota.** No road, office or class is given a share or a count to reach. A plan
    sizes each claim (a rent, a keep, a due) against a household's need, and the wrap-up reports who became elite and by what.
- **Every act is a pawn's** (Kim, 16 November). An act is done by a pawn who chose it, through a verb whose check holds for any
    pawn alike. A household, a village or the calendar never acts: a household's head does; a village's meeting first elects an
    alderman, who carries out what the meeting decided. The player has what its pawn has, no more and no less: a choice the player is
    given is a choice every pawn makes, and a new verb widens the game for both. Natural processes need no pawn (birth, death,
    growth, spoilage, an animal eating); debug verbs are the world's, not a pawn's.
- **References.** Founders is the reference for the early game, populous for the late game (many pawns). Report heirs without a target.

## 2. World, climate and travel

### World and scale

One finite, seeded archipelago of 280 × 200 hex tiles, each about 10–12 m across, so a house is one tile; distances, farmland and time are compressed rather than tiles mixed in size. The one grid is read at two scales, a site and land (Two readings of one grid, below).

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Map | One finite archipelago per seed, regenerable | Decided |
| Tiles | Pointy-top hexes | Decided |
| Terrain | Deep sea, sea, shallows, lakes, sandy shore, meadow, heath, broadleaf forest, pine forest, marsh, hills, mountains, snowfields. The island is forested: forest and pine are about half of the land at the median seed, a target, with open meadow under a tenth (The journey scale, below) | Agreed; the forest's share Decided, 15 Nov (Kim), its values Proposed (written by VB-444's plan, 16 Nov; the code holds the earlier values until that plan's tasks land) |
| Ground | Under the terrain, a layer a tile, for a Europe of 1300 to 1700, continental and Scandinavian. Bedrock on all land: granite (the common ground), limestone, sandstone and slate in provinces, with lenses of marble and soapstone. Deposits on few tiles, as veins: clay in low, damp ground; bog iron in marsh and on lake shores; iron, copper and lead with silver (one ore) in the uplands; tin in granite uplands and granite hills, twice as likely as first laid (Kim, 16 Nov: so that bronze by land trade is possible on most maps; VB-734); gold in the high mountains; peat in marsh; flint in low limestone and on its shores; amber, rare, on sandy shores; salt springs, rare, inland on limestone or sandstone, which flow on. Each island draws which kinds it has, by tuned chance: an island with everything is uncommon, and islands commonly differ. Deposits are finite, counted in loads, and a few sources do not run out (to be weighed when play is balanced). Nothing digs yet (backlog VB-357) | Decided, 11 Nov (the kinds, the layer, chance by island, finite); Decided, 16 Nov (tin); Proposed (the shares and amounts) |
| Mountains | Multi-tile rock relief; no peak glyphs | Decided |
| Rivers | The world has rivers | Decided, 30 Oct |
| River courses | Rivers rise in hills, mountains and marshes and run downhill to a lake or the sea, a few on each large island; brooks are narrow, larger rivers a tile wide; they carry fresh water and fish, drive water mills, and wet the ground along their banks, where hay grows well. Crossing is at fords and, later, bridges | Proposed |
| Tile scale | About 10–12 m per hex, a building plot; one house holds a family | Agreed |
| Mixed scale | By compression: distance about 15×, farmland about 200× in area, which is the same 15× in length, time compressed (see Time). One grid, two readings: a site about 10 m across, land about 150 m across (Two readings of one grid) | Agreed; the two readings Agreed, 11 Nov (VB-345); land at 150 m (it was 100 m) Decided, 12 Nov (Kim) |
| Map size | Four sizes: Tiny 120 × 85, Small 180 × 130, Medium 280 × 200 (56,000 tiles, about 20,000 of them land; the earlier single size), Large 380 × 270 | Decided |
| Generation screen | The game opens on a screen to set map size, seed and starting scenario, then generates the map; the same seed, size and scenario always give the same world | Decided |
| Regions | Hexagonal patches of radius 7 (169 tiles, centres 13 tiles apart) | Agreed |
| Walking and terrain | Routes and walking cost by terrain, each ground its own: meadow and fields 1, shore 1.15, heath 1.5, pine forest and hills 3, broadleaf forest 4, marsh 5, mountains 6, snowfields closed: rough ground is dear where no way crosses it (Kim, 15 Nov; the values Proposed, twice what each ground cost above meadow on 13 Nov, when they were 1.25, 1.6, 1.8, 2, 2.5 and 3.5), in the current route search (backlog VB-096). A worn way takes away a share of what the ground costs above meadow (a quarter trodden, three quarters a path, four fifths a lane) and is walked 3%, 10% and 15% faster: a road, which is a path or a lane, through thick forest costs 1.59 or 1.39, so a path is a bad road, half again as slow as open meadow and two and a half times as fast as the wood beside it, and a road over meadow 0.91 or 0.87 (The journey scale, below; VB-439; `TERRAIN` and `PATHS` in `src/sim/rules.js`). A step is also slowed by its slope, by Tobler's hiking function (Tobler 1993) made symmetric around the flat: its terrain cost is multiplied by exp(3.5 × S), S the slope, 2 for each unit of elevation between its two tiles (elevation runs 0 to 1 over the land; the heightmap is far steeper than ground, so the scale was set where the median step cost what it did, 1.5, and then raised a little, VB-439), at most 6, the same up and down, so routes keep to flat ground, a slope on hard ground costs in proportion and a ramp is cheaper than a cliff; a worn path does not flatten a slope (VB-271, VB-438; the three numbers are `SLOPE` in `src/sim/rules.js`, to be tuned with the balance; it was 6 added for each unit, at most 2). The route is chosen by its time weighed with discomfort (Worn paths, Sharing paths; VB-448), and walked in its time. Routes over regions (HPA\*) only if routes then cost too much (VB-097) | Agreed, 30 Oct; slopes Decided, 10 Nov, by Tobler's function 13 Nov (Kim); each ground's cost, roads and the steeper slope Decided, 13 Nov (Kim); rough ground dear and a path a bad road Decided, 15 Nov (Kim), the costs Proposed (written by VB-444's plan, 16 Nov; the code holds the earlier values until that plan's tasks land); discomfort in the choice Decided, 16 Nov (Kim), its values Proposed and its weight 0 until tuned |
| Uses of regions | Overview, territory, simplified simulation off screen, hierarchical pathfinding for 1,000 pawns (HPA\*, Botea, Müller and Schaeffer 2004) | Agreed |

#### Compression

- **Distance:** villages sit 13–15 tiles apart instead of a realistic 100–250 tiles of 10 m (1–3 km); at the land's 150 m they are 2.0–2.3 km apart.
- **Farmland:** a farm has 2–4 field tiles, each standing for about 2 hectares of sown land, about 200 hexes of 10 m (Two readings of one grid), so a farm sows 4 to 8 ha. A medieval household's about 12 ha counted its fallow, meadow and pasture too, which the game holds as other tiles.
- **Rejected alternative:** a separate world map with local maps to enter (as in RimWorld or Dwarf Fortress). It only pays off with hundreds of settlements and breaks continuous scrolling.

#### Two readings of one grid (Agreed, 11 November; VB-345)

A tile is read at one of two scales by what stands on it, and nothing is drawn finer than a hex.

- **A site** is about 10–12 m across: a house, a well, a pawn walking.
- **Land** is about 150 m across, about 1.95 ha a hex: a field, a meadow, a stretch of forest, the way between two villages. It is the 15× of distance applied to area. (Decided, 12 Nov, Kim; it was 100 m and 0.87 ha, Agreed 11 Nov. Of 100, 150 and 200 m, 150 leaves no quantity off by more than about 2.5 times: the farm's sown land and the villages' spacing fit, and the yield and the hours a hectare run low.)
- **Today's yields against it.** A field nets 75 rations after seed. A game day stands for about a week (7.6 days), so that is about 570 days of an adult's food; at 0.75 to 1 kg of grain a day, 430 to 570 kg net and 570 to 760 kg gross, or about 290 to 390 kg a hectare on 1.95 ha (650 to 900 at the earlier 100 m). Medieval grain is usually put at some hundreds of kilograms to a tonne a hectare (the order of magnitude only, from memory: no source was checked for it), so the game's field yields at the low end. Ploughing a field takes 20 hours, where an acre a day would make 1.95 ha about 48: work a hectare runs about 2.4 times fast.
- **Distances agree:** villages 13–15 tiles apart are 2.0–2.3 km apart at the land's scale, inside the realistic 1–3 km. Walking is 8 tiles an hour on meadow, 1.2 km an hour at this scale (Decided, 15 Nov, Kim; `SPEED`, it was 12 from 12 November and 3 before; written by VB-444's plan, 16 Nov; the code holds the earlier values until that plan's tasks land): the neighbouring village is 2.5 to 3 hours away, a field beside the house up to half an hour, the village's wood an hour and a half as the crow flies and two and a half by its path (The journey scale, below). The figures that follow were measured at 12: On a farm of 3 to 6 fields walking and carrying fell from about 42% of the hours on jobs to about 17% (founders and populous small, seed 7).
- **Time is the third compression:** a game day is about a week (Time and calendar).

#### Sizing at 1,000 pawns

| Unit | Size | People |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Farm | 3–5 tiles: a house and 2–4 fields | One household, 4–5 people |
| Village of 5 farms | 20–30 tiles, with well, hall and lanes | About 25 |
| Town with a market | 35–40 tiles: about 11 houses, a 7-tile market, workshops, streets | About 50, fed by surrounding villages |
| Whole population | About 1,000 built tiles plus 4,000–5,000 of woodlots, pasture, quarries and fishing | About 1,000 in 220 households and 25–30 settlements |

Hay meadows (see Animals) add 2–3 tiles to a farm that keeps animals, private or shared; the figures above predate them and need revising (Open).

The town's row is a size for the eye, from v0.3. Its streets and its market of 7 tiles are no layout rule: plots are grown as village houses are, and no town is expected before the ship (Settlements, Towns and regions, 16 Nov).

#### The journey scale (Kim, 15 November; the plan of VB-444, 16 November)

A game day stands for a week, yet travel felt fast and nobody on the island was a stranger. Kim chose geography over a pace that depends on the trip: a slower walk, a forested island, and rough ground that is dear where no way crosses it. The three work together: forest and dear ground each move the hours between villages by under an hour alone. The plan and its measures are `docs/plans/VB-444.md`; the values are starting values, entered in `docs/tuning.md`, and no tuning run was made.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The walk | 8 tiles an hour on meadow (`SPEED`; 12 from 12 November, 3 before) | Decided, 15 Nov (Kim) |
| The forested island | Forest and pine together are about half of the land at the median seed, as a target: `COVER.heath` 0.07 and `COVER.meadow` 0.2 (they were 0.3 and 0.54) give 52% at the median of six medium islands and 48 to 58% over ten islands, with meadow 5 to 9% and heath 3 to 6% (forest was 17 to 27%, meadow 20 to 25%). Kim's range by seed is 30 to 60%: the top is met and nothing reaches down to 30%, a target out that only the generator can mend, since every island shares the thresholds. Meadow is the generator's second driest low ground, not the low wet ground history gives it. Both are left to a task of the generator's own, which Kim starts | Decided, 15 Nov (Kim: about half, 30 to 60% by seed); the values Proposed; the spread and the wet meadow set aside, 16 Nov (Kim: the generator is not changed by this plan) |
| Dear ground | Heath 1.5, pine forest and hills 3, broadleaf forest 4, marsh 5, mountains 6 (Walking and terrain, above). By the formula that stands, trodden ground, a path and a lane cost: pine and hills 2.43, 1.36, 1.22; broadleaf forest 3.16, 1.59, 1.39; marsh 3.88, 1.82, 1.57; mountains 4.61, 2.05, 1.74; heath 1.33, 1.02, 0.96. An hour through pathless forest is 2 tiles; on a path, 5: where a way goes decides the journey, and a way must be a path before it carries anyone far | Decided, 15 Nov (Kim: dear ground, a path a bad road); the values Proposed |
| What it makes of the island | Populous medium at year 0, seeds 3, 7, 11 and 42, at 12 tiles an hour and at the journey scale: a village's nearest neighbour 1.4 to 1.9 hours and 2.6 to 2.9 at the median village, 5 to 14 and 13 to 27 for the worst placed; the median pair 5.5 to 14.5 hours and 14 to 37; the farthest pair by land 20 to 38 and 44 to 92; the walkable land within 12 hours of a green 32 to 40% and 27 to 29%. On the small map nothing is out of reach and the farthest pair is 55 to 60 hours at three seeds of four. These are the hours before anyone has walked the route: villages start with their own ways worn in and none between them | Measured, 16 Nov |
| Seating | Populous seats 800 on medium as a target and seats fewer where the island holds fewer (Kim, 15 and 16 Nov): 775 at seed 7, the island with least meadow, and 831 to 844 at seeds 3, 11 and 42. The scenario clears no forest. The same people sit in 22 to 30 villages where they sat in 19 to 23, a village being the size of its patch of meadow | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim) |
| Reaches of daily work | The reaches written in steps (the wood 12 and 27, fishing 6, foraging 5, the hunt 6, the herds' pasture 8, breeding 12, a farm's fields 4, the stone 12, the green 4, the common meadow 3, outland 8, a new village 10 to 30 from home) keep their steps and are read as what they are, distances; each is half again as long in hours as at 12. They are to become the household head's judgement, part of its plan: a tile is worked if the head judges the walk worth it (principle 14: the hours are the head's estimate). Until that is built the steps stand | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim) |
| The route search's reach | 67 hours, kept in hours (`ROUTE.hours`), so 536 meadow steps at 8; at 200 steps the villages' table would hold 65 to 127 of 155 to 435 pairs by land on medium. Cost (Rules for cost 8 and 9): a village's row at most about 13 ms and the table at most 230 ms a year, spread a row a step, measured at year 0 on populous medium; in running years route searches expand under 5% more tiles; a cached route is at most 616 tiles (it was 230) and the cache keeps its 8,000 entries. On `medium-ref`: owed to the night | Decided, 15 Nov (Kim: it may be raised); the bound Proposed |
| Years | From the session of 15 November, eight years on the small map, at 12 tiles an hour and at the journey scale; people at year 8, hungry-days over the run, mean grain of the last three years. Populous seed 7: 397, 40, 594 and 421, 34, 495. Populous seed 42: 414, 11, 566 and 408, 82, 528. Founders seed 7: 18, 1, 582 and 17, 18, 540. Two seeds and one map, a pointer: the tasks that build the scale report growth over the scenarios' seeds (Principles, The island grows without the player) | Measured, 15 Nov |

### Weather, seasons and daylight

The map has a day and night cycle, seasons with a temperature, and simple weather with wind and rain patterns. (Decided, 30 Oct.) How they show on the map and act on the simulation is Proposed.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Day and night | Light changes over the day: dawn, full day, dusk and night are visible on the map | Decided, 30 Oct |
| Weather | Simple weather with wind and rain patterns | Decided, 30 Oct |
| Seasons | Seasons with temperature | Decided, 30 Oct |
| Seasons on the map | Seasons show on the map: spring green, autumn colour, snow and ice in winter; on fixed dates first, then following temperature (see Settlements and look; backlog VB-083) | Agreed, 30 Oct; rewritten by slice 10's plan (16 Nov; Proposed): the snow, the ice and the season's colours follow the band's dates (Climate and travel beyond walking), not fixed dates (VB-083) |
| Determinism | Weather comes from the world's seed, so the same seed gives the same weather | Proposed |

#### Daylight (Decided, 30 Oct; the look at night Proposed)

- **Night is short:** dark from 22:00 to 4:00 at the equinoxes, with 2 hours of dusk and dawn (see Time and calendar).
- **The seasons move it a little:** the dark part runs from about 4 hours at midsummer to 8 at midwinter, a mild echo of Sweden's light summer nights and long winter dark that keeps winter nights short.
- **At night** the land darkens and cools in tone but stays readable; hearths glow through doorways and smoke holes, and the few pawns still out carry their own light.

#### Temperature (Proposed)

- **One curve for the year,** cold in winter and warm in summer, plus spells of a few days warmer or colder than usual; high ground is colder. The curve by latitude, its three bands a map and the winter's dates are in Climate and travel beyond walking (slice 10's plan, 16 Nov): a winter is hard or mild as a whole, and the spells of a few days are not built.
- **Below freezing,** snow lies on land and lakes ice over; the snow and the season's colours on the map follow temperature, not fixed dates.
- **Firewood use** follows the temperature instead of a fixed winter-to-summer ratio.
- **Spring work** waits for thawed ground, and growth starts with the warmth; a late frost or an early one in autumn is the frost risk of the farming year (see Goods and the farming year).

#### Wind and rain (Proposed)

- **Wind** has a direction and a strength for the whole archipelago that change slowly, mostly from the south-west and west, as in Scandinavia.
- **Rain** comes in fronts: large soft areas, a few regions wide, that drift across the map with the wind, so it can rain on one island and be dry on the next.
- **Rain stops** haymaking and the carrying in of sheaves, which need dry weather, and slows field work; hay rained on while drying loses value.
- **Wind** turns the windmill only above a set strength, carries smoke and moves the trees.
- **The year's weather factor** (see Goods and the farming year) comes from the season's actual weather instead of a separate draw: a wet harvest or a dry early summer makes a poor year. What a crop asks of the weather, and what answers until this is built, is in Goods and the farming year, Crops and the weather (11 Nov).
- **A game day stands for about a week,** so a day's weather reads as a week's weather.

### Worn paths

Paths are not built; they are worn into the land by people and animals walking. (Decided, 30 Oct; the speeds Decided; the counts, stages, fading and snow Agreed, 30 Oct; the rest Proposed.) Built in two steps: drawn first (backlog VB-094), then the faster walking and routes that prefer paths (VB-110), after terrain costs (VB-096).

1. **Each crossing counts:** every tile keeps a count of the pawns and animals that cross it, halving each game year so unused paths fade.
2. **Three stages:** trodden ground at a count of 50, a path at 200, a lane at 800, about 1, 4 and 16 crossings a day kept up.
3. **Faster walking:** walking is 3% faster on trodden ground, 10% on a path and 15% on a lane than on meadow, and the wear takes away a quarter, three quarters and four fifths of what harder ground costs above meadow, so a road (a path or a lane) is walked almost alike through any ground: paths draw routes without lanes ruling all travel. (It was 5%, 15% and 30% of the bare terrain's cost; Kim, 13 Nov, VB-439.)
4. **Sharing paths:** route-finding counts the faster walking, so a pawn takes a path when it is quicker even if it is longer; routes merge, and the merged path wears deeper. Lanes form between farms, the well, the fields and the commons wood, as desire paths do. A route is chosen by its walking time weighed with discomfort, what a walker minds though it costs little time (Kim, 16 Nov; `docs/path-effort.md`, VB-448): the search minimises time × (1 + weight × discomfort), the discomfort a named allowance a tile for its footing (uneven or overgrown ground, by its kind, which wear takes away as it does the ground's cost), its wet (a ford, marsh; wear does not drain it) and, made worse by what is carried, in two classes, on the back and with a cart. Discomfort only steers the choice: a walk takes its time alone and tires no one (fatigue is a system of its own), so the clock, the hours between villages and the Reach overlay's hours are those of the route taken. A ford's crossing is minded as twice its time: from bank to bank a walker goes round to a bridge up to twice the time of wading, and no further. One weight for everyone until slice 7, when a trait may move it. It lands at weight 0, verified working with the weight on, for Kim's tuning run to switch on.
5. **Land kept open:** grass does not grow back on a path, and forest does not spread onto one while it is used.
6. **Under snow,** lanes stay visible as trodden snow; paths and trodden ground are covered.

Villages had a village street (*bygata*) and fenced cattle lanes (*fägator*) that led the herds through the infield to the outland pastures; in the game such lanes are worn by use, not laid out.

### Carts (12 November)

A household's carts are items: built at the house, worn by use, mended, and at last worn out. (Decided, 12 Nov, Kim, in the distance review: carts as items a household builds, several of them, hand carts and ox carts apart, that wear; the loads; the head sees that there are enough. The numbers are starting values, from memory and unchecked. Built: `src/sim/carts.js`.)

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Loads | 30 rations on the back, 90 on a hand cart, 300 on an ox cart (a back load is 25 to 30 kg, a hand cart's 100 to 150 kg on a track, an ox cart's 500 to 800 kg, halved since sheaves and hay fill a cart before they weigh it down). The ox cart's 300 replaces the 90 "with a cart and two oxen" of 10 November, which is now the hand cart's | Decided, 12 Nov |
| Pace | A laden pawn walks at 0.9 of its pace with the load on its back, 0.8 with a hand cart, 0.6 beside the oxen (it was 0.8 for every load) | Agreed, 12 Nov |
| Taking a cart | A pawn taking a load takes the household's best free cart: the ox cart if the team is free (at no plough, with no other cart), else a hand cart, else the back. The cart is out until the pawn is home. A pawn released away from its farm with a cart out (a death, its household dissolving, a call from its work) leaves the cart standing where it was, with its load and its team, until a hand of the household fetches it home; one nobody fetches by the autumn frost is lost, and the record names it (Proposed, VB-649, VB-993, 17 Nov) | Decided, 12 Nov |
| What is carted | Sheaves and hay by the load (a meadow's 30 units of hay are five trips on the back, two with a hand cart, one with an ox cart; it was one trip whatever the load). Firewood: a trip's cutting is a back load, and with a cart the cutter stays and cuts further loads while the pile is short of its goal and the working day lasts. Dung is carted 1.5 times as fast with a hand cart and 2.5 times with an ox cart. Timber, stone and water are carried as before | Proposed (built) |
| Wear | Each trip wears a cart by 2%; below half it is mended, 3 hours at the house; at nothing it is gone, and the log tells it | Decided (it wears), Proposed (numbers) |
| How many | The head counts the loads that went on the back while more waited and no cart was free. At the winter's start, with 6 or more in the year and no project under way, the household builds another: an ox cart if it keeps a team and has none, else a hand cart. A cart that is not missed is not replaced when it wears out | Decided (the head sees to it), Proposed (the count) |
| Building | A project at the house, as the fishing boat's at the shore: 12 hours and 3 loads of timber for a hand cart, 30 hours and 6 loads for an ox cart, the timber felled in the village's wood | Proposed |
| At the start | A farm that has stood for years starts with a hand cart, and with an ox cart if it keeps a team; the founders land with the team's cart only and build their hand carts in their first years | Proposed |
| Measured | Founders small, seed 7: 6 hand carts built in 12 years, none worn out, mean condition about 0.75. Populous small, seed 7: 32 built in 6 years, 1 of 71 households without a cart. At 12 tiles an hour carts save little walking: on a farm of 4 fields, 179 hours a year of walking and carrying become 171 | 13 Nov |
| Underfoot | A cart goes where wheels can go: a hand cart on open ground, a path or a lane, an ox cart on open ground, a lane or a built way, neither up a steep step; today a cart crosses any ground a walker does. Sledges take the carts' place while snow lies (Climate and travel beyond walking, Modes and vehicles; VB-810, VB-811) | Proposed (slice 10's plan, 16 Nov) |
| Not yet | A cart is not drawn on the map; carts are not lent, sold or inherited apart from the household; iron for the wheels (slice 6) | Open |

### Climate and travel beyond walking (slice 10, 16 November)

Ways of travel beyond walking, and the climate that opens and shuts them. Written by the plan of VB-190 (`docs/plans/VB-190.md`, with its measures and reasons) in an unattended run: Kim's two decisions of 3 November stand as Decided (frozen water opens travel where it is cold enough, colder further north; the village grants passage for its land and every other holder must agree), and everything else here is **Proposed**, built on the plan's proposed answers to the questions it left for Kim (`QUESTIONS.md`, VB-190). Nothing is built; the tasks are VB-807 to VB-818, with VB-083, VB-115 and VB-721. Every number is a starting value, and the climate's figures are from memory and unchecked.

Water is the road and winter the season of travel and heavy carrying: a boat joins the villages that have a landing, ice joins everyone for some weeks where it is cold enough, and between the two lie the freeze-up and the break-up, when neither a boat nor a foot crosses water.

#### Climate (Proposed, 16 November)

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A world's latitude | Chosen when the world is made, beside size, seed and scenario: southern (57 degrees north), middle (59, the default), northern (62), far north (65). It is not drawn from the seed, so a seed's island is the same in every climate. The reference scenarios stay on the middle world, where the thaw and the first frost fall where they do today; other climates are reported with no target (Principles, References) | Proposed (the climate set at generation by latitude: target-v2, section 4.2) |
| North is colder | The map's north is colder than its south by 0.015 degrees of latitude a row, 3 degrees over the medium map, as compressed as its distances; read in three bands, thirds of the map, each at its middle row, so that a change of the ground takes a third of the map at once | Decided, 3 Nov (colder further north); the bands and the span Proposed |
| The year's curve | One curve of temperature a band: a January mean of 0.5 degrees at 56 north falling 1.25 a degree of latitude, a July mean of 17 falling 0.15, coldest on winter day 7; a hard or a mild winter moves the cold half of the year by up to 3 degrees, from the season's weather (Crops and the weather). High ground is colder by 8 degrees over the land's height, for a field's thaw and frost only: water lies at sea level | Proposed |
| The winter's dates | Computed once, on autumn day 1, and taken from a due-queue: at most six a band. **Frost:** snow lies, the ground and the marsh bear, lakes and rivers close to boats. **Lake ice bears** (60 degree-days of frost, a game day counting 7.6). **The sound shuts** to boats (150) and **sound ice bears** (300). **The thaw:** no ice bears, snow goes, the ground is soft (20 degree-days of thaw; 10 for a sound). **Open water** and firm ground (60) | Proposed |
| What a normal winter gives | Southern world: no snow, no ice; lake ice for 10 game days in a hard winter. Middle world (bands at 58, 59, 60): snow for 10 to 15 days, lake ice for 7 to 12, from about winter day 4 to 8 until spring day 3 or 4; sound ice in its north band for 4 days, and for 8 over more of it in a hard winter. Northern world: snow for 18, lake ice for 16, sound ice for 10. Far north: 21, 19 and 15, with open water only from spring day 9 to autumn day 10 | Proposed (`node docs/plans/VB-190-ice.js`) |
| The freeze-up and the break-up | Between the frost and bearing ice lie 2 to 4 game days, and between the thaw and open water 1 or 2: boats are laid up and no ice bears. A village joined to its neighbours only by water is alone then | Proposed |
| The wetness of a place | A tile's moisture from the rain the seed's wind brings and the water of its valley (`w.hm`, VB-342): it exists, and draining a wet field reads it (VB-721) | Proposed |
| The normal stands still | No slow cooling over centuries: in this curve a degree colder is a game day or two off a growing season of 27, and it would move every target slowly. If wanted later it is one term of the curve, as a scenario's setting | Proposed (a question for Kim) |
| Seed and its climate | Not built: seed does not carry the band it was grown in (VB-722, closed by the plan; the finding goes to the ship's plan, where seed from abroad arrives) | Proposed |

#### The ground by season (Proposed, 16 November)

The cost of crossing a tile is compiled into an array and changes on the winter's dates, a band at a time, never daily.

| From | The ground | Boats |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Frost | Snow lies: a tile with no lane trodden on it costs 1.3 times its cost; marsh bears and costs 1.5 (it is 5 in summer); sledges run and carts are laid up | Lakes and rivers are closed |
| Lake ice bears | A lake tile and every river tile is crossed on foot at 1, with no slope: a river needs no ford | |
| The sound shuts | | The sea is closed in that band |
| Sound ice bears | The sea within 3 tiles of land is crossed on foot at 1 | |
| The thaw | No ice bears; snow goes; soft ground costs 1.3 where no lane is; sledges stop and carts stay laid up | Still closed |
| Open water, firm ground | As in summer | Open |

- **Winter ways are not summer ways:** a winter way runs straight over mire and lake where the summer way goes round. Lanes stay trodden under snow (Worn paths), and a way trodden over the ice is worn as any tile is.
- **Nobody sleeps on the ice:** a journey's leg stops at the shore if it cannot cross before the day ends, and a date takes effect at 4:00, when nobody is out.
- **The table of hours between villages** (Inheritance, Between villages) gains a winter table, what people know of the ways of a normal winter of their climate, bounded at 24 hours; the journey itself reads the day's ground, and a traveller who finds the ice not bearing goes round or waits (Principles: the future is someone's estimate).

#### Modes and vehicles (Proposed, 16 November)

| Vehicle | Load, in rations | Pace laden | What it needs underfoot |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The back | 30 (Decided, 12 Nov) | 0.9 | Anywhere a walker goes |
| Hand cart | 90 (Decided, 12 Nov) | 0.8 | Meadow, field, stubble, shore, heath, a green, a ford, a bridge; other ground only on a path, a lane or a built way; no step whose slope costs more than 2 times |
| Ox cart | 300 (Decided, 12 Nov) | 0.6 | Meadow, field, stubble, shore, a green, a ford of a brook or stream, a bridge; other ground only on a lane or a built way; no step whose slope costs more than 1.6 times; marsh only on a causeway |
| Hand sledge | 90 | 0.85 | Where snow lies or ice bears, any ground a walker crosses |
| Ox sledge | 450: a sledge on snow carries more than a cart | 0.7 | The same; no step whose slope costs more than 2 times |
| Small boat (the fishing boat) | 150 beyond its rower, a passenger counting 40 | 0.5 a tile of water, and 3 to put out | The sea within 3 tiles of land, a lake; a river of the widest kind, 0.5 a tile down and 0.75 up, a ford a portage of 3 |
| Large boat | 900, or 12 people, beyond its crew of 4 | 0.35 a tile, and 3 to put out | The sea within 8 tiles of land, a lake |
| The ship | Slice 8 | | Wider water |

- **A cart's route** is searched on its own ground. Where it has none, or one half again as long as the walker's, the load goes by the next best (the hand cart, then the back), and the head counts it as a way that was lacking, not a cart. A cart wears a tile as three walkers do, so a carted way deepens into the lane an ox cart needs.
- **Sledges** are items as carts are, built, worn and mended alike. A carter takes the household's best vehicle for the day's ground. The head holds heavy hauling (timber, stone, firewood from the far wood) for the snow when it reckons the sledge's trips cost fewer hours and the work can wait; where no snow comes, nobody waits.
- **A boat lies where it was left.** A village's landing is the shore tile cheapest by land from its green, within 2 hours; a household's boat lies on the shore tile it was built on. A journey by water is a walk to the boat, a leg by water and a walk on, with people and goods aboard; the boat stays at the far shore until it is rowed back, and is not fished from meanwhile. Someone who moves across water (a bride, a servant) is rowed by a member of either household, who rows home again.
- **The large boat** is begun by a head whose loads waited for a boat, and puts out only with four who chose to row.
- **Not yet:** a ferry kept at a crossing for anyone's use, and what is given for a crossing (a standing deal between households); sails and the wind; boats lent, sold or inherited apart from the household; a horse.

#### Reach by mode and season (Proposed, 16 November)

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Three tables | Hours between two villages are known three ways: by land (as today), over the ways of a normal winter, and by boat, which is the walk to the landing, the putting out, the water from landing to landing and the walk on. The table by boat is searched once, when a village is founded: the water does not change | Proposed |
| Over the sea | An act between villages (a marriage, a visit, service, a guardian or kin home, kin help, a rite, the gathering, a goer to a green) needs a way by land, or a boat that one of the two households has, or ice that bears; it reads the hours of its own season and of its household's means. This replaces "no route by land" in Between villages, Over the sea (Decided, 10 Nov, as closed until this slice) when VB-813 is built | Proposed |
| A day there and back | The bound stays 6 hours each way; a boat changes who is inside it, and is away for the day | Proposed |
| The gathering on a cold island | Winter days 1 to 3 fall in the freeze-up. The gathering's day becomes a custom of the culture with two options: after the harvest (winter days 1 to 3, as Decided on 10 November, the start of every culture of today) and midwinter (winter days 8 to 10, when the ice bears: the winter fairs), which the cultures of a northern or far north world start with | Proposed: it moves a Decided date for those cultures (a question for Kim) |
| Regions and market potential | Reports still (Settlements, Towns and regions), shown three ways, on foot, by boat and in a normal winter, at 3, 6 and 12 hours; a household's estimate of a green names the mode it reckoned with | Proposed |
| What it makes of the island | Populous medium at year 0, seeds 3, 7, 11 and 42, at the journey scale, by a scratch search: 0 to 132 of 231 to 435 pairs of villages have no route by land; 10 to 20 of 22 to 30 villages have a landing; pairs within 12 hours are 35 to 61 on foot, 42 to 109 by boat from the landing, 46 to 77 with lakes, rivers and marsh frozen and 63 to 101 with sounds frozen too; the people within 3 hours of the median village are 38 to 58 on foot and by boat, and 38 to 75 over ice. A boat widens the long circle, not the short one, and nothing here gathers a town | Measured, 16 Nov (`docs/plans/VB-190.md`, section 2) |

#### Built ways, passage and upkeep (Proposed, 16 November; passage Decided, 3 November)

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Built ways | Facilities, with stretches of tiles and a condition a stretch (Goods, recipes and facilities). Three kinds: a **track** (stumps and stones out: 8 hours a tile through pine or over hills, 12 through broadleaf forest, 3 over heath; the tile then costs what a lane costs and carries an ox cart), a **causeway** (logs over marsh: 16 hours and 2 loads of timber a tile; the tile costs 1.2 and carries carts) and a **bridge** (6 loads of timber and 40 hours, twice that over a river: Kim, 12 Nov). A made road and a street wait for a town; a blazed trail is the rung below the track (VB-447) | Proposed; the bridge's cost Decided, 12 Nov |
| Where a way is built | Only along a worn way, or to join two: the worn way is the desire line and the survey, and nobody lays out a road. Once a year the alderman reckons what a stretch would save in a year, from the crossings counted on its tiles and the loads that went on the back for want of a way (for a bridge, the detour by the nearest ford), and puts to the meeting the stretch whose saving over 5 years most exceeds its building hours, if any does. The meeting votes and the alderman carries it out, as village work with timber from the village's wood; one way a village at a time. A head may build a track on its own holding by the same reckoning. It is a pawn's estimate and is logged with its figures | Proposed (it generalises Kim's answer of 12 November on where a bridge is built, under the principles of 16 November) |
| Wear and upkeep | A track loses 10% of its condition a year, a causeway 8%, a bridge 5% (Kim, 12 Nov); below a half a track no longer carries an ox cart, a bridge is closed below a fifth, and at nothing a track is the worn way under it. Who mends is a custom, `wayDuty`: the village on its work days (the start of every culture), each farm a lot in proportion to its fields (the road lots of the Swedish farms), or the builder. A neglected stretch is named at the meeting; a fine waits for the cases (Loans, obligations and cases) | Proposed; the bridge's decay Decided, 12 Nov |
| Passage on foot | Read where a tile lies, so that one ground serves every walker. A field from its sowing to its reaping, and a yard, is crossed by nobody, its holder included, but along a path or a lane on it; a field that would cut a house, a field or the green off stays open. The practice `passage` of the village whose land it is says whether standing hay may be crossed (`free`, the start of every culture) or only the ways (`ways`) | Decided, 3 Nov (fields and yards are never crossed; a practice for meadow and pasture); how it is read Proposed |
| The right of way | A built way holds a `pass` right for every tile of its stretch before it is begun: from the meeting for the village's commons and outland, from a household's head for its field, meadow or yard, from the other village's meeting for its land. It is asked as an offer and answered by that head's own estimate of what the way saves and costs its household, and by what it owes the asker; a refusal bends the way or stops it, and the record says who refused | Decided, 3 Nov (Kim's decision 22); the asking Proposed |
| Later | A toll as a standing deal; standing from a public work (the bridges magnates built and marked with rune stones); the Crown's roads | Open |

#### Cost (Proposed, 16 November)

Named before building (Rules for cost, 9), at about 1,000 living on medium, in tiles settled by a search. The winter's dates: 18 due entries a year at most. The ground: three compiled arrays (walker, hand cart, ox cart) of a number a tile, 0.7 MB, a band's rows rewritten on a date; the route cache keeps its one bound of 8,000 entries and is emptied on a date as on any day a tile changes cost. The winter table: a row a village once a year, 5,000 to 8,200 tiles a row where lakes freeze and 15,300 to 17,000 where sounds do, a third to seven tenths of the land table's cost on the middle world and the whole of it or more in the far north. The table by boat: 5,100 to 6,900 tiles of water a village with a landing, once. The reaches of daily work stay on foot and on land. No search mixes modes, so routes over regions (VB-097) are not needed by this design; the wrap-up measures their trigger once more. The slice claims 4 ms a game day at `medium-ref` on the middle world, a year's mean, 3 of them the winter table's (its bound falls to 12 hours if the night shows it over); the far north is reported beside it. The wrap-up (VB-818) measures it.

## 3. Time and calendar

A year has 4 seasons of 12 days, so one game day stands for about a week; at 1× a day lasts 48 seconds and a year about 38 minutes.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Calendar | 4 seasons × 12 days = 48 days a year | Agreed |
| Day length | 48 s at 1×, slow enough that walking reads as walking (at 12 tiles an hour a pawn crosses 3 tiles a second at 1×: brisk; Kim, 12 Nov) | Agreed |
| Speeds | Pause, 1×, 3×, 10×, 30×, uncapped (backlog VB-084) | Agreed (to 10×), Decided (30× and uncapped, 30 Oct) |
| Uncapped speed | "Uncapped" runs the simulation as fast as the computer allows | Decided, 30 Oct |
| Running in the background | An option lets the simulation keep running while the game's tab or window is not in focus (backlog VB-084) | Decided, 30 Oct |
| Short nights | The dark part of the day is shorter than a real night, and pawns need less sleep, so more of each day is active; nights are not interesting to watch, and this speeds up play | Decided, 30 Oct |
| Daily rhythm | Work from 6:00 to 18:00, home by evening, indoors at night; lengthened from 7:00–17:00 because nights are short (backlog VB-105). Kim kept this over a fixed night with work from sunrise to sunset, when merging the two lines of design (30 Oct) | Decided, 30 Oct |
| Night and sleep | Dark for about 6 hours at the equinoxes (22:00 to 4:00), from about 4 hours at midsummer to 8 at midwinter; pawns sleep in the dark (backlog VB-105) | Decided, 30 Oct |
| Twilight | Dusk and dawn of 2 hours each; pawns sleep about 6 hours. The dark lasts 6 − 2 × cos(2π (doy − 15) / 48) hours, centred on 1:00 (backlog VB-105) | Agreed, 30 Oct |
| Yearly round | Sowing in spring; haymaking in summer; harvest in early autumn; village meetings on autumn day 10; moving round on autumn day 11; the gathering of villages and its fair after the harvest (Decided, 10 Nov), on winter days 1 to 3 (see The gathering) | Agreed |
| Stall period | Animals stand in the byre from about autumn day 9 to spring day 6, 21 of 48 days (see Animals) | Agreed |
| Pace of play | A year takes about 38 minutes at 1× and about 4 at 10× | Agreed, trade-off noted |

**Growth and time conflict.** Frontier populations with open land grew close to 3% a year from births. From 10 to 1,000 takes about 150 years, 5–6 generations, or roughly 10 hours at 10×. The game is meant to be slow: 40 hours or more to reach full population is fine, with growth from births and, in a later stage, very limited immigration by boat (Decided). The target is at least 1% a year in the long term (Decided, 12 Nov; Balance loops and targets, Growth); at exactly 1%, 10 to 1,000 takes about 460 years, about 29 hours at 10×, so a run of the intended length rests on growth nearer 2 to 3%, or on a scenario that starts larger.

**Run length.** A single run should last 40–80 hours (Decided). At about 38 minutes a year, 150 years is about 95 hours at 1×, so a run would mostly play between 1× and 3×.

## 4. People, households and family records

Every pawn is a person on record with parents, marriages and life events; households are where people live and work, which is not the same as family.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Founders | 10 unrelated founders, 5 women and 5 men | Decided |
| Founder households | Four couples on four farmsteads (three around a village green, one lone farm), plus a farmhand and a maid serving in two of them | Agreed |
| Household and family | Separate: servants live in households not their own | Agreed |
| Records | Person records (sex, birth, death, father, mother) kept apart from union records with start and end dates, as GEDCOM (the Genealogical Data Communication standard) separates individuals and families | Agreed |
| Life events | Born, married, moved, inherited, share received, cause taken up, died | Agreed |
| Naming | Given name and patronymic (-son, -dotter); married women keep theirs. Founders' fathers are known only from the patronymic, all different and never another founder's given name | Agreed |
| Kinship | Malécot's kinship coefficient, shown for spouses. The marriage limit is judged by civil degree: generations up from one person to the nearest common ancestor and down to the other (uncle–niece 3, first cousins 4, second cousins 6); siblings and half-siblings (2) never marry | Decided (limit by degree), Agreed (coefficient shown) |
| Roles | Farmer (*bonde*), mistress of the house (*husfru*), farmhand (*dräng*), maid (*piga*), son and daughter of the house, retired (*undantag*), child | Agreed |
| How roles are assigned | By position in the household; seeding follows medieval custom, but roles are data and not tied to sex | Agreed |
| Children's work | From 7, children work at reduced efficiency: fetching water, weeding and helping at the harvest; from about 8 they can herd animals, as herding was children's work (see Animals). Starting values: a rate of 0.3 of an adult's at 7, rising by 0.1 a year to 0.9 at 13 (backlog VB-104) | Decided (from 7, reduced), Agreed (herding, jobs, rates), 30 Oct |
| Mortality | Age-based pre-industrial risk of death, shaped like a Gompertz–Makeham curve and roughly matched to Coale and Demeny's (1966) model life tables; can be switched off | Agreed |
| Health | Each pawn has health from 0 to 100. Hunger h (0–1) drains 4 × h² a day (9 until backlog VB-080), 1.5 times as fast under 5 and from 60; a fully fed day restores 3; health 0 is death, so a starving adult dies in about 25 days and a small child in 17. Below 50, health raises the death risk, up to 3 times at 0, and it slows work by up to 30% at 0, on top of hunger | Decided (slows work; drain 4, 30 Oct), Agreed (rest), 30 Oct |
| Births | Natural births drive growth: until the ship (slice 8) the island grows by births alone, and nobody arrives who has not travelled there; settling families come with the ship. Children are spaced by explicit pregnancy and nursing: a mother nurses for a year (48 days), and the child's death ends it, so a lost infant is replaced sooner (Marriage, nursing and growth, below). Some are born outside marriage, from courting and relationships between pawns, with the man she was with as father; an unmarried mother and the father often marry quickly. Starting targets: about 7 births for a woman married at 20 and 5.5 at 25; about 20% of children die before age 1 and a third before 15; 1% of mothers die in childbirth | Decided (growth from births, spacing), Agreed (starting targets); Decided, 13 Nov (Kim: an infant's death ends nursing; no immigration before the ship, and nobody appears who has not travelled); written by VB-405's plan, 16 Nov (was Under revision since the balance review of 12 Nov) |
| Courting | Unmarried pawns may court from 16; marriage stays at 18 for women and 20 for men, and still needs a place to live and kin no closer than the limit, and is made as an offer at the moving round that both households answer. Hunger of 0.5 or more postpones the marriage, not the courting. About 5% of births at first come while courting, and about 3 in 4 such couples marry within the year if they can. The true father is recorded and, for now, known to all. A widow or widower minds the age gap half as much, so a widowed holder may marry a younger landless adult and bring the farm to the marriage (balance review; remarriage was common in pre-industrial Sweden, Lundh 2007) | Decided (from 16; true father), Agreed (rest), 30 Oct; Agreed (remarriage), 2 Nov; from 9 Nov a courting couple marries through the households' negotiation: their wish moves their heads to an offer at the moving round, and is enough by itself for those who speak for themselves (Inheritance, Marriage as a negotiation: Decided 9 Nov; the courting row Agreed 9 Nov; brought in line by VB-233, 10 Nov); Decided, 13 Nov (Kim): the age answers to scarcity through the gate a couple must pass, and nothing sets it: 18 and 20 are the least ages and not the valve (Marriage, nursing and growth, below; VB-405's plan, 16 Nov; was Under revision since 12 Nov) |
| Pawn interactions | Pawns who meet (working near each other, at the well, on the green, at home) may interact, each pair at most once a game hour; the first kind is talk. A relationship holds familiarity (the tie), an opinion from −1 to 1 and a courting flag; kin links stay in the family records. Opinion has a direction and is the sum of the standing (spouse, parent, child, sibling, housemate) and timed memories that gain up to a cap and fade, as in RimWorld; becoming friends or falling out is a life event. In each waking hour a pawn talks with at most one partner, chosen by familiarity and liking; by the pair's fixed compatibility the talk is a chat, a good talk or cross words. Idle pawns seek out those they like. Talk shows as a short bubble near the camera, and the pawn panel lists the people a pawn knows best and mood modifiers such as “talked with a friend +5” | Decided (talk first; mood shown; 30 Oct), Agreed (rest, 30 Oct); Decided (opinion from memories, one partner an hour, numbers in backlog VB-060, VB-061, VB-125; 31 Oct) |
| Stats and traits | Twelve traits, each acting on at least two rules (Traits, talk and stories, below) | Decided, 3 Nov; the rules and numbers Proposed, 15 Nov (VB-186) (was: come later) |
| Inherited traits | Founders draw hidden values from their culture, and children take after their parents in part: the two parents' mean, drawn toward the mean, plus chance (How a trait is passed on, below) | Decided, 3 Nov (partly inherited); the numbers Proposed, 15 Nov (VB-186) |
| Family tree | Generations in rows, spouses linked, children under their parents; the dead greyed, heirs marked; father, mother, spouse with kinship, children, siblings and life events per person | Decided (a view), Agreed (content) |

### Traits, talk and stories (slice 7, 15 November)

Slice 7's plan (VB-186), on Kim's decisions of 3 November (twelve traits; stories, after the experiment; pawns and the player may lie)
and the gossip experiment's seven answers of 12 November (`docs/gossip-experiment.md`), which stand as starting values. The shape is
Decided where a row says so; every number is Proposed, a starting value its task reports and the wrap-up (VB-063) hands to a tuning
run. A trait is neutral at 0.5: a population of average pawns behaves as today's.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Traits | Twelve, continuous and latent (0 to 1), shown only as labels at the tails: values (generosity, piety, ambition, honour), temperament (sociability, temper, diligence, openness) and aptitudes (body, hands, husbandry, wits). Values come mostly from upbringing, temperament and aptitudes partly by inheritance. A trait acts on at least two rules and never chooses for the player (target-v2, section 4.4). The tables below give the rules, the labels and how a trait is passed on | Decided, 3 Nov (twelve, as sketched); the rules and numbers Proposed, 15 Nov (VB-186) |
| Personal values | Held as traits; each culture gives their distribution (Customs and culture, A culture's values) | Decided, 3 Nov |
| Skills | Learnt by doing, faster with the matching aptitude; they fade unused (The mill and the loom; backlog VB-360) | Proposed (target-v2, section 4.4) |
| Opinion | Derived, never stored: a's opinion of b is the kin standing's value (spouse, parent, child, sibling, housemate), plus affinity, 0.6 × the pair's compatibility × min(1, a's tie to b / 8), plus a's memories of things that happened between them (a claim taken, a border lost, a bull lent, a refused match) and of what a has heard of b (Stories, below), clamped to -1..1; goodwill's gratitude adds to the same sum. Talk grows the tie only: its kind (a chat, a good talk, cross words) is drawn from compatibility for the bubble and leaves no memory, so two compatible neighbours become friends at the pace their tie grows. Only reaching close friends (0.45) or enemies (-0.5) is an event, left only 0.1 past its line; friend (0.25) and dislike (-0.2) are the page's words. The band is checked where the pair's tie or memories change, never by a pass over pairs (VB-240) | Decided (Kim), 10 Nov; what is heard, Proposed, 15 Nov |
| Gossip | Stories: an event and who was in it, passed on where people meet, believed by the hearer's opinion of the teller and the story's fit. The experiment (VB-187) compared it with opinion averaging, which stays for attitudes to customs only | Decided, 3 Nov; after the experiment, 12 Nov |
| Slander | Pawns and the player alike may lie; a discovered lie is the source of a counter-story about the liar, of higher valence, scaled by the culture's values | Decided, 3 Nov |

#### The twelve traits and the rules each changes (Proposed, 15 November)

A trait acts only as a contribution to a declared rule, or as a weight in a pawn's own choice. Each contribution is linear in the
trait and 1 (or 0) at 0.5; where a row names a rule of a later slice, that slice's task reads the trait through `Traits.of`.

| Trait | At the tails | Acts on (at 0, to 1) | Later |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Generosity | tight-fisted, open-handed | The holder's own reserve in allowance, 40 to 0 days of need (it was drawn per pawn; never the floor). The share of its surplus a host lays out at a feast, 0.5 to 1.5 times the custom's quarter | Lodging a stranger |
| Piety | irreverent, devout | Going to the season's rite (chance 0.3 to 0.9). The pull of what was learned young on its attitudes (the anchor), 0.5 to 1.5 times | The doctrine's side in a contested key; offerings (slice 8) |
| Ambition | content, ambitious | The wish to move out or claim a farm at the moving round (the pressure it needs, 1.5 to 0.5 times). Calling an ale evening (chance 0.5 to 1.5 times); with low honour, inventing a story about an enemy | Pressing a debt, standing for office (slice 5); craft and trade priority (slices 6 and 9) |
| Honour | slippery, a man or woman of their word | How a breach is felt: the memories of a claim taken, a border lost, a refused match, cattle in the crop and a work day shirked, 0.5 to 1.5 times. How a lie is judged: the counter-story's valence as this pawn holds it, 0.5 to 1.5 times; and its own lies (none at 1) | Keeping a deal's terms (slice 5) |
| Sociability | reserved, sociable | Starting a talk at its turn (chance 0.5 to 1, reached at 0.5) and seeking company when idle (0.25 to 0.75). Passing a story on in a talk (0.15 to 0.45), which is the reach of gossip; going to an ale evening and, by its own wish, to the gathering | |
| Temper | mild, hot-tempered | Friction in chemistry (below): two hot tempers like each other less, so their talks are more often cross words. A talk of cross words turns into a quarrel, which both remember and others tell of (chance 0.05 × the two tempers' sum) | |
| Diligence | idle, diligent | A head's planning day: every third day, every second, or daily (built, VB-249). How long an idle pawn waits before it looks for work again, 4 to 2 hours | A member's initiative outside the plan |
| Openness | set in their ways, curious | How fast its attitudes move (malleability, 0.5 to 1.5 times). The chance to take up a cause, 0 to 2 times (today's norm entrepreneur) | Trying a new crop (Crops, How the head chooses) |
| Body | frail, strong | The learning rate and the cap of skills of heavy work (felling, digging, threshing, carrying), each 0.75 to 1.25 times. Health regained on a fed day, 2.5 to 3.5 | |
| Hands | clumsy, deft | The learning rate and the cap of the crafts' skills (spinning, weaving, smithing, pottery, building), each 0.75 to 1.25 times | Quality of what is made: the odds of a piece's workmanship, as a tenth of skill either way (16 Nov; The metals and the stones, Workmanship) |
| Husbandry | no hand with beasts, good with beasts and crops | The learning rate and the cap of field and herd skills, each 0.75 to 1.25 times. A head's skill (with wits; Work, foresight) | |
| Wits | slow, sharp | A head's skill (with husbandry). How long a story is remembered, 0.5 to 1.5 times its half-life | Estimating a task's end; reckoning at the fair |

A label is shown beyond the 15th and 85th percentiles of the reference distribution (under 0.345, over 0.655), not of the living: a
culture high in honour then shows more people of their word, which is what it is. The pawn panel lists a pawn's labels and nothing
else of its traits; a rule that a trait moved says so where the rule is explained ("own reserve 12 days: open-handed").

#### How a trait is passed on (Proposed, 15 November)

One equation for all twelve: `value = mean + b × (the two parents' mean − mean) + chance`, the chance normal with spread
`0.15 × √(1 − b²/2)`, so that the spread of a trait stays 0.15 from one generation to the next, and the value kept within 0.02 to 0.98.
A trait is written twice in a life, at birth and at 15, and never by a daily pass.

| Group | b | The parents | The mean | Set |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Temperament | 0.45 (twin studies of the Big Five: 0.4 to 0.5) | Father and mother by birth | 0.5 | At birth |
| Aptitudes | 0.5 | Father and mother by birth | 0.5 | At birth; at 15 a hungry childhood takes up to 0.2 from body and 0.1 from wits (the days the child's household was hungry, of 96; stunting) |
| Values | 0.6 (Cavalli-Sforza and Feldman 1981: children take values from parents and from the many) | Those who raised it: the holder and the mistress of the household it lives in at 15 (its own parents where it lives with them; a child in service takes after the house it serves in) | Its culture's mean for that value | At birth from its parents by birth, as a child's value; at 15 for good |

A founder, and anyone a scenario places, draws from its culture's distribution. This answers the open question on inherited traits:
yes, and no trait is copied.

#### Chemistry (Proposed, 15 November)

The pair's compatibility, which was a seeded number alone, becomes what target-v2 sketched: `0.7 × the seeded personal term (today's)
+ 0.5 × likeness − 0.3 × friction − 0.3 × the customs between them`, less the age gap as today, within -1..1. Likeness is how close
the two are in the four values and in sociability, 0 for a pair as far apart as two drawn at random; friction is the two tempers' sum
less 1; the customs between them is the share of customs, hard ones counting most, on which they lean to different options. The last
term is Like seeks like (Balance loops and targets; Decided, 12 Nov): pawns whose customs differ think less of each other, and it is
only ever a loss, so it keeps a difference that exists and does not make one. Compatibility is still a function of the two pawns and
nothing stored for the pair; it moves while a value is still a child's and with the pair's attitudes, slowly. The weights are tuned
so that the shares of close friends, friends, dislikes and enemies stay about what they are.

#### Where pawns meet (Proposed, 15 November)

`sim/encounters` owns who meets whom: a table of kinds, each saying who is present, whom a pawn may draw as a partner, how strangers
mix and what else happens there. Encounters have only social effects; deals, matches and hiring arise elsewhere from who is present.

| Kind | Present | A partner is drawn from | Else |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| House | The members inside | The housemates, by familiarity and liking | As built |
| Outdoors | Those within 30 units | Up to 8 near, by familiarity and liking | As built |
| Pasture | Herders at their herds | Herders within 120 units, calling distance | Herding calls carried far, and herders kept company by them: the answer to VB-136's question, yes, herders meet on the pasture |
| Occasion | Those who came: the meeting, a wedding, a funeral ale, a feast, an ale evening, the season's rite; the gathering's green (VB-296) | Up to 8 of those present, by familiarity and liking plus a floor for strangers (0.5 at an ale evening and the gathering, 0.2 elsewhere), so that people who did not know each other meet | What is said to all, at a meeting or a rite, everyone present knows first-hand |

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The talk round, by turns | A pawn has a talk turn every fourth waking hour, four a day, spread by its id, and may be drawn as a partner in any hour, so still at most one talk an hour. A talk stands for a longer conversation: the tie gains 0.8 a talk (it was 0.5, at about 6.6 talks a pawn a day; now about 4.2), so a day's talk builds the tie it built. The hours at home that also grow a tie are counted at the turn. This is the slice's saving: the hourly round asks a quarter of the living, not all | Proposed, 15 Nov (a trial on populous small, seed 7, 3 years, at 0.7 a talk: close friends 2.8% of relations before and after, friends 7.2 to 7.3%, dislikes 6.7 to 6.2%; marriages and births the same) |
| The ale evening | A holder with a barrel of ale that no coming wedding, feast or gathering needs may open its house for an evening, from the end of work to 22: the small, frequent sibling of the feast, and the village alehouse before there is a town to keep one. Chance a day 0.1, times its sociability and its ambition (each 0.5 to 1.5); one a village a day. Asked: the adults of its village and of villages within 1.5 walking hours; each comes by its own wish, by its sociability, its opinion of the host and the walk (chance 0.25 × (0.5 + sociability) at home, falling to 0 at 1.5 hours), at most the 20 a barrel serves. The host pours as at a wedding (Ale and standing): the guests remember it and the host gains standing. Guests walk there and home; the barrel is the host's own. The standing alehouse, which sells ale, waits for a town, and none is expected before the ship (Settlements, Towns and regions) | Proposed, 15 Nov |
| The season's rite | Three times a year (spring day 1, summer day 6, winter day 7; autumn has the meeting) a village with a green holds a rite on it at noon, for two hours: the spiritual life pawns have before any Church (Kim, 3 Nov, decision 8), and the service once there is one. Each adult of the village, and of a lone farm within 2 walking hours, comes by its piety; work is not stopped for it. The one who leads is the holder present highest in piety times standing, read each time and never an office: it tells the gathering the story it holds most worth telling, which all present then know first-hand, and each who came knows it a little better (a tie of 0.25 to the leader only, never between all pairs), so a devout, respected holder pulls on the village's attitudes. An established Church (slice 8) takes the rite over. **On a village's own days** (Kim, 16 Nov): each village keeps its three rites on days of its own, as a parish kept its feast day, so that neighbours can attend each other's. A village's days are drawn once, at its founding or the scenario's start, each within `RITE.spread` 5 days after the season's day above (spring 1 to 6, summer 6 to 11, winter 7 to 12; winter days 1 to 3 are the gathering's), from a seeded stream; nothing staggers them against the neighbours' but chance. The adults of another village within `RITE.reach` 2 walking hours come by their piety, falling with the walk to nothing at the reach, and may bring goods to deal (Settlements, Towns and regions). Whether a culture keeps own days or common days is a rule of the culture (Customs, Rules of a culture) | Proposed, 15 Nov; own days Decided, 16 Nov (Kim), their mechanism and numbers Proposed (VB-775) |
| Mood | Each pawn has a mood of 50 plus its modifiers, shown in its panel and read by one rule: a giver's mood moves its allowance (what it would give up to another in one deal) by up to 10 days of its household's need, `MOOD.gives` (backlog VB-875), so a grieving or cold holder holds back and a newly married one gives more; nothing else reads it.
