| Cost | Before slice 10: 10 numbers drawn a year and two years of them kept in `st` (rye stands across two); a reading a field at its reaping. From slice 10: regions × days for the sums, the weather's own line of the budget, and the same reading | |

What each reads (the harvest is multiplied by each line that applies; w and r are the season's warmth and wetness):

| | Helped by | Hurt by | The year that fails it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Barley | A warm summer: + 0.10 × w | A dry summer: − 0.15 at the driest; a wet autumn, on the sheaves: − 0.15 at the wettest | A dry summer and a wet harvest; a cold summer, by the frost |
| Winter rye | Nothing; it is the steady crop of a dry year | A cold winter with little snow, the bare frost: − 0.30 when both are at their worst; a wet summer at its reaping: − 0.15 | A hard, snowless winter |
| Peas | A wet spring: + 0.05 × r | A dry summer: − 0.25 at the driest | A drought |
| Hay | A wet spring: + 0.15 × r | A wet summer, on the swathes: − 0.15 at the wettest | A dry spring and a wet haymaking |

- **The hedges this gives:** a dry summer is poor for barley and peas and leaves the rye whole; a wet summer is good to no crop but worst for rye and hay; a hard winter touches only the rye. Barley and hay, which every farm has today, fail in different years.
- **Barley alone stays about as it is:** its reading is tuned so that its mean and spread are today's factor's (0.75 to 1.2, about 0.975 on average), so a village with barley only is fed as now. A run's state changes all the same, and the tasks report the numbers before and after.
- **A head cannot see the season coming,** so foresight is not prediction: a good head's answer to the weather is the mix, the early sowing and the seed kept of two crops.
- **Not yet in:** the wetness of a place (a river bank, a marsh's edge; the weather's rain a tile, once merged, is its normal), high ground's short season, and oats, the crop for both. With the wetness of a place comes draining a wet field, a project a head may start (VB-721), and with climate by region seed as a strain fitted to its own (VB-722); both wait for slice 10's plan.
- **As built (VB-354, 22 Nov):** the draw is the sum of two even draws (a triangle on −1 to 1); the frost on autumn sday 7 to 11, two years in three on the last (sday 11, where it fell before: a bound narrowed from the first draw's mode, sday 10, after a measured fourfold rise in hungry household-years); barley's factor base 1.076 × (1 + 1.87 × its reading), within 0.5 to 1.3. The timing's cost in hunger waits for a tuning run (`docs/tuning.md`).
- **The normal stands still:** a season's weather varies about a normal that does not move over a game. Whether a slow cooling over centuries belongs in the game is slice 10's plan's to say (VB-190; Kim, 16 Nov).

#### What crops touch (11 November)

| System | What changes | Where it is settled |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The work calendar | Rye is sown in the harvest's first days and reaped at haymaking; fallow is tilled in summer; peas come in before the barley. The harvest stays the peak, but less sharply, and the autumn's start is more crowded | Threshing waiting for winter is not built by this arch: the wrap-up (VB-356) measures hours by season against VB-333's first, and proposes it only if the autumn needs it (Agreed, 11 Nov) |
| Sowing first (VB-023) | Holds within a season: a hoed field is sown before the next is hoed, whatever its crop | VB-350 |
| Soil and manure (VB-137) | Wear and mending become the crop's; rest and peas mend to 1.0, manure alone above. Manure's numbers stand | VB-348, VB-349 |
| Fresh land | Soil 1.4 serves every crop; a good head puts the rye there | VB-351 |
| Yield over time (VB-160) | The survey is made (16 Nov): a rotation raises a field's yield by its mends and no further, and what rises over decades is tilth, built by dung. A field in courses asks less dung for its soil, and the farm's surplus goes to tilth (VB-718) | VB-717, VB-718 |
| Animals, fences and deer (VB-136, VB-139) | Rye stands green while the herds are on the stubble in autumn, so a broken fence costs more; fallow is not grazed; straw and oats as fodder wait for oats | Left to VB-136 and VB-139, which read the field's crop and stage; noted in the wrap-up |
| The pot | Barley and rye are one source, grain; peas are pulses, a second | VB-349 |
| The stores | Grain a crop, as goods in subtypes; the seed reserve a crop | VB-347 |
| Help, gifts, shares | Move grain in the proportion the giver holds it; they carry crops with them without a rule of their own | VB-347, VB-352 |
| The fair (slice 4) | A sack of rye is a good like another; a head who wants a trial has a need for seed. Villages with different crops have a first thing to trade | Slice 4's worth table gains the crops; no task here |
| Loans (slice 5) | Seed corn after a failed year is the first loan; whether it is repaid in the crop lent is that slice's | Open (slice 5) |
| Mill and loom (slice 6) | Flax, hops, turnips and wheat come with their chains; ale is brewed of barley by its rule as now | Slice 6's plan |
| The food balance (VB-048) | A farm in three courses needs a fourth field; the retune of yields and fields a farm reads rations by crop | The wrap-up VB-356 hands its numbers to VB-048 |
| The player (VB-253, VB-256) | Verbs for a field's rotation and for sowing out of turn; the plan panel lists fields and seed | VB-355 |
| The page | Each crop drawn at each stage; rye green in autumn | VB-355 |
| Saves and behaviour | New fields on fields, stores, villages and pawns, each with invariants. Barley-only runs stay as they are (`node tools/same.js`) until the scenarios' crops (VB-353) and the weather (VB-354), which report their numbers before and after | Each task |
| Cost | A choice a field a year; a village a year at its meeting; a reading a field at its reaping. Nothing daily, nothing over pairs, nothing that grows with history | Named in each task; measured in VB-356 |

### Long work and processing

Farm work and building are slow and span several days; a game is about incremental progress, and a single run lasts 40–80 hours. (Decided)

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Multi-day jobs | A job keeps its progress when pawns stop to sleep, eat or answer something more urgent; they go home at night and carry on next day, or someone else allowed to take it does | Decided |
| Tilling | Several days of work per field, scaled to the internal time: about 20 work hours with a hoe | Decided, Agreed (hours) |
| Sowing | Quicker than tilling | Decided |
| Summer upkeep | Growing fields post a little work to keep them up; neglect lowers the yield. Weeding takes 2 work hours every 6 days per field; each round missed cuts the yield by 8%, at most 40% | Decided, Agreed (numbers) |
| Harvest | Work-intensive: reap the ripe crop, dry it, then thresh to get the grain out. Sheaves wait unthreshed in the barn; threshing takes about 6 work hours per field's crop | Decided, Agreed (hours) |
| Haymaking | Several days per meadow: mow, dry, carry to the hay barn | Agreed |
| Milling at home | Grain is ground into flour in the house with a hand quern (*handkvarn*); slow, about 1 work hour per 4 units of grain. Flour goes further than grain: a daily household job makes rations from flour at 1:1, or from grain at 1.25 grain per ration | Decided, Agreed (numbers) |
| Milling at a mill | Much faster; a water mill by a river, or a windmill, likely dearer to build; often owned by the village and built as a commons job | Decided |
| Windmill | The first mill is a windmill, until rivers for water mills are generated: a village of 3 or more farms builds one at the moving round on a free open tile within 6 steps of the green, hills preferred; 140 work hours with 10 loads of stone and 20 of timber; it grinds 5 times as fast as the quern; households of other villages within 10 steps pay a toll of 1 in 16; wind is ignored at first (backlog VB-093) | Agreed, 30 Oct |
| Miller and baker | In towns, milling becomes the miller's role, which supplies the baker | Decided |
| Building | Slow: clear the site and prepare the foundation; fancier, house of more standings also need lumber prepared and stone hewn and carted | Decided |
| Building jobs | Each key step is a job; a person or the village may prepare materials over a long time | Decided |
| Run length | 40–80 hours for one run, comparable to Europa Universalis IV and Crusader Kings III | Decided |

Water mills need rivers, which the world does not generate yet; what a house of more standing brings its owner is not yet defined (both Open).

### Known gaps

- Grain still lacks outlets beyond feed and seed: storage losses, tithes or trade are needed. Balance review (Agreed, 2 Nov): grain in store spoils, 5% a year of the first year's need held, 20% of the second, 40% of the rest, and the village store 20% a year, so a good farm's store levels off at a year or two of need; tithes and trade stay open.
- Hunger now has consequences (backlog VB-028): it can kill, mostly infants and the old, slows work, and postpones marriages and births; who eats first when food is short is a custom (Decided). Starting values: a year of hunger about doubles deaths, mostly infants and the old; work slows by up to 40%; marriages wait while hunger is at half or more (Agreed). Health (see People) adds a starving pawn's death within a season.
- A grown family eats more than 3–4 fields give after seed; see Systems overview, Food balance (Proposed).
- Money only makes sense once towns and markets exist.

### Animals

Hay sets how many animals a household can winter, and the animals give milk, meat, wool, draught and manure; the first version has cattle, sheep and one wild grazer, and a hard winter limit. (Decided: species, wild grazers only, hard limit. Mechanics: Agreed)

The old Swedish saying *äng är åkers moder*, “the meadow is the mother of the field”, sums up the loop: meadow hay fed the animals through winter and their manure fed the fields. Myrdal's volume on medieval farming in *Det svenska jordbrukets historia* (1999) describes it, with the fenced infield of fields and meadows and the open outfield where animals grazed in summer.

#### Herds

| Herd | Made of | Gives | Needs | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Cattle | Cows, a pair of oxen, young stock | Milk in summer (butter keeps for winter), calves in spring, meat at slaughter, manure, draught | Byre (*fähus*) space; about 1 hay unit per cow per stall day | Decided (species), Agreed (rest) |
| Sheep | Ewes, rams, lambs | Wool, lambs, meat, manure | About 0.2 hay units per head per stall day; graze heath and hills that cattle do poorly on | Decided (species), Agreed (rest) |
| Wild grazers | One species to start: roe deer, with elk perhaps later | Meat and hides when hunted | Forest edges and meadows; can damage crops | Decided (wild grazers only; roe deer, 30 Oct) |

- **Ownership:** each household owns its cattle herd and its sheep flock as two herds. A herd is the one unit that moves, grazes and posts jobs; its cattle are a short list of named animals (sex, birth, kind: calf, heifer, cow, bull, steer, ox), so calving, gelding, oxen and culling work and the log can name them, while sheep are counts (Agreed, 31 Oct; backlog VB-131).
- **The founders' herds:** not every farm keeps animals; at least one farm keeps a bull, since oxen are castrated, and at least one keeps a ram; a farm with sheep keeps 4 ewes (Decided, 30 Oct). Three of the four founders farms keep cattle (2 cows each, the first also a bull) and two keep sheep (4 ewes each, the first also a ram); one keeps neither. One pair of oxen in the group, at the bull's farm; the others hoe until they raise oxen from bull calves (Agreed, 30 Oct).
- **Food from animals:** a cow in milk gives 0.5 rations a day in summer, part of it kept as butter for winter (Agreed). Slaughter gives meat, a cow 15 rations' worth, an ox 18 and a sheep 3, which a household job salts into food that keeps; stored food spoils after a year (Decided, 30 Oct; meat values lowered from 40 and 8 after the review, so a living cow is worth more than her carcass, Agreed 31 Oct).
- **The byre** holds 6 cattle at first and is enlarged later by a building job (Agreed, 30 Oct).
- **Numbers** are starting values to tune.

#### The year

- **Spring:** turn the herds out around spring day 6, cart the winter's manure to the fields, till with the oxen.
- **Summer:** daily herding and milking; haymaking as the main work.
- **Autumn:** after harvest, the household decides how many animals to keep against its stored hay; the rest are slaughtered for salted meat.
- **Winter:** daily feeding and mucking out in the byre, from about autumn day 9 to spring day 6, 21 of 48 days.

#### Hay and the hard winter limit

- **Stored hay** is counted daily against the animals in the byre. (Decided)
- **When hay runs out,** the household slaughters in an emergency for lean meat; if nobody acts, animals die and give nothing. (Decided)
- **Hay comes from meadows** the household has made and claimed, and its share of common meadows (see Land and ecology). A meadow tile might yield about 30 hay units, enough for 1–1.5 cows over winter. A household rakes and mows only while its barn and the hay lying mown hold less than two winters' need of its animals; with no animals it mows nothing (balance review, Agreed 2 Nov). Hay in the barn spoils as grain does, by winters held: 5% a year of the hay up to one winter's need, 25% of the second winter's worth, 50% of the rest (Kim, 2 Nov; backlog VB-159).
- **The first winter** of a new settlement needs a hay stock brought along, or the founders' animals die in their first season.

#### Ties to existing systems

| System | What animals add | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Land and ecology | Herd grazing and haymaking keep land open under the forest-spread rule | Agreed |
| Fields | Manure restores soil worn down by harvests; oxen speed up tilling | Agreed |
| Jobs | Summer work: haymaking, herding, milking; autumn slaughter; spring manure | Agreed |
| Fences | A broken stretch lets cattle or deer into the crops and costs yield | Agreed |
| Roles | Children from about 8 herd; milking ranks high for the mistress of the house and maids | Agreed |
| Customs | Three voted customs (grazing on the commons, stocking for winter, herding) and two rules of the culture (hunting, sharing hay from common meadows): Customs of the animals, below | Decided, 16 Nov |
| Inheritance | Livestock is the main movable wealth: siblings' shares can be paid partly in animals, and a cow and a few ewes give a new household its start | Agreed |
| Buildings | A byre and hay barns join the building jobs; the byre's size caps the herd | Agreed |
| Commands | A herd is a non-person entity acting through commands: move, graze, come home | Agreed |

Limiting grazing per farm (“stinting”) was a common rule for village commons; Ostrom's *Governing the Commons* (1990) is the standard account of such rules, against Hardin's “tragedy of the commons” (1968). Vera's *Grazing Ecology and Forest History* (2000) argues that large grazers keep landscapes a mix of open land and forest.

#### Customs of the animals (Decided, 16 November 2026; the numbers Proposed)

Kim's answers of 16 November to the plan of VB-141 (`docs/plans/VB-141.md`, which holds the options weighed and the cost). The points are Decided; every number is a starting value to tune. The plan's history was written from memory, with no source checked, so none is cited here. No custom sets a herd's size, its milk or a hunt's yield: each changes what the land, the hay or the herder gives, and the rules that stand do the rest.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Which customs | Three are voted customs of a village, in Custom dimensions: grazing on the commons, stocking for winter, herding. Hunting and the sharing of hay from common meadows start as rules of the culture, not voted on and not drifting, and become voted customs once villages have reason to differ | Decided, 16 Nov |
| What overgrazing costs | A crowded pasture lowers the cows' milk, which is what gives a stint its meaning. A cow's milk of the day is multiplied by 1 − `bite` × (pressure − `downAt`) / `downAt`, no higher than 1 and no lower than `floor`, where pressure is the grazing pressure of the tile her herd grazed. Up to what a middling herd's week leaves on a tile (`downAt`, 300) nothing is lost; `bite` 0.3, so twice that costs 30%; `floor` 0.5, the milk of a herd with no herder. Forest spread reads the pressure as before, and a pasture does not wear to heath (Later) | Decided, 16 Nov (milk; spread unchanged; no wear); Proposed (the numbers; VB-709) |
| Grazing on the commons | How many animals a household may put on its village's outland pasture. **By land share** (default): the village grants each household a stint in cow-equivalents (cattle 1, a sheep 0.25, as a herd loads its pasture): the commons' capacity times the household's share of the village's fields and meadows, by tile. The capacity is the grass of the village's outland pasture tiles times 0.25 cow-equivalents a tile at full grass (`HERD.commons.cap`), counted at the autumn meeting. **Open:** no stint, as built. **The meeting allots:** each autumn, in proportion to the head each household winters, cut to the capacity; the alderman carries it out (once there is one, VB-314). A household takes only its stint's worth to the commons; the head beyond it stay at the farm as a herd with no herder does (half the milk, and they may stray), graze its own meadows' aftermath, or go at the autumn count. Nothing forbids keeping more: the commons does not feed them | Decided, 16 Nov; Proposed (the numbers; VB-710, VB-716) |
| The stint as a right | Once rights are held apart (VB-311), a stint is the right `graze` on the village's outland, held by the village and granted to the household for a year on the basis of the custom; until then it is a number the household holds (Land and rights) | Decided, 16 Nov (by right, after VB-311) |
| A let holding | Its share of the commons follows the use right: it is the tenant's, who works the land. An owner who works none of it gains no grazing by owning it | Decided, 16 Nov |
| The landless | Under "by land share" a household with no land has no stint. The village's remedy is its custom: open grazing, or the meeting's allotment. A household may fail (Principles) | Decided, 16 Nov |
| Another village's herds | A village's outland is closed to them under "by land share" and "the meeting allots", and open to them only under "open". A herd turned away is told in the log; a grievance between the villages waits for border disputes (VB-297) | Decided, 16 Nov |
| Stocking for winter | The margin of the autumn count (First version, The byre): the count keeps the animals whose winter's need, times the margin, the hay covers. **To the hay** (default): 1.1, a tenth in reserve, as built. **Keep the herd:** 0.8, hay for four fifths of the winter: the head expects to stretch it, culls less, and keeps a larger, thinner herd. Its cost is carried by the rules that stand: a day without fodder is a lean day and costs next summer's milk, and a herd unfed two days running loses a head a day. No feed comes from the village store. The count is also where a household chooses between a larger herd and meat: this plan adds no slaughter for a feast | Decided, 16 Nov; Proposed (0.8; VB-711) |
| Herding | **Each farm its own** (default), as built. **By turns** (*byahjord*): one farm a day sends the herder, who gathers the village's herds from their yards, keeps them on one pasture of the outland and leads them home; the other farms send nobody. The turn passes in the order of the farms, a farm herding one day for each 2 cow-equivalents it keeps, and at least one. It holds in a village of 3 or more farms with herds. A hired herdsman comes later, as a third option | Decided, 16 Nov (turns weighted by head kept); Proposed (the numbers; VB-712) |
| Hunting | A rule of the culture: **free** (default; anyone, on land that is no house, field or meadow, as built), **on the village's own land** only, or **by the village's leave**, a season's grant by the alderman. With rights held apart, hunting is a right of its own kind, `hunt`, as fishing is. The stricter options belong with a Crown or a lord (slice 8) | Decided, 16 Nov (a rule of the culture first); Proposed (the options, the right; VB-713) |
| Hay from common meadows | A village may make a meadow of its own, as it makes a common field; its hay lies in a village store and is fetched by those it is shared to. A rule of the culture shares it: **by work** (default; each takes what it mowed), **by lot** (strips drawn each year), **by head** wintered, or **by need**, the alderman sharing it. Not before the alderman (VB-314) | Decided, 16 Nov (later, a rule of the culture); Proposed (the options; VB-714) |
| Order | The cost of overgrazing and the stocking custom first, neither waiting for anything; grazing by right after VB-311; herding, hunting and the sharing of hay in a later slice of the animals, the hay after VB-314 | Decided, 16 Nov |

#### First version (Agreed, 31 October 2026)

The review of 31 October (`docs/review-animals.md`) set starting values for the first version, to tune in backlog VB-048; its questions and answers are in `QUESTION_ARCHIVE.md`.

| Point | Starting value | Task |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hay a stall day | Cow, bull, ox 1; heifer, steer 0.6; calf 0.4; ewe, ram 0.2; lamb 0.1; 21 stall days, autumn day 9 to spring day 5 | VB-131, VB-134 |
| Breeding | On spring day 3 a cow of 3 or more calves with chance 0.85 if a bull of 2 or more lives within 12 steps of her farm; a ewe lambs (0.3 chance of twins) with a ram within reach; 15% of calves and 20% of lambs die. Taking the cow to another farm's bull is remembered as a favour ("Lent us the bull") | VB-131 |
| Meadows | 30 hay a tile × site (meadow 1, marsh 0.8, heath 0.6; × 1.3 by a river or water) × weather; claimed and cleared (4 h + stones), within 6 steps; households with animals want 1.2 times their winter need | VB-132 |
| Haymaking | Rake 2 h in spring (unraked 80%); mow 16 h a tile from summer day 1 to autumn day 3 (men); dry a day; carry 4 h | VB-133 |
| The byre | A daily fodder job (women's work); the autumn count on autumn day 8 keeps what the hay × 1.1 and the byre's 6 cattle allow, the least valuable going first, oxen last; meat is salted within 3 days or spoils | VB-134 |
| Milk | 0.5 rations a cow in milk a day, half without a herder; 40% churned to butter | VB-135 |
| Herding | One herder from 8 years for a household's herds, 9 hours a day to a pasture within 8 steps; an unherded herd may get into a crop through a broken fence, and the field's holder blames the fence's keeper, as the provincial laws did | VB-136 |
| Soil | −0.08 a harvest to a floor of 0.75; a load of manure +0.04 to 1.15; 0.2 loads a head of cattle a stall day. Each load also adds 0.0011 to the field's tilth, and dung beyond the cap is spread for tilth alone (Goods and the farming year, Yield over time) | VB-137; VB-717, VB-718 |
| Oxen | A team works one tilling hand at 2.5 times | VB-138 |

#### Cost

About 440 domestic herds and up to 100 wild ones at full scale; only a few dozen sprites are drawn near the camera. Counting grazing where herds graze makes it a few hundred updates a day (Agreed, 31 Oct). A profile of 31 October puts the work and economy systems at under a tenth of the simulation's time and the social layer at two thirds; the animals' budget is 10 ms a game day at populous medium (`docs/review-animals.md`, Performance).

#### Later

Predators, pigs, horses, poultry, disease, breeding and traits, summer farms (*fäbod*), and leaf fodder from pollarded trees, which would tie trees per tile to the hay economy.

### Goods, recipes and facilities (to be written: slices 4 and 6, VB-183 and VB-185)

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Goods in subtypes | Goods are typed with subtypes (granite and marble are both stone; cow's and sheep's milk), each with the quantum that changes hands (one head, one sack) and how it perishes | Decided, 3 Nov (subtypes, Kim's description); Proposed (the rest, target-v2, section 4.3) |
| A good, its material and its kind | A good is what it is for (a whetstone, a vessel, a roof) and is made of a class of material (stone, metal, clay, wood); the kind (slate or sandstone, copper or bronze) makes a better or worse one, not a different good. Quality is the kind's physical properties, read by the good's use (how long it lasts, how fast it works, what it can hold); its worth in gifts and standing follows from custom. A deposit needs no use of its own, only a property that matters. The kind is the subtype, carried from the material into the good made of it (The metals and the stones, below). A made good also has a grade of workmanship, one of five (shoddy, sound, good, fine, masterwork), drawn at its making by odds that shift with the maker's skill; the grade scales everything the kind's properties do, and its worth. Skill makes a made good better as well as faster; it never multiplies a crop's yield (Workmanship, below) | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim's principle, and its second paragraph the same day) |
| Title and custody | Who owns a thing and where it is are apart: a cow in a neighbour's byre, grain at the mill, cargo aboard | Proposed (target-v2, section 2) |
| Recipes | Human transformation of goods; a recipe may bind the subtype it was given (any stone hewn into blocks of the same stone); building is a recipe whose output is a stage of a facility | Proposed (target-v2, section 4.3) |
| Facilities | Built things on sites, from houses and mills to roads and harbours, each with an owner, perhaps an operator, its capabilities and who may use it | Proposed (target-v2, section 4.3) |
| Textiles | Spinning as background work and weaving as partial work: the missing labour sink of the slack seasons; cloth keeps its worth | Proposed (target-v2, section 5.6) |

### The mill and the loom, the kiln and the quarry (slice 6, 11 November)

Slice 6's plan (backlog VB-185), on Kim's answers of 11 November. The slice is built in two halves: the mill and the loom first (the
quern and the mill, wool to cloth and dress, tools and iron, skills, occupations), taking from the ground only iron and the stone of
querns and millstones; then the kiln and the quarry (pots, peat, brick, tiles and lime, and the two better houses), and with it, since 16 November, the
island's own metals and the stones' other goods (The metals and the stones, below). The shape is
Decided; the numbers are Proposed, in the game's work hours (576 an adult a year), each task reporting its own and the wrap-up tuning them.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Skills | A skill a craft and pawn from 0 to 1, learnt by doing, faster with aptitude: a novice works at 60% of the rate and a master at 130%; halfway in about 400 hours; a tenth lost a year unused. So "the smith" and "the weaver" come of hours worked, and a beginner is still of use (VB-360). Since 16 November skill also sets the quality of what is made: a piece's grade of workmanship is drawn by its maker's skill (The metals and the stones, Workmanship; VB-761). This moves the decision of 11 November, by which skill set only the rate of work; the rate stands as it was. Skill still never multiplies a crop's yield (Crops and rotations, Where skill acts: the orchestrator's assumption, stated to Kim on 16 November and not contradicted) | Decided, 11 Nov (moderate; rate only); moved, 16 Nov (Kim: for made goods, quality too); Proposed (numbers) |
| Facilities | Built things on sites, kept in one registry: an owner, a kind, a condition, and building in stages, each stage a recipe of hours and loads and a step of the owner's plan. The bloomery, the kilns and the mill are the first; houses and barns move in when a slice needs it (VB-361) | Proposed (target-v2, 4.3, cut to the slice) |
| Wool to cloth | A fleece is spun into a skein of yarn as background work indoors, beside minding the house, mostly in winter (40 hours); two skeins are woven into a length of cloth on a loom the household builds (12 hours, partial work). Cloth keeps and is a good of the fair. Dress for a household of six takes about a third of one adult's year, nearly all of it spinning (VB-144, VB-362) | Decided, 11 Nov (spin, weave, wear); Proposed (numbers) |
| Dress | Each person wears a length of cloth out in about 3 years. In rags, winter's cold costs half as much health again and standing falls. Dyed and fine cloth wait for the ships (VB-363) | Decided, 11 Nov; Proposed (numbers) |
| Who spins | A practice, "Spinning and weaving are": women's work (default) or everyone's indoor work. It decides whose winter is idle, and villages may drift apart on it (VB-362) | Decided, 11 Nov |
| The quern | A hand quern makes grain go a quarter further. The stone is a good of place and wears out in about 20 years, so the need never ends: sandstone grinds at full speed, granite a fifth slower; a household without one eats its grain unground. The first households have one (VB-009). The first good whose kind sets its quality (Goods, recipes and facilities) | Decided, 11 Nov (a good of place, and it wears); Proposed (numbers) |
| The mill | A village of 3 farms or more builds a water mill where it has a river in reach (100 hours), else a windmill (140 hours, as agreed on 30 Oct); it grinds 5 times as fast, and other villages pay a toll of 1 in 16. Both need a pair of millstones and iron fittings, so a village may have to trade before it can build. The stones are of sandstone, or of granite grinding a fifth slower, as the quern: the mill then grinds 4 times as fast as the sandstone quern. This moves the decision of 11 November, which asked for sandstone (VB-093) | Decided, 11 Nov (water where possible, millstones and iron); moved, 16 Nov (Kim: granite millstones too, a fifth slower); Agreed, 30 Oct (hours, toll) |
| Tools | A household's tools wear with field work and are mended in winter; work goes at 70 to 100% by their condition. Iron lifts the ceiling: without it tools mend only to half (wooden spades and ards, work at 85%), with it to full. Iron is worth trading for, and nobody starves without it (VB-121) | Decided, 11 Nov; Proposed (numbers) |
| Iron | Two steps. A household with bog ore builds a bloomery, a small furnace, and smelts ore with wood into blooms (4 loads of ore and 6 of wood, 10 hours, a bloom); mending and making tools is winter craft work at home. A smithy comes when someone specialises; rock iron waits for mines (VB-364, on VB-359's digging) | Decided, 11 Nov (smelt, then forge); Proposed (numbers) |
| Occupations | A pawn is called by the craft that leads its hours of the last three years, with hysteresis; a view only, read by no rule (VB-365) | Proposed |
| Pots | Clay's first use: pots fired in a pit at home keep the store from damp and mice, halving the grain's yearly loss where they cover it; a quarter break a year (VB-366) | Decided, 11 Nov (pots, then brick); Proposed (numbers) |
| Peat | A household short of wood near a bog cuts peat in summer; dried, two loads burn as one of wood. It spares the forest near marsh villages (VB-367) | Decided, 11 Nov; Proposed (numbers) |
| Brick, tiles and lime | A brick kiln fires clay into brick and roof tiles; a lime kiln burns limestone to lime; both eat much wood (VB-368) | Decided, 11 Nov (brick after pots; lime for mortared walls); Proposed (numbers) |
| The houses | Four grades. The cottage on the bare ground; the cottage on a dry stone foundation (VB-332); the fine timber house, of hewn lumber on that foundation, not mortared, with a roof of slate or tiles and a brick hearth (VB-369); and the stone house, the one of most standing, its walls of stone or brick laid in lime mortar (VB-370). Each grade gives more standing through the well-kept house and wears more slowly; a village with no limestone must get its lime by exchange | Decided, 11 Nov (the four grades; no mortar under timber; mortar for stone); Proposed (numbers) |
| Left for the ships | As decided on 11 November: salt from springs, flint and amber; copper, tin, lead, silver and gold; marble and soapstone for the church (slice 8). Moved since: salt is boiled at a spring with the fair (VB-700); the metals, amber, soapstone and marble enter with this slice's second half (Kim, 16 Nov; The metals and the stones, below). Left: flint, the church's own uses, lead, pewter and coin | Decided, 11 Nov; moved, 16 Nov (Kim) |
| Cost | Every craft is a job on its household's planning day; skills, dress, querns and the store's pots are values read in closed form, with no yearly pass; facilities are few and indexed by owner and tile. Each task names its line, and the wrap-up measures the slice on the late saves (VB-371) | Proposed |

### The metals and the stones: goods by purpose, kinds by property (slice 6's second half, 16 November)

The plan of VB-702 (`docs/plans/VB-702.md`, with its measures), on Kim's answers of 16 November in a session. Kim's principle
(Goods, recipes and facilities, above: A good, its material and its kind) orders it: a good is what it is for and is made of a material; the kind makes a better or
worse one, not a different good; quality is the kind's physical properties, read by the good's use; worth follows from custom; a
deposit needs no use of its own, only a property that matters. The shape is Decided where Kim answered; every number is Proposed,
from general reading not checked by search, for each task to report and the wrap-up to tune.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| When the metals enter | The island's own metals (copper, tin, lead, silver, gold) enter with slice 6's second half, as goods of place: mined from found veins, smelted, and made into the goods below. The bell's use by the church, and coin, wait for the church and the Crown (slice 8). This moves the decision of 11 November, which left them for the ship | Decided, 16 Nov |
| Quality | Two things make it. The kind's physical properties, with worth following: no single number stands for a kind. And the workmanship of the piece, one of five grades (Workmanship, below). Two things of one good, kind and grade are alike | Decided, 16 Nov |
| Workmanship | A made good has a grade: shoddy (a malus), sound (neutral), good (a small bonus, quite common), fine (a medium bonus, rare), masterwork (a large bonus, rarer still). It is drawn when the piece is made, from odds that shift with the maker's skill at the craft: a novice's work is mostly shoddy, a practised maker's mostly sound or good, a master's mostly good with fine and masterwork possible. The grade acts on everything the kind's properties do (how long the piece lasts, how well it works) and on its worth: "a shoddily made spade will break more easily, and can have a worse edge" (Kim). Grades are counted; no piece has a record or a maker's name (VB-761) | Decided, 16 Nov (five grades; by the maker's skill; on all that the kind acts on, and worth; counted); Proposed (the numbers, below) |
| Which goods are graded | The made goods a household keeps and uses or shows: a farm's tools (their ironwork), whetstone, grindstone, quern, millstones, pot, kettle, cloth and the dress made of it, bell, memorial stone. Not graded: materials (ore, blooms and bars, stone, lime, brick, tiles, yarn) and food and ale, whose making skill only speeds; the thing of worth, which goes by weight (a worked ornament is the town's silversmith's); and what is built (houses, facilities, boats, carts), whose quality is a condition | Proposed |
| What a grade scales | Life: a durable's years, or the share that breaks a year, or the hours that wear a set of tools. Working: the effect the good's use reads, measured from having none (the wear a whetstone saves, a quern's speed, the edge tools give); not what a thing holds, which is its size. Worth: custom's, five multipliers a culture, rising faster than use. A good's table is kind by grade, compiled from the kind's properties and these rows | Proposed |
| Choosing among grades | A head keeps the best for its own use (dress by rank in the household), pays what it owes in the least, and gives in the best where the gift is for standing; what is left it deals, each grade a lot at its own worth. A target is on a good or a kind, never on a grade: a head chooses who makes, the most skilled hand it has, or gets the piece from a better maker, by its estimate of what its own hand's work will be worth. The played pawn has the same choices | Proposed |
| Workmanship in the page | One mark at the corner of the good's icon, the same wherever the good appears: none for sound, a notch for shoddy, one pip for good, two for fine, three for masterwork. A store's row shows its pieces by grade as one strip of five; a good's table of kind by grade opens from its icon; a pawn's skill shows the same strip as that pawn's own expectation. The word is workmanship: a house's grade is another thing (VB-763) | Proposed |
| A good's kinds | A good has a small fixed list of kinds (at most 8): those whose properties let them do its work at all. They may come from more than one class of material where the purpose is one (a pot of fired clay, soapstone or bronze). A new kind is a row (extending a system) | Proposed |
| Kind in state | A good's kind is its subtype in the goods registry (`whetstone/slate`; Goods in subtypes, above), which gains the good's material. A store counts a good by kind, as grain is counted by crop: the stone heap by its rock, ore, metal, pots, things of worth. A durable a household has one of (a quern, a whetstone, a grindstone, a kettle) is its kind and when it was made, worn out in closed form. A graded good carries its grade in the same shapes: five counts a kind, and the durable's record gains its grade; the grade is not part of the good's id, and the ledger books the good, never its kind or grade. No record for each item. A recipe binds its material's kind into its product; a use that takes any kind takes the one of least customary worth first | Proposed |
| Worth by kind | Custom's table of worth has a row for each good and kind; the properties never compute it | Proposed |
| Mines | Any household digs a found vein on its own land, its village's outland or the wilderness, by hand, as clay is dug, a load a trip, and owns what it dug. The rock is broken by fire-setting, which takes wood (3 hours and a load of wood for a load of ore; twice the hours and half as much wood again once the tile is half dug). The pit is the tile: no facility. A mine in shares with a common furnace (the *bergslag*) comes later, as a kind of body after slice 5's bodies (VB-736) | Decided, 16 Nov (hands, as clay; whose ore; shares later); Proposed (numbers) |
| Prospecting | A head who wants ore and knows no vein sends a pawn to the uplands for a day; the chance is that of work near a vein (In the ground). A verb any pawn has (VB-737) | Proposed |
| Smelting | Ore is heavy, so it is smelted near the mine and the metal is what travels. Rock iron ore goes to the bloomery at a better yield than bog ore (ore is a good whose kinds differ in richness); copper, tin and lead-silver ore need a furnace, a facility a household builds. Lead-silver ore gives lead and a little silver; lead is stored and waits for the church and the town (VB-738) | Proposed |
| Iron first | Iron is the need (tools that wear); bronze is plenty and standing. Bronze is no kind of tool | Decided, 16 Nov |
| The kettle | A copper kettle, beaten from sheet, is the household's vessel for a brewing, later for the dairy and the dye. A brewing with one gives two barrels for 6 hours where one without gives a barrel for 4. It wears through in 30 years: copper's recurring sink, and a good a copper village has without tin (VB-739) | Decided, 16 Nov (a copper kettle for brewing and dairy, that wears); Proposed (numbers) |
| Bronze | An alloy of copper and tin (a seventh tin), made by whoever holds both: each metal is carried to the caster. Tin lies apart from copper, so bronze is the good two places make together | Decided, 16 Nov (household goods and the bell); Proposed (the mix) |
| The pot's kinds | One good, the household's pot (Pots, above), of three kinds. Fired clay: a quarter break a year. Soapstone, carved: a twentieth break a year, so a soapstone village has a pot every household wants. Cast bronze: does not break; a dowry good and an heirloom, inherited as movable worth (VB-739, VB-744) | Decided, 16 Nov (the cast bronze pot as dowry and heirloom); Proposed (soapstone as a kind; numbers) |
| The bell | A village's meeting decides to have a bell and its alderman carries it out: the holders give the bronze, a caster casts it in a pit by the green, and the village's holders gain a term of standing at the gathering. It cracks rarely and is recast. Its use by the church waits (VB-740) | Decided, 16 Nov (the meeting decides, the alderman carries out); Proposed (numbers) |
| Things of worth | Silver, gold and amber are kinds of one good: worth that neither spoils nor wears. Before coin they are goods of worth only: kept, given (a dowry, a morning gift, a fine, a gift to a follower), worn (an icon on the pawn, and a term of standing under a cap), buried. At a swap they count at their customary worth like any good: barter until coin stands (The fair). Gold is as silver at about ten times the worth. Amber, gathered on the shores that hold it and bored into beads, is the third kind (VB-741) | Decided, 16 Nov (silver and gold); Proposed (amber as a kind; numbers) |
| The hoard | The sink of what does not wear: a pawn buries worth at its house; where it lies is that pawn's knowledge, told to the heir at death if it could be, else lost with it. The worth stays on the tile, and work there may find it again, as a vein is found (VB-742) | Decided, 16 Nov (the lost hoard is silver's sink); Proposed (numbers) |
| The whetstone | One good, a hand hone, cut from slate or sandstone. Slate gives the keener edge: a farm's tools wear at 0.75 with it, 0.9 with sandstone's; a slate whetstone lasts 5 years, a sandstone one 3. "Slate" stands for the fissile fine-grained rocks (VB-743) | Decided, 16 Nov (one good, two kinds, slate the keener); Proposed (numbers) |
| The grindstone | A turned wheel that regrinds a worn edge, so the winter's mending takes 0.7 of the hours. Sandstone is its only kind; it lasts 20 years (VB-743) | Decided, 16 Nov (sandstone the only grindstone); Proposed (numbers) |
| The heap by its rock | The stone heap keeps each load's rock, so a quern is of the stone it was hewn from, not of the rock under the green; quarrying reaches heath as well as hills and mountains, since limestone was quarried on flat ground (VB-733) | Proposed |
| Marble | A kind of stone: it builds as any stone, burns to lime as limestone does, and is the finest kind of the memorial stone. Fonts and altars wait for the church (VB-744) | Proposed |
| The memorial stone | When a holder dies the heir may raise a carved stone at the farm: the one carved good before the church. Its kinds are granite, sandstone, limestone, soapstone and marble, from the plain to the finest by custom; the heir gains a term of standing by the kind, fading over a generation, under one cap with the well-kept house (VB-745) | Proposed |
| Tin | Tin's chance is doubled and it may lie in granite hills as well as the granite uplands, so that bronze by land trade is possible on most maps. Measured before: a village within 30 steps of tin on 6 of 21 small maps, and villages with copper and with tin on the same map on 4 of 21. A trial of the change gave 13 and 8 of 21. Tin only is raised (Kim, 16 Nov): copper's chance stays, and since copper bounds the pair (a village within 30 steps of copper on 11 of 21 small maps), bronze by land is possible on about 8 maps of 21, not on most; the rest is left for the tuning run (VB-734) | Decided, 16 Nov (twice as likely; granite hills; tin only); Proposed (the shares) |
| Waiting | Flint, until lighting a fire is a decision (a journey, a camp). For the church (slice 8): fonts, altars and soapstone carving; amber for rosaries and incense; lead for roofs and windows; the bell's church use; coin. For the town (slice 9): pewter, iron pans and cast cauldrons | Proposed |
| Cost | About 30 numbers more a household (counts by kind, and three durables); properties compiled at load into a row a good, kind and grade, read where a job or a planning day already runs. Workmanship at 1,000 living (about 190 households): a grade is one small number on some 2,000 records that exist without it (5 durables a household and a dress a pawn) and five counts in place of one for the 3 to 5 graded kinds a household holds; about 30 KB of a save, nothing in a step, some 1,000 draws a year. Mining and prospecting are jobs on the planning day through the digging's kept search; furnaces are few, indexed as facilities; a hoard is one record, a lost one a tile's worth in the world's layer. No pass over pairs, no daily pass | Proposed |

**Goods by purpose.** The goods this plan adds or recasts; the others of the same shape are grain by crop, fuel (wood, and peat at
half) and tools (wood, and iron). Each row is the good's sound grade.

| Good | For | Material | Kinds | The property that differs, and what reads it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Whetstone | an edge kept | stone | slate, sandstone | grain: the wear of tools; hardness: its life |
| Grindstone | an edge reground | stone | sandstone | grit: the hours of mending |
| Quern | flour | stone | sandstone, granite | grit: grinding speed |
| Millstone | the mill | stone | sandstone, granite | grit: the mill's speed |
| Pot | the store kept | fired clay, stone, metal | clay, soapstone, cast bronze | how long it lasts; worth by custom |
| Kettle | a brewing, the dairy | metal | copper | what it holds; its wear |
| Tools | the farm's work | wood, metal | wood, iron | hardness: the ceiling of mending |
| Roofing | the fine house | stone, fired clay | slate, tiles | none read yet |
| Building stone | foundations, walls | stone | any | hardness: hours to hew, for walls |
| Lime | mortar, limewash | stone, burnt | limestone, marble | none |
| Memorial stone | the dead remembered | stone | granite, sandstone, limestone, soapstone, marble | grain and hardness: hours to carve; worth by custom |
| Thing of worth | plenty that waits | metal, amber | silver, gold, amber | worth by custom; amber is brittle |
| Bell | the village's standing | metal | bronze | none |
| Ore | the furnace | ore | bog, rock iron, copper, tin, lead-silver | richness: metal a load |

**The kinds.** Each with the properties its goods read. Stone: granite (hard, coarse, slow to hew), sandstone (an even grit), limestone
(soft; burns to lime), slate (fine grain; splits into sheets), soapstone (cut with a knife; unharmed by fire), marble (burns to
lime; takes a polish), flint (strikes a spark). Metal: iron (hard, takes an edge), copper (beaten to sheet; wears thin), tin (alloys),
bronze (casts; does not crack; rings), lead (soft, heavy), silver and gold (neither spoil nor wear). Amber is a material of its own:
light, brittle, washed ashore.

**Workmanship (Kim, 16 November; the numbers Proposed).** What each grade multiplies, as named tunables (`WORKMANSHIP.life`,
`WORKMANSHIP.work`; worth is a row of custom):

| Grade | Life | Working | Worth, by custom |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Shoddy | 0.7 | 0.85 | 0.6 |
| Sound | 1 | 1 | 1 |
| Good | 1.1 | 1.05 | 1.25 |
| Fine | 1.25 | 1.12 | 2 |
| Masterwork | 1.5 | 1.25 | 4 |

The odds of each grade by the maker's skill (`WORKMANSHIP.odds`: named at three levels and read in a straight line between them;
one draw a piece, from a stream of its own):

| Maker's skill | Shoddy | Sound | Good | Fine | Masterwork |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0, a novice | 60% | 35% | 5% | none | none |
| 0.5, practised | 15% | 45% | 33% | 6% | 1% |
| 1, a master | 2% | 23% | 50% | 20% | 5% |

A practised maker's pieces average the sound row (life 1.01, working 1.00), so the numbers of every good stand as written and the
grades spread around them; a novice's average 0.83 of the life, a master's 1.12. A master works at 1.3 of the rate and its pieces
are worth 1.47 sound ones, a novice at 0.6 and 0.77: an hour of the master's making is worth four of the novice's, which is what
draws a village towards "the smith" and "the potter". The hands trait moves the odds as a tenth of skill either way (People,
The twelve traits). A mishap (Work, The time taken) costs time, never a grade.

### Goods of place and side crafts (slice 4, 10 November)

Villages must differ before they need each other: where neighbours make the same things, a market has little to move
(`docs/review-markets.md`, sections 2.2 and 5). So some goods can be made only where the land gives them, and households make them
beside their farming, for the fair. The first row and the principle of the second are Kim's (10 November); the rest is the approach
proposed with them, for slice 4's plan (VB-183) to confirm.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Local differences | Villages differ in what their land lets them make, and this is what draws them to deal with each other | Decided, 10 Nov |
| More specialised work | There are more specialised jobs, so that villagers can turn to producing more than their sustenance | Decided, 10 Nov (the need); Agreed, 10 Nov (side crafts and the ladder below) |
| Goods of place | A good of place needs a resource within a village's reach: fish (sea shore, lake or river), tar and charcoal (pine forest), bog iron (marsh, smelted with charcoal), stone for querns and whetstones (stone ground). A village without the resource cannot make the good and must get it by exchange. The plain's own goods are those every farm has: grain, butter, cattle, wool | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| First goods | Slice 4 builds two, and ale beside them: dried fish, a food that keeps and counts as a source in the pot (Mixed meals), and tar, for boats and timber (What each good is for, below); they need a drying rack, a tar pit (*tjärdal*) and, for the sea, a fishing boat. Iron waits for tools that wear (VB-121), stone for the mill (slice 6), salt and cloth of quality for the ship (slice 8) | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| Side crafts | A household stays a farm and takes up a side craft in its slack hours (*bisyssla*; the many trades of the Nordic peasant), as a kind of long work with its own job kinds. It is the sink the idle hours lack beside textiles (target-v2, section 5.6), and it needs no new kind of household | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| Why make more than is needed | The surplus is a holder's project (Work, the third layer: one project by values): a household aims its side craft at something it lacks and can only get at the fair: the other villages' goods, an animal, a dowry. Nobody produces for sale in general; with no coin there is nothing general to sell for | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| Skill and tools | Hours at a craft raise the pawn's skill at it, and a craft's facility (a boat, a tar pit, later a smithy) is a lump one household builds and owns. Both make the household that began a craft better at it than its neighbours, so a village comes to have "the fisher" and "the tar burner" without any rule naming them (the derived roles of the vision) | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| The ladder | Three steps: a side craft; a craft household, which holds less land than it needs and lives partly by exchange; a specialist, which holds no field. It is a household's label, read from the craft's share of its food over the last 3 years, with hysteresis, and no rule reads it. **No longer stands** (16 Nov; Settlements, Towns and regions): that each step needs a market held more often (a fair twice a year, a monthly, a weekly market), since nobody calls a market, and that a specialist has "a plot and no fields" by rule: a household lets its fields go by its head's own estimate (Goods and the farming year, Fields a household wants). In the mocks the food a craft brings rises smoothly with the farms in reach, with no jump | Agreed, 10 Nov (the three steps); the reading, 16 Nov (VB-189) |
| A well-kept house | A well-maintained house gives its holder standing, up to a cap: the first answer to what a better house brings its owner. The house has a condition from 0 to 1 that wears 0.1 a year; 4 work hours of upkeep raise it 0.1, offered once it is under 0.8. Standing gains 0.15 times the condition (a holder's is 1.6), and a holder's spouse gains the same; so it is earned by work and lost by neglect. Tar makes this source easier: tarred, a barrel lasting 6 years, the house wears 0.067 a year and the term is 0.18 times the condition. The cap is the ceiling that tunes it (target-v2, section 6.2) | Decided, 10 Nov (standing, the cap, tar's two effects); Agreed, 10 Nov (numbers) |
| The fishing boat | The game had no boats, and tar needs one to be needed. A household by the sea or a lake of 20 tiles or more may build a fishing boat, a facility kept on a shore tile, as long work of 20 hours from timber. A trip in it brings 8 rations where the bank gives 4. It needs a barrel of tar every second year: untarred for two years it fishes at half, after four it is lost. Nobody travels in it; travel by water is slice 10's | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| Ale and standing | Ale is brewed as a side craft from grain above the household's floor and reserve: 10 rations of grain and 4 hours give a barrel, which keeps one season and serves 20 guests. A guest's opinion of the host gains 0.05, fading over 3 years; the host's standing gains 0.05 a barrel poured in the last 3 years, at most 0.2. At the gathering the gain is shared among the host village's holders by what each poured | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| Quanta and worth | Customary worth in rations, one table for every culture until norms learn from deals (slice 8): grain, a sack of 10 rations, 10; salt meat, butter and dried fish, a lot of 10 rations, 15 (they keep, and add a source to the pot); a barrel of tar 8; a barrel of ale 14; a cow 40, an ox 50, a heifer or steer 20, a sheep 6. Heirs' shares paid in animals use the same table (VB-140). Wool and cheese are not goods yet and wait for slice 6 | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| Cost | A side craft is ordinary jobs on a household's planning day; goods of place add one reach test per village and resource, refreshed when its land changes | Proposed |

**In the ground (11 November, VB-357).** The world holds the resources these goods of place need (World and scale, Ground): bog iron
lies where it is drawn, not in every marsh, and "stone ground" is the bedrock, so a village's stone has a kind. Kim's answers of 11
November (Decided; the numbers are each slice's plan's to set):
- Known and found. Bedrock, clay, peat and bog iron are known to all: they show at the surface. The upland ores (iron, copper, lead and
  silver, tin, gold) are found: a vein is unknown until a pawn has worked or herded near it for a season, or prospects for it, and the
  views show only what is known. Built (VB-358): knowledge is the village's; a finished job within 2 tiles of a vein finds it with chance
  work hours / 60; a played pawn sees what its village knows, a watcher everything, the unfound faint. Prospecting as work of its own
  comes with the first good that needs ore (16 Nov: VB-737; The metals and the stones).
- Bog iron grows back, slowly, up to what the tile began with: the peasant's lasting source, where rock iron is the rich and finite one.
- Quarried stone (VB-332) is of the tile's bedrock. A foundation takes any kind; later uses want the right one. Picked field stone is granite.
- What each is for (11 November; rewritten on 16 November by the plan of VB-702, on Kim's answers: The metals and the stones,
  above, holds the goods and their kinds): clay, limestone and sandstone with slice 6 (pots, daub and brick; lime burnt for mortar;
  querns, millstones and grindstones, and the poorer whetstone; granite the slower quern and millstone); bog and rock iron with tools that wear (VB-121), rock iron from a
  mine; slate for the keener whetstone and the roof; soapstone for the pot that lasts; marble for lime and the finest memorial
  stone; copper, tin, lead, silver and gold with slice 6's second half (the kettle, bronze for the cast pot and the bell, things of
  worth), no longer with slice 8, which keeps coin and the church's uses. Amber is a thing of worth beside silver and gold. Peat is
  fuel (VB-367) and a spring gives salt (VB-700). Flint is laid and waits (Proposed: for fire, when lighting one is a decision).
- Amounts and the share of endless sources stay placeholders until the first good that digs names a year's use: then a village's vein
  should last about three generations, and an endless source be worth a town.

**What each good is for.** A good has worth only through a need of pawns or households, or through standing (Kim, 10 November: Decided);
no good is wanted because a table says so. The goods of place, and the goods beside them that the gathering gives a use, with what
grounds each and when it could come. Dried fish, tar and ale are Agreed for slice 4 (10 November); the rest is Proposed.

| Good | Made where | What it is for | When |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Dried fish | Sea shore, lake or river | Need: food that keeps through winter and spring, and a second source in the pot (Mixed meals) | Slice 4, on fishing (VB-117) |
| Tar | Pine forest | Need: a fishing boat must be tarred or it leaks and is lost, so the fishers need the forest; tarred timber decays more slowly. Standing: through the well-kept house (below) | Slice 4 |
| Ale | Any village with grain to spare | Standing: what a host pours, at the gathering, a wedding or a funeral ale, is what its guests remember; a use for surplus grain other than letting it rot | Slice 4 |
| Furs and hides | Forest and wilderness with game | Need: winter dress and footwear. Standing: dress | With roe deer (VB-139). Built: a kill gives one hide to the hunter's household, and a household wants a quarter of a hide a member, a need the fair reads; nothing wears or uses a hide yet (leather for shoes and harness, VB-911) (Proposed, VB-424, 17 Nov) |
| Honey and wax | Broadleaf wood and meadow | Standing: mead at a feast; later wax for the Church | Slice 6 or 8 |
| Lumber and hewn stone | Pine forest; stone ground | Standing: the fancier houses of Long work need both, and a village without them must get them | Slice 6 |
| Querns, whetstones and grindstones | Stone ground: sandstone or granite for the quern; slate or sandstone for the whetstone; sandstone for the grindstone | Need: flour goes further than grain (Milling at home); edges kept, so tools wear more slowly and mend sooner | Slice 6 |
| Pots | Clay; soapstone; a caster with copper and tin | Need: the store kept from damp and mice. Standing: the cast bronze pot, a dowry and an heirloom | Slice 6 |
| The copper kettle | A copper vein, a furnace and a smith | Need: more ale a brewing, later the dairy. It wears through | Slice 6, second half |
| Things of worth: silver, gold, amber | A lead-silver or gold vein; a shore with amber | Standing: worn. A dowry, a morning gift, a fine, a gift to a follower; plenty that waits, and may be buried and lost | Slice 6, second half; coin slice 8 |
| The bell | Where copper and tin meet | Standing: the village's, at the gathering; its meeting decides | Slice 6, second half; the church slice 8 |
| The memorial stone | Stone ground; finest in marble | Standing: an heir's, for the dead of the house (Proposed) | Slice 6, second half |
| Bog iron | Marsh, with charcoal from pine | Need: tools that wear and are mended (VB-121), so faster work | With VB-121 |
| Salt | Ships | Need: keeping meat, fish and butter | Slice 8 |
| Cloth | Every farm with sheep; fine cloth from ships | Need: dress. Standing: dyed and fine cloth. It keeps its worth | Slice 6; fine cloth slice 8 |


## 9. Exchange, goodwill and obligations

Help in hunger is the first exchange, a gift (The gift, below, from slice 1's plan, backlog VB-182; see also Goods and the farming
year). The fair, below, is barter between villages, begun on 10 November for slice 4's plan (VB-183); loans, obligations and cases, below, are
slice 5's (VB-184, 11 November).

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Goodwill | An opinion that is also a currency, in discrete units (helped build a house, gave a cow): its own measure on the relation between two pawns, tightly coupled to opinion by culture. It makes parties accept unfavourable terms, short of starving themselves; an unfavourable deal changes it in turn | Decided, 3 Nov |
| Value and acceptance | Customary worth decides what a deal was worth, possibly influenced by need; need decides whether it is accepted | Decided, 3 Nov |
| Booking | A deal's residual is booked between the two acting pawns only; a household's allowance in a deal is likewise its agent's, toward the other agent | Decided, 3 Nov |
| Goodwill at retirement | A share of the holder's goodwill passes to the new holder, set by custom (half for now, in every culture, until a custom of inheritance takes it up; The gift, Who books) | Decided, 3 Nov; half, 12 Nov |
| The floor | No pawn accepts a deal that takes its household below its floor (seed and some days of food); the player's pawn may, with a warning that names who goes hungry | Decided, 3 Nov |
| Lumps | Goods change hands in whole quanta, so most deals leave a residual that goodwill absorbs; silver, divisible by weight, shrinks it | Proposed (target-v2, section 5.2) |
| Obligations | Every explicit claim (deal, loan, share, rent, tithe, tax, toll, wage, dowry, fine, service) names a debtor, a creditor, what is owed, when, and who enforces it; the heirs' shares are the first | Proposed (target-v2, section 4.4) |
| Money | Until coin, the economy is barter: goods for goods, with goodwill as the change (Decided, 10 Nov). Silver arrives from outside first, as payment by weight; minting comes later, by an established institution. Taxes drive the adoption of money (Decided, 10 Nov): the Crown first takes its tax in named goods, then asks for it in silver, and a household that owes silver must sell for silver, so coin spreads from those who have it (ships' merchants, the landed) to every farm that is taxed. Slice 8 designs it (VB-188) | Decided, 3 Nov; barter and taxes, 10 Nov |

### The gift (slice 1, 8 November)

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lumps and occasions | Goods change hands in whole quanta, at notable moments (a lean spell, the fair), not in daily top-ups, so that each record (an event, a ledger entry, a goodwill balance) marks something worth remembering. Help in hunger is the first occasion, once a season; whole quanta arrive with goods and the worth table (slice 4) | Decided, 8 Nov |
| Goodwill is one decaying number | Between two pawns, one signed balance that decays toward zero in proportion to its size; the dealings themselves are remembered by stories (slice 7), not by goodwill | Decided, 8 Nov |
| The gift | Exchange's first mechanism: one party gives goods and asks nothing back. Help in hunger is a gift: the hungry household asks, and the kin household's holder gives. The goods move through the stores; the gift's customary worth is booked as goodwill to the giver's credit. A fetched gift's records (its events, the tie, goodwill) are booked at the yes, since rights move at once, and its grain at each arrival; a far gift carried by the giver's man still books all at his arrival (Proposed, VB-461, 17 Nov). What an ask is given is set aside where it lies and fetched by the asker's household, at any distance; an ask with no one of the asker's free to fetch is refused (Proposed, VB-861, 17 Nov). The one given answers yes or no (Inheritance, The offer); a gift that answers the receiver's own ask, as help does, needs no second yes, and an unplayed pawn takes a gift. A refusal of goods, a gift or an ask, is remembered by both: the one refused, "Refused what we offered" (-0.1, 2 years), the one who refused, "We refused them" (-0.05, 1 year); a refused match keeps its own memory (Marriage as a negotiation, Consent) (Proposed, VB-690, 17 Nov) | Agreed (help), 30 Oct; Proposed (as Exchange's mechanism, VB-182); the booking and the answer, Proposed (VB-461, VB-690, 17 Nov) |
| Acceptance | A gift goes ahead when it leaves the giver's household at or above its floor and is no larger than the giver's allowance toward the receiver. Whom help asks, and for how much, stays help's rule | Proposed (VB-182) |
| The floor | The seed reserve and 20 days of the household's need | Agreed, 8 Nov |
| Allowance | How much one agent would give up to another in one deal, in rations, never below 0: the giving household's grain above its floor; less the holder's own reserve beyond the floor, 0 to 40 days of the household's need, drawn per pawn (a stand-in for generosity until traits, slice 7); plus goodwill's term, what the giver owes the receiver less what the receiver owes beyond 30 days of its household's need, which slice 4 switches on. No kinship term until help reaches beyond kin; no custom term until a custom of helping exists. With goodwill's term off, a kin household gives what it gives today. Opinion feeds allowance beside goodwill (Kim, 9 Nov, target-v2's decision 30): with slice 4, a term of the giver's opinion of the receiver (−1 to 1) times 10 rations, one sack, so never more than a sack either way (VB-220) | Agreed, 8 Nov; opinion's term Agreed, 10 Nov |
| Unit and decay | Customary rations; grain is worth 1 a ration until worth becomes a norm (slice 4). A balance is read from its value and when it last changed, so it decays with no step of its own: it halves in 4 years (tunable from 2 to 20). One under a ration counts as forgotten, and is dropped when either pawn next deals, reaches its cap, or dies. A pawn has at most 32 counterparts; the smallest balance goes to make room | Agreed, 8 Nov; one number decaying when read (Kim), 8 Nov |
| Goodwill and opinion | The debtor is grateful: +0.15 × s / (s + 20) for a balance of s rations, fading as the balance decays; it adds to the derived opinion (kin standing, affinity and memories: Traits, talk and stories, Opinion) as one more term. How long a debt has stood, and an old debt souring the creditor, are for stories (slice 7), since a balance keeps no age. One set of values for every culture for now. Slice 4 switches this on with allowance's goodwill term; until then goodwill is booked and shown only. It replaces the memory "A gift of food" proposed for VB-039 | Agreed, 8 Nov; by size only (Kim: one number), 8 Nov; adds to the derived opinion (Kim), 10 Nov |
| Who books | The two holders. The village store's gifts and its yearly filling book nothing until the village has an agent; a household without a holder books nothing. At a handover (a retirement, or a holder's death), half of the old holder's balances pass to the new holder, and a retired holder keeps the rest. A balance with the heir himself is halved: the half that would pass has nowhere to go. Other deaths end a pawn's balances until a person's estate is designed (slice 5) | Agreed, 8 Nov |
| What the player sees | The pawn panel lists up to 5 balances by size ("Owes Erik Larsson 34 rations", "Is owed 12 rations by Anna Persdotter"). On a holder's panel, each row adds how much it would give that pawn, with the parts of its allowance on hover | Agreed, 8 Nov; no reason or year (one number), 8 Nov |
| The player's gift | The player's pawn gives by the same command as any holder, to any household. Its pawn's allowance does not bind it, since the player decides for its pawn; it may give below its floor, with a warning that names who goes hungry (Decided, 3 Nov). The page's side follows slice 2. Its gift is carried, as every gift (nothing teleports): by default by one of the giving household who may carry, the played pawn first, at most a back-load (`TRAVEL.load`, 30 rations), the gift form naming the carrier; or set aside where it lies and fetched by the receiving household, which is the only way for a larger gift and when no one of the giver's may carry, and is refused when no one of the receiving household can go. Between two households of one farmstead nothing is carried (Proposed, VB-1111, 18 Nov). One rule holds for every gift: an unasked gift (the player's, the good-year gift) is brought by the giver's own, since the walk is part of the deed; what answers an ask is fetched by the asker's household, as near kin's help and the village store's are. Help from kin beyond a day's walk is the one exception, carried by the giver's man on a journey (VB-291): the hungry household would spend days fetching it, and the giver's man is the one with grain to eat on the road (Proposed, VB-1130, 19 Nov). A gift to the played pawn's household is asked, and it has `HELP.answerDays` (1 day) to answer; unanswered, its pawn answers as it would (Proposed, VB-461, VB-690, 17 Nov) | Decided (the floor), 3 Nov; Agreed (rest), 8 Nov; the carrier Proposed (VB-1111, VB-1130) |
| The feast (VB-407, 15 November) | Plenty's side of the gift. At an occasion at its farm (a wedding, a funeral ale, its harvest home) a household whose grain stands above its seed and 2 years of its need (`FEAST.above`) lays out food as well as ale: up to a quarter (`FEAST.share`) of what it holds beyond those 2 years. Only those who came eat it, each at most 3 rations at a sitting (`FEAST.perGuest`, a day's food three times over, ale counted at its grain); what is not eaten goes back into the store. As built (Proposed, VB-458, 17 Nov): the sitting is at most 3 rations a ration of need of those who came (a child eats half), less the ale poured at its grain; a feast too small to give each who came a day's food is not laid out, since a meal is eaten there or at home, never split; it is laid out from the grain above the seed, a ration of grain a ration of food, with no cooking; and the holder's generosity takes 0.5 to 1.5 times the share. A guest who ate has eaten: the meal is not eaten again at home | Decided (Kim, 13 Nov: the occasion, the 2 years, the quarter); the sitting's limit Proposed (a physical bound) |
| The harvest home | An occasion of its own kind, once a year for each household above the feast's threshold when its grain is in, held at its farm in the evening as a wedding is. It asks the holder's kin and then the households its holder has the strongest ties to, nearest first, within a day's walk and by land, up to 20 guests (ale's number, `BREW.guests`). A guest walks there and home, and goes to at most one feast a day | Decided (Kim, 13 Nov: whom, the cap); the rest Proposed |
| What a feast books | Each guest household is booked goodwill to the host's credit, between the two holders, for the customary worth of what its members ate and drank, by the gift's rule; a household without a holder books nothing. An occasion that pours ale and lays out no food books no goodwill: its ale's memory and standing are its own (Proposed, VB-458, 17 Nov). The host's standing stays ale's rule (0.05 a barrel for 3 years, at most 4 barrels). The loop: plenty is turned into goodwill, goodwill into help in a bad year and a voice at the meeting; its brake is goodwill halving in 4 years, so a household must keep feasting to keep its standing | Decided (Kim, 13 Nov) |
| The good-year gift | On its planning day after the harvest, a holder whose household is above the feast's threshold may give unasked to a household it has a tie to, within a day's walk and by land, that holds under a year of its need: kin first, then by the strength of the tie. It gives up to its allowance and at most one back-load (`TRAVEL.load`, 30 rations), carried there by one of its own as far kin's help is (nothing teleports); a gift under a sack (10 rations) is not worth the walk and is not made, so a holder with a large reserve of its own (the stand-in for generosity) gives seldom and one with none gives most years. One gift a household a year. Goodwill is booked by the gift's rule | Decided (Kim, 13 Nov: unasked, kin first, the allowance); the load and the sack Proposed |

### The fair: barter between villages (10 November)

From the markets review (`docs/review-markets.md`) and Kim's answers of 10 November. Inside a village goods move as they do now, by
help and goodwill; the fair is where households of different villages, who owe each other nothing, exchange. It is not a thing of its
own: it is held at the gathering of villages (Settlements, The gathering), which is why people are there. Slice 4's plan (VB-183) adds
quanta, the worth table and need curves. Grain loans, once part of this slice, move to slice 5 with debt (Kim, 10 Nov; VB-184).

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A barter economy | Goods are exchanged for goods, in whole quanta; need decides whether a party accepts, customary worth says what the deal was worth, and the residual is booked as goodwill between the two who dealt (Value and acceptance, Booking and Lumps, above). There is no coin and no price list until money arrives (Money, above) | Decided, 10 Nov |
| The fair follows the gathering | A gathering has the fair its attendance bears: a few households swapping at a small gathering, rows of loads and animals at a large one. The fair has no site, date or reach of its own | Decided, 10 Nov (the principle); Proposed (the rest) |
| Deals between strangers | Two pawns who have not dealt before have no goodwill, so their allowance toward each other is near zero: a deal passes only when both gain by need, and many fail because the lumps do not fit. Deals that failed for want of change are counted, as the measure of how much the island wants money. One pawn deals for its household (its holder or spokesman); a back load is 30 rations' worth of weight (3 sacks or lots, or 2 barrels), a cart with two oxen 90, and an animal walks | Agreed, 10 Nov (target-v2, section 5.2) |
| Trade partners | A deal leaves a small balance, and a balance is a reason to deal again: on each of the fair's 3 days a dealing pawn proposes to every pawn present it holds a balance with, then to 4 others, in seeded order. Over the years two holders of different villages become partners, and that tie is what later carries a lodging (The gathering, Lodging), a loan or a match between their children | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| Keeping goods | To get round the double coincidence of wants, the goods that keep and that every household can use (butter, salt meat, dried fish, tar, a sheep; cloth once it exists) are accepted beyond the taker's own need, at their customary worth, up to 30 rations' worth held at once (one back load), to be passed on in a later deal. They are goods and nothing more; the one accepted most widely is the island's nearest thing to money before silver | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| What may be exchanged for what | Goods fall into three classes: food; animals, cloth and labour; land. The second holds only animals for now: cloth comes with slice 6, and a day's work given later is a promise, which moves with the loans to slice 5. Everything keeps a worth in rations, the one unit of account, and custom says which exchanges across classes are proper: within a class a swap is ordinary; grain for a cow in a good year is admired; a cow taken for grain in a dearth is allowed, and remembered as a hard bargain (target-v2, section 5.4); land is not offered at a fair at all, until slice 5's custom "Debts are paid with" allows it | Decided, 10 Nov (classes); Agreed, 10 Nov (the rules; no labour days yet) |
| Need | What a good is worth to the household taking it, against its customary worth. Food: 1 a ration when its stores reach the next harvest with its reserve, rising in a line to 3 at empty stores and falling to 0.5 for food beyond two years' need; a food the pot lacks counts 1.2 times, up to a fifth of the household's need. An animal: 1.5 times its worth to a household with none of its kind, 1 otherwise, 0 if its hay will not carry it through the winter. Tar: its worth when a boat or the house is due, else only as a keeping good. Ale: its worth to a household with an occasion ahead (a wedding, a funeral ale, hosting), half otherwise | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| A small fair is the right result | Households that feed themselves and owe nothing bring only their margin. The fair grows when villages differ (Goods of place), when households owe (slice 5) and when they are taxed (slice 8); it is not tuned to move much grain before then | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| Dealing beyond the yearly gathering | The same barter runs on a village's green at every gathering day (its meeting, a rite, the yearly gathering), among those present, and at a household's door on other days, by a walk there (Settlements, Towns and regions). Nobody calls a market | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim) |
| Cost | At most 60 dealing pawns a gathering times about 12 proposals a day for 3 days: about 2,000 a gathering and 12,000 a year on medium, spread over the hours of the fair's days so that no 6-minute step carries more than a few hundred. The work grows with those at the fair, not with their pairs | Agreed, 10 Nov |

### Loans, obligations and cases (slice 5, 11 November)

Proposed by slice 5's plan (backlog VB-184, 11 November 2026): the alderman's road of target-v2 (section 5.5, trace 7.1), with grain
loans moved here from slice 4 (Kim, 10 Nov). A lean year lends grain; two unpaid harvests make a case at the meeting; the remedy
is labour, or a field that passes to the creditor while its old family farms it as tenants (Land and rights). Its answers
stand as Agreed (Kim, 12 Nov; `QUESTION_ARCHIVE.md`, VB-184).

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Obligations | One record for every explicit claim (Obligations, above): a debtor, a creditor (bodies: households or villages), what is owed (goods in whole quanta, or work hours), when it falls due, and the enforcer (the debtor's village). Slice 5's kinds: the heirs' shares (today's, moved in unchanged), loans, rent, days owed and wages. Days owed are one kind with three sources (VB-404's plan, 15 Nov): a debt's remedy, the alderman's keep and a patron's due; each is a count of days one household owes another within a year, called by the creditor's holder for a day at least 3 days ahead and worked as tasks of the creditor's plan by one adult of the owing household, who walks there and carries his meal. Each open one is in a due-queue by its due hour; a step touches only what is due (rule 4), and a met, forgiven or lapsed one leaves the live index and is told once in the record (rule 1) | Proposed (target-v2, section 4.4), 11 Nov |
| Grain loans | When help in hunger has gone round and a household is still short (The gift; help's rule, kin first), its holder asks to borrow: from households of its own village, the most grain to spare first, then from its trade partners met at the gathering. A lender spares what it holds above its floor and its own need to the next harvest, and lends at most what the borrower lacks to reach that harvest with its floor, in whole sacks; it does not lend to a household whose holder it holds in enmity (opinion below −0.3), nor to one that holds no field and has a loan unpaid. A loan is one command that changes everything or nothing (target-v2, 4.1); the grain moves through the stores, and nothing is booked as goodwill, since the debt is explicit | Proposed, 11 Nov (Kim, 10 Nov: grain loans are slice 5's) |
