| Interest and due | The practice "Loans of grain are repaid": with a quarter more (default) or as lent. Due when the borrower's next harvest is in (the end of autumn, after threshing); paid then from grain above its floor, in part if it must | Proposed, 11 Nov |
| Overdue and default | A loan unpaid at its due is overdue, and falls due again at the next harvest; unpaid after the second, it is defaulted, and its creditor may press (target-v2, trace 7.1: two harvests unpaid) | Proposed, 11 Nov |
| Pressing | On its planning day after a default, the creditor's holder presses, extends a year, or forgives: it presses more the larger the debt against its own year's need and the higher its ambition (a stand-in trait until slice 7), less the higher its opinion of the debtor's holder; forgiving books the debt's worth as goodwill to its credit (The gift). The player as holder chooses, by the same verbs | Proposed, 11 Nov |
| Cases at the meeting | A pressed debt is a case on the agenda of the debtor's village's next meeting (autumn day 10), heard in that village's own step. The procedure is the practice "Who judges a case": the meeting's voices (default; weighted as customs are, under "Who speaks at the meeting") or the alderman alone. Under the practice "One's own cause", the parties' households stand aside (default) or vote. Each voice upholds or not by its opinion of the creditor's holder against the debtor's, its own position (a household that owes leans to the debtor) and its leaning on "Debts are paid with"; a tie goes to the alderman's voice, and with no alderman the case is extended. Upheld: the remedy is carried out; not upheld: the debt is extended a year, and the creditor remembers it. A decision keeps its ballots and margin (target-v2, 4.5) | Proposed, 11 Nov |
| Remedies: "Debts are paid with" | A new practice: labour; labour, then land (default); land. **Labour:** the debtor household's adults owe days of work to the creditor, a day (8 work hours) for each 3 rations owed (the worth table's day's work), within a year, as tasks of the creditor's plan taken on the basis `debtor`; each hour is booked against the debt. **Land:** a field passes (Land and rights, Foreclosure) when the debt left is at least half a field's worth (60 rations, `SHARES.perField` / 2); the field's worth above the debt is booked as goodwill to the debtor's holder. Under "labour, then land", land is taken only for labour owed and not given within its year, at the next meeting. Nothing else is taken: no animals, no person | Proposed, 11 Nov |
| Partial failure | No land left to take, or a debt under half a field: what remains stands as labour owed; a debtor dissolved: its successor owes (Households and other bodies, A dissolved debtor); a creditor dissolved: its successor is owed | Proposed, 11 Nov |
| Debtors' politics | Self-interest pulls attitudes by a household's position (How attitudes move): one that owes leans to "labour" and "as lent", one that is owed to "land" and "a quarter more". It is the loop that opposes land concentration (target-v2, section 6.1), slower than it: land moves in years, practice in decades, so land concentration is tuned by its ceiling (the share of a debtor's fields a case may take, one a hearing) and not by this opposer (target-v2, 6.2). The same pull opposes the office (Roads to the elite): a household that pays the alderman's keep leans to a lesser keep (from a share of the surplus to days of work, from days to nothing) and to choosing by turns, the alderman's own household the other way; a client leans to its patron's side in a vote while the tie lasts (Patronage) | Proposed, 11 Nov; the payers' pull added by VB-404's plan, 15 Nov |
| Hired hands | The basis `hired` (Work, Who may take a task): at sowing, haymaking and harvest, a holder whose task has waited 2 hours for hands hires a landless adult of its village with no task of its own for the rest of the day, at 3 rations a day, a wage paid at the day's end from its stores; a landed household hires first, and a household below its floor does not hire | Proposed, 11 Nov |
| What the player sees | The village log tells each loan, default and case in one line ("At the Ås meeting: Olof Persson's case against Bengt Eriksson was upheld; a field passes to Olof's household, and Bengt's household farms it as tenants"); the meeting log shows a case's ballots as a custom's (VB-082); the household panel lists its debts owed and held, its tenancies let and held, and its class; the Land overlay shows tenancies. The player as holder borrows, lends, presses, extends, forgives and lets land by the same verbs as any holder | Proposed, 11 Nov |
| Cost | At `medium-ref` (211 households, 1,005 living): a few hundred open obligations at most, in the due-queue; loans asked once a season by a short household, each looking over its village's households (at most about 30); cases a few a village a year, each heard over at most 60 voices in the village's own meeting step; the class yearly per household, a village a step; two new bases of eligibility read from a compiled index (labour owed per pawn, the day's hired), so a task's check stays one lookup. Five new practices add a quarter to each pawn's attitudes (14 customs with 44 options to 19 with 55). Claimed: 4 ms a game day, a year's mean, and no 6-minute step over 60 ms (`docs/cost.md`, 1.2) | Proposed, 11 Nov |

## 10. Customs and culture

One culture holds a single set of customs with a default when generated; members' attitudes drift every game day, villages change their practice only at a yearly meeting, and remote villages can drift off into new cultures. (Decided)

Customs are grounded in history, and a custom's default leans to gameplay where the two part. New customs may be added freely for now; Kim will later review them for a smaller set with the same effects. (Decided, 12 Nov)

&#91;embedded content: customs cycle · daily drift, yearly round\]

Daily contact moves attitudes; only the yearly round turns them into practice, customs and splits, and its moves, like marriages from courting, change who meets whom.

### Two layers

- **Attitudes, per pawn:** a preference over each custom's options, updated every game day (about weekly). Decided.
- **Custom in force, per settlement:** a village applies the culture's custom unless its own practice differs. Practice changes only at the yearly village meeting (*bystämma*) after harvest. Decided.
- **The culture's customs** change only at the yearly reckoning after all meetings. Decided.
- **Every system reads customs** rather than hard-coded rules: succession, marriage, claims, who may speak. Agreed.

### Custom dimensions

Gender eligibility, number of heirs, timing of succession and claims on empty farms are customs (Decided). The full set, default “Old Nordic” positions and malleability (1 = easiest to change) are Agreed. Who makes a match (Proposed, VB-826) follows Disputed claims; the three customs of the animals (Decided, 16 Nov) are its last rows; their options are told in Goods, production and animals, Customs of the animals.

| Custom | Options | Default | Malleability |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Claims on empty farms | Kin of the old family first (*bördsrätt*); villagers first; anyone; the meeting assigns | Kin first | 1.0 |
| Cleared land belongs to | The village allots it; whoever worked most; whoever it was cleared for | The village | 0.9 |
| When the heir takes over | At the holder's death; when the heir marries, parents retiring on the farm; when the holder turns 60 | When the heir marries | 0.8 |
| A daughter's share | Equal to a son's; half a son's (*systerlott*) | Half | 0.65 |
| How many heirs | One heir takes all; one heir buys out the rest; land split between heirs | Buys out the rest | 0.55 |
| The widow | Keeps the farm for life; keeps a right to her keep; inherits outright | For life | 0.45 |
| Who can inherit | Sons only; sons first; any child; daughters first | Sons first | 0.35 |
| Who speaks at the meeting | Landholders; all household heads; all adults | Landholders | 0.3 |
| Which child inherits | The eldest; the youngest; chosen by the parent | The eldest | 0.25 |
| Where a married couple lives | With whoever holds land; at the husband's home; at the wife's home | With the land | 0.2 |
| Closest kin who may marry | Third cousins or further; second cousins or further; first cousins or further; uncle–niece or further (civil degree 8, 6, 4 or 3 and up; siblings never) | Second cousins or further | 0.12 |
| Heir to their own parents' farm | Steps behind other relatives, so farms are not combined; keeps their place, so farms may be combined | Steps behind (Agreed) | 0.6 (Agreed) |
| Who eats first when food is short | Equal shares; children first; those who work first | Equal shares (Agreed) | 0.5 (Agreed) |
| Children born outside marriage | Inherit like other children; inherit after children born in marriage; do not inherit | After children born in marriage (Agreed) | 0.5 (Agreed) |
| Disputed claims | The first claim stands; the strongest claim stands (the one most recently worked or renewed); kin of the tile's last owner first; the meeting decides by voice-weighted support | The first claim stands (Agreed) | 0.7 (Agreed) |
| Who makes a match | The heads (a spokesman asks whatever the member's wish or courtship; a member's no costs the head's opinion twice over); the heads, with the couple's consent; the couple (each speaks for itself, and a woman asks from the first asking day) | The heads, with consent (Proposed, VB-826, 19 Nov) | 0.4 (Proposed) |
| Grazing on the commons | By land share; open to all; the meeting allots (once there is an alderman) | By land share (Decided, 16 Nov) | 0.4 (Proposed) |
| Stocking for winter | To the hay; keep the herd | To the hay (Decided, 16 Nov) | 0.6 (Proposed) |
| Herding | Each farm its own; by turns for the village (*byahjord*) | Each farm its own (Decided, 16 Nov) | 0.3 (Proposed) |

### How attitudes move (Agreed)

- **Anchored social influence:** a pull towards the weighted average of one's contacts, anchored to what was learned young (Friedkin and Johnsen 1990).
- **Contacts:** weighted by time together at work, in the household and at the well or green, plus kin; ties fade with a half-life of about a year.
- **Standing:** landholders and elders weigh more (Boyd and Richerson 1985; Henrich and Gil-White 2001); traits later.
- **Self-interest:** a pull towards options that would help one's position, such as a landless daughter towards equal shares, or parents of young children towards feeding children first when food is short.
- **Scarcity:** when adults cannot find a spouse the marriage limit allows, attitudes shift towards letting closer kin marry; siblings never. When allowed spouses are plentiful, attitudes drift slowly back towards a stricter limit (Decided).
- **Upbringing:** children start from their parents and the village norm (Cavalli-Sforza and Feldman 1981).
- **Norm entrepreneurs:** now and then an adult takes up a cause for 5–15 years, usually one already in the air locally. Added in calibration.
- **Persuasion:** advocates pull directly on their contacts, in proportion to their share of each contact's ties. Added in calibration.
- **Advocacy input:** one slot used by entrepreneurs, events and influential pawns, and by any later player role.
- **Legible customs:** each change in the meeting log shows the support for the old and new option and the margin needed, and customs within 5% of changing are noted; the pawn panel shows where a pawn leans and the cause it argues for; a cause taken up and a change of practice are announced (backlog VB-082; Agreed, 30 Oct).

### Meetings, culture and splits

- **Village meeting:** practice moves from one option to another only if voice-weighted support beats it by a margin, about 25%. The margin grows with the village's tie to the centre and below 10 voices; returning to the culture's custom is easier. Agreed.
- **A village's meeting** (Decided, 10 Nov; Kim, code review). Every village meets on autumn day 10, as today, each in its own six-minute step from noon, in the order of village id, and a lone farm meets as a village of one. In its step: the interests of its people, its tie to the centre, the causes its adults take up, and its meeting. The culture's reckoning follows once all its villages have met, and then the land meeting, which settles outland between villages (backlog VB-278; the first village turns by the year: Decided, Kim, 10 Nov; built by VB-280, 16 Nov).
- **Culture reckoning:** support across settlements, weighted by size, tie and centrality. The margin grows with roughly the square root of the culture's size, with extra inertia below 40 people. Agreed.
- **Tie to the centre:** travel distance, with sea crossings costing more, plus living kin across the gap. Distant villages drift more easily. Decided (principle), Agreed (measure).
- **Fission:** automatic when a weakly tied settlement stays far from the culture's customs for 3 years; hard customs count most. The same split function can be called by events later. Decided.
- **Cities:** will count more at the reckoning and change faster, through more contacts and newcomers. Decided (principle).
- **The gathering:** like-minded villages near each other meet once a year, and meeting draws their customs together (Settlements, The gathering). Decided, 10 Nov (the purpose); how it joins the reckoning and the tie to the centre is Proposed there.

### Pace

All customs change on the scale of generations; concentrated effort can do it within one; villages are stable and larger cultures slower (Decided). Current calibration, one generation taken as 30 years:

| Test | Easiest custom | Middle | Hardest |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Natural drift, 300 people | About 3 generations | 4.6–5.7 generations | None within 200 years |
| A coalition with 20% of the voice | 1.1 generations | 1.4–1.7 generations | 2.1 generations |

A village with a 10% tie forms local practice after about 1.5 generations, against 2.1 for a 60% tie. Cultures of 100, 300 and 1,000 people first change in about 2.2, 2.8 and over 6.7 generations.

### Practice, norm and doctrine (to be written with slices 4, 5 and 8; slice 5's practices Proposed, 11 November)

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Kinds of custom | Practice (a shared rule with options: today's customs), norm (a usual quantity learned from what people do, such as what a cow is worth), doctrine (an institution's rule on the keys it claims), and each culture's distribution of personal values (A culture's values, below) | Proposed (target-v2, section 4.4) |
| Who holds values | Individuals, institutions (the Church) and social bodies (a village, a city, a court) | Decided, 3 Nov (Kim's description) |
| Doctrine against practice | A resolver tips practice to doctrine at some point; while a key is contested, attitudes change fast, and a splinter faction of the Church may form | Decided, 3 Nov |
| New customs | About ten new practices and four norms arrive with the slices that need them, each read through one door | Proposed (target-v2, section 4.4) |
| Slice 5's practices | Five, in the table below, read through the customs in force like today's 14; each joins the meeting's votes, the drift of attitudes and the culture's reckoning as they are, and each pulls on self-interest as Debtors' politics says (Exchange, Loans, obligations and cases). Usury as a doctrine waits for an established Church (slice 8), which may claim the key "Loans of grain are repaid" | Proposed, 11 Nov (VB-184) |

| Rules of a culture | A lighter kind beside the voted practices: a rule the whole culture holds, with its options and the one every culture starts with, not voted on at a meeting and not drifting; a culture that splits off keeps its parent's. The first is what follows a meeting that does not decide (Work, Village routines). The animals add two, hunting (free) and how hay from common meadows is shared (by work), each to become a voted practice once villages have reason to differ (Animals, Customs of the animals). Slice 9 adds one, the rite's days: each village its own (the one every culture starts with), or the same days everywhere. It is a rule of the culture and not a voted practice because nothing yet gives a pawn a stake in it; the days themselves are each village's own, so villages differ under it from the start (16 Nov, VB-775) | Decided, 10 Nov (the first); Decided, 16 Nov (the animals' two); the rite's days Proposed, 16 Nov (VB-189) |

Slice 5's practices (Proposed, 11 November 2026; VB-184), with default and malleability as the custom dimensions give them:

| Practice | Options | Default | Malleability |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Debts are paid with | Labour; labour, then land; land | Labour, then land | 0.5 |
| Loans of grain are repaid | With a quarter more; as lent | With a quarter more | 0.4 |
| Who judges a case | The meeting's voices; the alderman | The meeting's voices | 0.3 |
| One's own cause | The parties stand aside; the parties vote | The parties stand aside | 0.35 |
| How the alderman is chosen | Elected by the meeting; by turns among the farms | Elected by the meeting | 0.3 |

### A culture's values (slice 7, 15 November)

The fourth kind of custom (Practice, norm and doctrine): what a culture's people hold dear, as the distribution of the four value
traits (People, Traits, talk and stories). Proposed by slice 7's plan (VB-186) on Kim's decision of 3 November that personal values
are traits and each culture gives their distribution.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| What a culture holds | A mean for each of generosity, piety, ambition and honour; the spread is the reference's, 0.15, for every culture. Old Nordic starts at 0.5 for all but honour, 0.6: a culture of one's word and one's name | Proposed, 15 Nov |
| What it does | It is the mean a child's values are drawn toward when they set at 15 (How a trait is passed on), and the distribution a founder or a placed pawn draws from. It scales how a discovered lie is judged (its mean honour, 0.5 to 1.5 times the counter-story). Nothing else reads it: a culture's values act through its people | Proposed, 15 Nov |
| How it moves | It is never set: at the culture's yearly reckoning it becomes the mean of its living adults' values, so it follows who lives, marries, leaves and raises children. No pull holds it to where it began; with a few hundred adults it wanders about 0.01 a generation by chance alone, and further only where a value changes who survives or who has children | Proposed, 15 Nov |
| A split | A culture that splits off (Meetings, culture and splits) takes the mean of its own adults at once, so two villages that differed in values before the split differ as cultures after it | Proposed, 15 Nov |
| Values and customs | Values do not vote: a practice changes by attitudes, as before. They move attitudes only through the rules of the traits (openness and piety on how fast and how far a pawn's attitudes move), and they join chemistry, so people of like values befriend each other | Proposed, 15 Nov |
| Watched | The wrap-up (VB-063) reports each culture's four means over 150 years, and each trait's mean and spread among the living: a trait whose spread collapses or whose mean runs to a tail has been selected for, and the report names the rule that did it. Reported, with no target (the island is not held to a distribution) | Proposed, 15 Nov |

## 11. Settlements, towns and regions

### Settlements and look

Villages are clusters of farmsteads around a green with a well sweep, fenced together; the buildings follow medieval Swedish practice rather than later Falu red farmhouses.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Village | Farmsteads around a green with a well sweep (*brunnsvinge*); the infield fenced by a roundpole fence; a commons wood nearby. The green is the village's centre: the houses stand round it and their fields lie outward of them (Generated villages) | Decided (look), Agreed (layout); the green as the centre Decided, 12 Nov (Kim) |
| Every farm in a village | Every farm belongs to a village; a lone farm is an ordinary village of one farm, with its own meeting and a generated village name, and its map label shows the farm name only; an empty farm is owned by its village. When a village gains a second farm, it can lay out a green and dig a well as village work; they are not made automatically | Decided |
| Farmstead | Unpainted log cottage (*stuga*) with a turf roof on birch bark and a smoke hole (*ljore*) instead of a chimney; storehouse on posts (*härbre*); gapped barn (*lada*); yard; woodpile | Decided (look), Agreed (buildings) |
| For animals | A byre (*fähus*) on the farm; small hay barns out in the meadows | Agreed |
| Signs of life | The woodpile grows and shrinks with the stock; smoke thickens when cooking, in the evening and in winter; sheaf stooks (*skylar*) stand on reaped fields; herds graze near the farms | Agreed |
| Seasons on the map | A tint per season over the painted land (fresh green in spring, full in summer, ochre and russet on broadleaf and meadow in autumn, bare broadleaf in winter; pine stays green), and snow from winter day 2 to spring day 3 with lakes iced over, until snow can follow temperature (backlog VB-083) | Agreed, 30 Oct |
| Worn paths | Walking wears the ground: trodden, path and lane by crossings, fading over a year; lanes show as trodden snow. Drawn first (backlog VB-094); the faster walking decided under Land and ecology, Worn paths, follows (VB-110) | Agreed, 30 Oct |
| Farm names | Village farms by compass direction from the green (Norrgården, Södergården); lone farms (one-farm villages) by surroundings (Strand, Lunden, Backa, Myra, Ängen) | Agreed |
| Name generators | Names come from generators, several per kind of entity, and in the long run cultures prefer different generators. For farms, landmarks and owners' names combine with compass directions, so big villages need no numbers | Decided, 30 Oct |
| Pawns | Wool kirtles in undyed or plant-dyed colours, hose and turnshoes; hoods for men, and for all men in winter; a linen headcloth for married women, braids and a band for unmarried ones | Decided (good-looking), Agreed (dress) |
| Pawn size | Drawn about twice true size against buildings, so individuals read at play zoom; children smaller | Agreed |
| Animation | Walking, hoeing, broadcast sowing along the rows, reaping with a sickle, chopping, drawing water, mending fences, carrying sheaves, firewood and water | Decided |
| Empty farms | Buildings grey and decay into ruins; fields go to meadow and, near woodland (within 2 steps of forest, until forest spread exists), to scrub; full decay art comes with claims | Agreed |
| Generated villages | The village generator makes more varied and realistic villages, which look like villages that have grown in play; once worn paths exist, generated villages have them | Decided, 30 Oct |

#### Generated villages (Proposed)

- **Grown, not stamped:** the generator grows each village farm by farm by the rules of play: a household builds where a household in play would, takes fields near its house, and the village lays out a green and digs a well once it has two farms. A generated village and one grown over a run then look alike.
- **Worn in:** after the farms stand, the generator walks a few simulated years of daily trips (fields, well, wood, the neighbours) so the village starts with its paths and lanes.
- **Laid out by the land (Decided, 12 Nov, Kim; built in the generator, `src/sim/layout.js`):** a village is a green and then a farm at a time. A house stands on a tile touching the green, or hanging together with the village so far (beside one of its houses or fields); of the best such sites it takes the one whose land is best: the worth of the block of three fields it would take counts for it, and the fertility of the house's own tile against it, since a house stands on the poorer ground and spares the good, as a Swedish village sits on the stony rise beside its clay. Its fields lie no nearer the green than the house, so the green stays the centre, and the ring of tiles round the green stays open.
- **Fields beside the houses (Decided, 12 Nov, Kim):** a field may touch a house, its own or a neighbour's; there is no ring of yards kept free (the yard is drawn inside the house's own tile). A field is chosen by its fertility less 0.3 a step from the house (`FIELD_NEAR`; it was 0.15), within 4 steps. On populous small at four seeds the mean way from a house to its fields fell from 2.9 steps to 1.5, and nine houses in ten have a field touching them. Placement in play follows the same rule (built 13 Nov; `farmedLand`, `src/sim/layout.js`): a household's next field, a new meadow and a plot's field may touch a house, lie off the ring round the green and no nearer the green than their house; a plot's house stands by the generator's score. Fields made in play lie a mean 1.4 steps from their house on founders small, seed 7, over 12 years (2.8 before), and 2.1 on populous small over 8 years (2.7).
- **One form:** the irregular cluster village (*klungby*), the most common form in medieval Sweden, and lone farms (*ensamgårdar*). The laid street and its row (*radby*, the regulated villages of the plains under *solskifte*) were built with VB-113 and removed on 12 November: a village has no laid street, and its lanes wear in where its people walk. Farms differ in age, size and number of buildings.
- **Fitted to the land:** villages follow shores, slopes, rivers and the edge of the forest, and avoid stony and wet ground for houses.

### The gathering (slice 4, 10 November)

The first thing that happens between villages, and what the fair, marriages across villages and the settling of borders are latched
onto. In history the gathering came first and the market rode on it: people met for law, cult and matchmaking, and traded because they
were there (`docs/review-markets.md`, section 2.3; the *ting*, and the Disting at Uppsala, a thing, a sacrifice and a fair in one). The
rows marked Decided are Kim's of 10 November; the rest is proposed with them for slice 4's plan (VB-183), which takes over the occasions
of VB-126.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The gathering | Once a year, villages that lie near each other and think alike gather at a village's centre (a *ting*). Its purposes are to hold their customs together, to make marriages between villages, and to resolve disputes over borders; a fair is held at it (Exchange, The fair) | Decided, 10 Nov |
| The host | A village hosts, on its green. Villages compete to host | Decided, 10 Nov |
| How a village comes to host | At its autumn meeting a village with a green may offer to host; at the same meeting every village chooses the gathering it will attend that winter. The choice is counted in hours of walking: a village goes to the host with the fewest effective hours, if under 12, a day's walk (Between villages, Near and far). The walking hours between the two greens (Between villages, Distance); plus up to 4 for customs that differ (hard customs count double; struck as built, below); less half an hour for each household of kin in the villages that went there last year, at most 2; less or plus up to 2 for the disposition between the two villages (below); less 1 if it was held last year; less up to 1 for the ale its host poured. A host's own walk is 0. A village attends one gathering. A gathering that fewer than three villages choose is not held, and its villages choose again; a lone village far from any has none. As built (Proposed, VB-294, 17 Nov): the hours for customs that differ are not counted, the disposition (the holders' opinions, which carry chemistry's customs term) standing for them; a village with a green offers to host when it would go to its own gathering first; the smallest gathering under three is put out of the running (the host with fewer farms first) and its villages choose again, until each that is left has three or more; the choice is the meeting's, carried out by its alderman (VB-314; until then the meeting's command), and without a quorum the culture's practice for a meeting that does not decide applies (nothing: no gathering that winter; as last year: last year's choice) | Agreed, 10 Nov; **Under revision** (Kim, 12 Nov): the hours added for customs that differ are to be replaced by opinion between pawns whose customs differ (Balance loops and targets, Like seeks like); slice 4's tasks VB-294 and VB-296 read this before they are built; resolved as built, Proposed (VB-294, 17 Nov): the customs hours struck |
| Who gathers | Villages at most a day's walk from the host, 12 walking hours by the one measure of Between villages (Inheritance, Between villages: distance and journeys), on foot; water is not crossed, so islands gather apart for now (Kim, 10 Nov; Over the sea, there) and travel by boat waits for slice 10. And close enough in customs to wish to: a village that has split from a culture (Meetings, culture and splits) is not drawn to its old gathering | Decided, 10 Nov (near and like-minded; a day's walk; islands apart); Agreed, 10 Nov (measures) |
| When | After the harvest: winter days 1 to 3. Fields sown in spring ripen from autumn day 3 to 12 and are then dried and threshed, so these are the first days free of it: the grain is in, the autumn slaughter is done (the count is on autumn day 8), the animals stand in the byre, and the first snow lies from day 2. It comes after the village meetings (autumn day 10) and the moving round (day 11), so each village goes to the gathering it chose at its meeting a few days before, and a border claim raised at a meeting is heard the same year | Decided, 10 Nov (after the harvest; the days) |
| Going there | Pawns prepare and travel in person. The household plans it as an occasion (a kind of plan, Work): it chooses who goes, packs provisions for the days away and what it means to exchange, as loads (a back load, or a cart with oxen), and those who go walk there and back as a journey (Between villages, A journey; Provisions and the season); those who stay keep the animals and the fire | Decided, 10 Nov (prepare and travel); Proposed (details) |
| Who goes | From each household: its holder or spokesman; its unmarried of marrying age; anyone party to a dispute; others as hands for the loads and by their own wish (sociability, until traits a stand-in). Never everyone: at least one adult stays | Proposed |
| Lodging | Those who come need a place to sleep, in winter and under a roof. As at the things and fairs of the North, where guests lodged with kin and guest-friends and the rest were quartered by the hosts or raised booths, a goer sleeps: at home, if its village is within two hours' walk; else with kin in the host village; else with a pawn there it holds a goodwill balance with (its partner); else where the host village puts it, in a barn. A farm of the host village lodges up to 10 guests (4 in the house, 6 in the barn), kin and partners first. A village whose goers the host cannot lodge is turned away at the choice and goes to its next gathering, so a larger village can host a larger gathering. Lodging a guest who is not kin is booked as a small gift, 1 ration a night, to the lodging holder's credit: the start of a partnership. Booths on the green, for a fair too large for the farms, are not planned: a market place is a label and brings nothing (Towns and regions, 16 Nov) | Decided, 10 Nov (they need a place to sleep); Agreed, 10 Nov (the rule) |
| Customs drawn together | Those present meet (encounters at a venue), at most 6 new contacts a pawn a gathering, and attitudes move by contact as they do within a village, so villages that gather drift toward each other and away from those that gather elsewhere. The culture's reckoning counts a gathering's villages as tied to each other; a culture that fills more than one gathering is a candidate to split along them | Decided, 10 Nov (the purpose); Agreed, 10 Nov (the mechanism, the cap) |
| Marriages between villages | The round pairs villages by distance, and a match beyond a day's walk asks a suitor's visit (Inheritance, Between villages: Ease of mixing; The suitor's visit); the gathering adds to that and does not narrow it. Having been at the same gathering within the last 3 years counts as the visit (two villages of one gathering may lie up to 24 hours apart) and adds 0.5 to the matcher's score, where the village term is 2 at home and 0.45 at a day's walk. Spokesmen (Marriage as a negotiation) negotiate there, and a match agreed at the gathering is concluded there, by the acts the moving round uses, since the round is past. The suitor's winter visit falls in the same days, so a man looking for a wife goes to the gathering first. Courting across villages starts there too, once courting exists. Still a first version (Kim, 10 Nov), to be revamped as the negotiation weighs terms such as the dowry (VB-276) | Decided, 10 Nov (the purpose); Agreed, 10 Nov (the rule, as reconciled with Between villages; the term's 0.5 a starting value) |
| Borders | A dispute between two villages over a tile (Land, Infield, outland and wilderness) is brought to the gathering both attend and decided by the holders present from the other villages, under the Disputed claims custom of the gathering's villages; the cairns are raised after it. Two villages of different gatherings, or of none, settle it as now | Decided, 10 Nov (resolved at the gathering); Agreed, 10 Nov (who decides) |
| Disposition between villages | That others judge a border implies that villages stand in some relation to each other. It is read, never stored: the opinions and goodwill balances that the holders of one village already hold toward the holders of another (each pawn's counterparts are capped, so the reading is bounded), with the border grudges of 1 Nov among them. It leans a judge toward one party, weighs in a village's choice of gathering, and moves with what happens at one: a case lost, a match made, a fair deal or a hard one | Agreed, 10 Nov |
| The peace of the gathering | For its days no one presses a grudge: the host village's holders keep the peace and hear a quarrel over a deal on the spot. A breach is an event of the record, told in every village that was there | Proposed |
| What the host gains | Standing for the host village's holders, and for the one who presides most, by the ale they pour (Goods, Ale and standing); the trade nearest home; later, when there are obligations, a due from those who trade (slice 5 or 8). This is why villages compete, and the first step of the alderman's road (target-v2, section 7.1) | Proposed |
| The district | The villages of one gathering are a district (*bygd*, later *härad*): the first unit above the village, read from where villages go, never stored apart from that choice | Proposed |
| What the player sees | Households packing the day before; lines of walkers and carts on the paths to the host village; the green full for three days, with loads and animals set out; a report in the log (who came, what was settled, matches made, what changed hands). The player's pawn goes or stays by the player's choice and takes part in person: deals, a match for a child, a voice in a border case | Proposed |
| Cost | Named before building, on medium at about 1,000 living: the choice, a read of the table of walking hours (Between villages, Distance), no search of its own; the walk, about 220 households times 2 to 3 goers once a year, less than the moving round; encounters, at most 6 a pawn present; the gathering's days are entries in the due-queue. With the fair (Exchange, The fair) the slice claims 2 ms of the budget's 200 ms a game day, a year's mean, and no step over 60 ms on `medium-ref`; its wrap-up (VB-304) measures both | Agreed, 10 Nov |

### Towns and regions (slice 9, 16 November)

Slice 9's plan (VB-189; `docs/plans/VB-189.md`), on Kim's answers of 16 November in three sessions. Two mocks stand behind its
findings (`docs/cities/mock-town-emergence.md`): toy models, good to a factor of two, cited here as findings and never as the game's
measurements. The history behind "nobody calls a market": markets held by custom on Sundays and saints' days in England (Letters,
*Gazetteer of Markets and Fairs in England and Wales to 1516*) and landing places under a gathering's peace (Sindbæk 2009) were
checked; the Disting and the Scania herring market are from secondary accounts; Swedish fairs on church dedication days, the
*landsköp* ban and Snorri's text were **not verified**.

**What no longer stands** of the text this section held until 16 November, and of what other sections said with it: settlement
tiers that switch things on (booths, a town meeting, a culture's kind, a first row of plots at a count of market dates); a town
that grows by how often its market is held, with a list of dates its host keeps; burgage plots of a house and a yard with no
field, along a laid street from a market square; households that must buy their food; and the pull to town as a rule of the
moving round.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Markets and towns | Markets arise between villages and can grow into towns; a region holds a town or city and its villages, about two cities a map, from geography rather than a quota; alehouses mix villages | Decided, 3 Nov (Kim's description); how far the mechanics carry it before the ship: No town before the ship, below |
| A market place and a town are labels | Words read from what people do, shown and reported with hysteresis. No rule of the simulation reads them, and no threshold switches anything on: a place has measures, not a tier. Like a pawn's occupation (Goods, The mill and the loom), the label is a derived view | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim) |
| Nobody calls a market | Markets ride on gatherings. Pawns bring goods to gatherings that happen anyway (the village's meeting, the season's rite, the yearly gathering), each by its own estimate of who will come, learned from past days. No pawn, village or calendar calls a market, and no occasion is made for dealing; a day with much dealing is what the record calls a market. A granted market waits for the Crown (the ship) | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim) |
| Dealing on the green | On a gathering day dealing is on the village's green, and everyone taking part, buyer and seller, walks there with what it means to give; the fair's barter, unchanged (Exchange, The fair), among those present. A household of another village sends a goer only to deal when its estimate of that green on that kind of day is worth the walk there and back, at what an hour is worth in that season | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim); the goer's weighing Proposed (VB-769) |
| A household's estimate of a green | Its own (Principles, The future is someone's estimate): the customary worth it dealt there, by kind of day (a village's own day, a yearly gathering), blended 0.7 old to 0.3 new at each visit; at most 8 greens a household, the least visited dropped. A green never visited is judged by what the household has heard (stories, slice 7); until stories exist, at half of what its own green gives it. On its planning day before an occasion the head packs what it holds over its targets as a load, if the estimate covers the carrying | Proposed (VB-769) |
| The walk to a door | On any other day a pawn may walk to a named household to deal: a head short of a good it cannot make, who knows a household that has it within a day there and back, plans the walk on its planning day, carrying what it means to give; at the door an offer, then a swap or a refusal that both remember. A household knows at most 4 households a good, those it has dealt with or seen dealing. The walk is any pawn's act, the played pawn's among them. In the mock most dealing is at doors (60 to 77% of worth) | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim); the numbers Proposed (VB-770) |
| Rites on a village's own days | So that neighbours can attend each other's, as a parish kept its own feast day: a village holds its three rites on days of its own, and the adults of villages within the rite's reach may come (People, Where pawns meet, The season's rite; Customs, Rules of a culture). In the mock the greens' share of the makers' dealing rises from 36% to 50% | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim); the mechanism and its numbers Proposed (VB-775) |
| What a place's label reads | Measured at each year's end over the last 3 years and shown for every village: **outsiders**, the households of other villages that dealt on its green in a year; beside it **dealing days**, the gathering days on which at least 3 households dealt there; and the share of its people's work that is not farming (Balance loops and targets, Strata). A **market place** is a green where 12 or more households of other villages dealt, at 3 year ends running; it stops being one under 6, at 3 year ends running. It was the one measure that fell into two groups in the mock (under 3 at 87% of village-years, 24 and more at a gathering's host), so a market place is, before the ship, a gathering's host. **Town has no cut point** until something makes one: no measure of either mock sets a town apart, and a word is not put on a place the rules have not produced | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim: outsiders, with dealing days beside; no cut point for a town); 12, 6 and 3 Proposed, read off a toy model and waiting for the first real run (VB-771) |
| Plots | Always grown, as village houses are (Inheritance, Clearing a new plot): no laid street, no square, no plot of another kind. A plot household holds whatever land it can get; no rule says it has no fields | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim) |
| A craftsman's food | Appears by mechanics: a maker who cannot yet live by the craft keeps farming, herding, a cow, hired days. Its head sizes its fields against its own estimate of all the household's food, the craft's among it (Goods and the farming year, Fields a household wants), so a household lets fields go slowly as its craft's poor years stop frightening it; none is told to. In the mock 63 to 85% of a place's makers still hold a field, and the part-fed craftsman is the normal state | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim) |
| A landless maker asks for a plot | Who may ask for a plot elsewhere for custom's sake: the landless with a craft (a younger son, a servant), and nobody else; a maker with a farm stays on it. Among the rungs of its wish it holds "a plot at V", valued by its own estimate of its custom there (the households it has dealt with or seen dealing, shared with the makers of its craft it knows of there) against its kin where it lives, the walk to its material and the fields to be had. It asks through the offers of the reworked moving round: an offer at any time of the year, the round its due date, one village a year; the move needs the pawn's yes and the receiving head's or village's (Inheritance, Leaving home; VB-682) | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim); the weighing Proposed (VB-772) |
| No town before the ship | A finding, accepted (Kim, 16 Nov). With no market called, dealing at gatherings and at doors, and makers free to ask for a plot where their custom is best, makers spread and do not gather: the fullest village holds 10 to 22% of an island's maker households in every variant tried, a place begun gathered comes apart in a generation, and nothing locks in. An island of 1,000 ends with some 17 to 21 part-farming maker households, one to three villages with three of them, and one busy market, the three days of the yearly gathering at each district's host. So slice 9's content is village craftsmen and a yearly market at each host; the town itself waits. The third mock leaves it standing: with the planned facilities the fullest village holds 12 to 17%, and with an elite household and a ship's buyer beside them 17% | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim: accepted); the third mock's sentence Proposed (VB-776); the numbers are three toy models', good to a factor of two (`docs/cities/mock-town-emergence.md`) |
| What the town waits for | Something that gathers makers, which neither mock holds. Three candidates: a shared facility, costly to build and worth sharing (a kiln, a bloomery), which a third mock tries first (VB-776); the elite's household as buyer and patron (slice 5); and outside demand and a granted market (the ship). A third mock in which the fullest place holds a third or more of an island's makers, and still holds them after 60 years, reopens this section. **The third mock (VB-776, part three of the mock's document) did not reopen it.** A shared facility does not gather makers: the planned ones cost 0.01 to 0.15 of a household's year of spare hours and change nothing; one costing two years keeps the landless out of the craft, so the island has fewer makers and the fullest village 22% of them, the same three households; its capacity never binds, since the makers of one craft at a place share one custom; and its builder's heir ends as a collector of dues. An elite household wanting 5 to 20 farms' goods adds its own custom and no more (15%). A ship's buyer on known days feeds the makers within half a day of the harbour, not the harbour (12 to 17%). What gathered makers in the toy was demand at a point that only those who live there can serve, of the order of all that the island's farms give its makers (36 to 41%). So the town waits for the ship, and the ship's plan chooses whether its demand is a point or a district: the facility is no longer a candidate, and slice 5's household alone is not one | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim: the third mock, as a task); the reopening test and the third mock's reading Proposed (VB-776; a toy model's numbers, good to a factor of two) |
| Crowding | The urban penalty is crowding, wherever people live close, not a flag on town people: Balance loops and targets, Deaths | Decided, 16 Nov (Kim) |
| Service, lodging and marriage in a town | Left as a shape: a head short of hands for its craft offers service, a lodging is a basis of the offer, and "Marriage in town" may be a custom. Each needs a place with households short of hands, which nothing yet produces. The standing alehouse and booths on a green wait likewise; no label brings them | Proposed, a skeleton |
| Region and market potential | Reported, never rules: a village's market is the green where its households dealt most over 3 years, and a region is the villages of one such green; market potential (the people in reach weighted by walking hours, Harris 1954) is shown beside it as an explanation that places nothing. Handed to the plans of slices 8 and 10, which change reach | Proposed (VB-189); replaces "Settlement tier and regions" (target-v2, section 4.6, which still says tier: VB-777) |
| Town against country | Whether trade outside a town is proper is a custom for the ship's Crown to press; nothing before it | Proposed, a skeleton |
| Cost | Named before building, at about 1,000 living on medium: a head's memory of its food, 56 numbers a household, added where a food operation is booked; crowding, a compiled factor a household refreshed when a house is founded, left or rebuilt, nothing a day; dealing on a green, about 2,000 proposals a rite day spread over its hours, under 0.1 ms a game day over the year; some 300 walks to a door a year; the label and the reports, yearly, outside the simulation. The slice claims 1 ms a game day of the budget, a year's mean; its wrap-up (VB-774) measures it on `medium-ref` and `small-y40` | Proposed (VB-189) |

## 12. Institutions and the world beyond

Not built yet; slice 8 writes this section (backlog VB-188).

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Rank | Only outside institutions grant rank. Before contact there is an implicit Crown (the landed; a landed big man may act as arbiter) and an implicit Church (each pawn's spiritual life; an implicit priest in a town) | Decided, 3 Nov |
| The outside world | Late in the game, limited contact with an abstract outside world: a port draws merchant ships that sell exotic goods and buy in bulk; diplomats and a few immigrants arrive | Decided, 3 Nov (Kim's description) |
| Immigration | Very limited immigration by boat, in a later stage | Decided (v0.3); Decided, 13 Nov (Kim): none before the ship, and nobody appears who has not travelled there (Balance loops and targets, The outside world); who comes and goes, and how many, is slice 8's plan's (VB-188; the settling families, VB-636) |
| Pawns abroad | Keep their identity and ties and follow the same rules at a coarse cadence; a merchant who returns next summer is the same pawn | Proposed (target-v2, section 4.5) |
| Disease | Ships bring sickness, and towns crowd it; the largest gap in the design (target-v2, section 10.3) | Open |

## 13. Balance loops and targets

Slices 0 and 9 write this section (backlog VB-181, VB-189); the loops and their probes are listed in target-v2, section 6.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Balance points | Many systems balance themselves, and tuning the game means tuning their balance points: peasant fertility is held in check by taxation and by higher death rates in crowded towns | Decided, 3 Nov (Kim's description) |
| Population | About 1,000 living pawns on a medium map (see Vision and scope) | Decided, 3 Nov |
| Growth | The island grows without the player. Yearly growth of a run is `(end / start)^(1 / years) − 1`. Over a scenario's seeds: the median seed grows at least 1% a year; at least three seeds in four end above their start; at most one in eight ends below half its start. Not every seed need grow. Founders is held to it as the early reference; populous while land is free (the loops hold it after); heirs is reported without a target | Decided, 12 Nov (1%, most seeds, the references); Agreed, 12 Nov (the three numbers) |
| Pace | The pace the game aims for is a band that is reported, beside the target above and never in its place: about 2% a year while land is free, slowing as homes grow scarce, and held near 1,000 by the marriage valve. By births alone ten founders reach 1,000 in about 230 years (200 to 300); the ship's settling families shorten that (The growth budget, below) | Decided, 13 Nov (Kim: births alone until the ship; the plan restates the pace); the numbers, VB-405's plan, 16 Nov |
| What is judged | Island-wide numbers only. Hunger presses (Vision and scope, Principles), so measures of single households (the hungriest household, the share of households hungry, hunger's share of deaths) are reported as descriptions and have no target | Decided, 12 Nov |
| Collapse and tuning | A collapse is a reference scenario whose median seed shrinks; it may be tuned away at once. Anything short of a target but not a collapse is reported and left: parameters are tuned together, for gameplay balance, in runs Kim starts. A formula that computes something other than it says is a fix, not tuning | Decided, 12 Nov |
| Strata | 600 farming, 325 in towns and specialised work, 75 elite, per 1,000, at the end game; open to challenge. A band for a developed island, not a quota. **Counted by work**, in full-time equivalents of hours worked, with heads beside it (households by their relation to production: elite by the class view, the rest by The ladder). On the work line all craft hours count as work that is not farming, a household's own dress and tools included, with carrying to deal, dealing and service; farming is fields, hay, herds, fishing, hunting, gathering and their carrying; the elite's hours are those of an elite household's members. **Household work is reported apart**, outside the three figures: cooking, water, firewood, grinding at the quern, building and upkeep (with grinding and building in, the middle figure reads 403 before anyone specialises). So the middle figure reads how much of the island's working time is not spent getting food, not how many specialists there are: about 239 from every farm's own crafts once slice 6's goods exist, 263 to 275 with the makers of the second mock, near 0 today. It falls as work specialises (a master weaves in 71 hours what a novice weaves in 153), so the report gives beside it the share of non-farm hours whose product left the household, which rises. **The target is not expected to be met before the ship**: 325 needs about half as much craft work again as a farm's own, which an elite's building and service and the ship's demand may add, and the 75 needs slice 5's elite. The target stands until Kim changes it; the report prints the measure, the target and the residual every night and never fails | Decided (the target); Decided, 16 Nov (Kim: by work, all craft hours, heads beside; the orchestrator: household work apart, a close call); the figures are a toy model's (`docs/cities/mock-town-emergence.md`); a band's width, 550 to 700 / 250 to 400 / 50 to 100, is Proposed and not applied (VB-765). The food it needs: The balance review, below (12 Nov) |
| Bands | Full-simulation bands are reported nightly and never fail; reduced scenarios (a tiny map, a few years) are asserted and may fail outside their band | Decided, 3 Nov |
| Loops | About twenty loops, each declared with its stock, probe, setpoints and bands, calibrated fast loops first | Proposed (target-v2, section 6) |

### The balance review (12 November 2026)

A session with Kim on 12 November went through the game's built and planned systems to find where the design falls short of the vision,
collected the mechanisms as cards, and tested a draft map of them with eight experiments: five mock models, measurements on the game,
a survey of abstractions, and accounts built at the target. The cards, the findings and what remains to do are in `docs/balance/`
(`README.md` first). Rows marked Decided are Kim's statements of that day; Agreed were proposed in the session and accepted; Proposed and
Open are neither. The experiments' findings are evidence, not decisions: where they contradict a point of this document, that point is
marked Under revision and names the task that settles it. Mock numbers mix the game's rules with invented ones.

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The balance map | One static diagram that shows what holds the game in balance at the macro scale: which mechanisms act on which balance properties, in what way (a steady push, a loop that balances, a loop that reinforces), how strongly and how fast. It serves development and tuning. Mechanisms are collected as cards, each checked against the design and the code; the details stay in the cards and the map shows the important ones | Decided, 12 Nov |
| Its form | A causal loop diagram of properties and levers (drafts 1 to 3) is not enough: only 3 of its 40 links could be written as a model from their sign. The map needs seven kinds of thing: stocks with target bands, flows that add up, gates with thresholds, setpoints (what is tuned), conditions (not tuned in a game), processes between households, and consequences. Proposed shape: the people stocks and the food split in the centre, conditions above, a strip of eras below, a table of dials as legend (`docs/balance/README.md`) | Proposed, 12 Nov (after the experiments) |
| Balance properties | Population; food produced (a farming household's output over its need) and the share of it extracted; food kept a head; goodwill in circulation; idle hours; the town and craft share; the elite share; land concentration; the spread of customs between villages. The 600 / 325 / 75 split, the population and land concentration are the three Kim named first; "a lot of things" besides | Decided (the three), Agreed (the list, a draft), 12 Nov |
| Where a farm's food goes | Eaten, taken (rent, tithe, tax), feasted, sold, or it rots. The split is the map's main dial: feasted food feeds goodwill and standing, sold food the town, taken food the elite, and rot nothing. A herd is an outlet too: fodder and hay become an animal that keeps, and it is designed with the customs of the animals (VB-141). The split is a property that is reported, not a target: of the grain harvest, the shares sown, eaten, brewed, feasted, given, put in the village store and rotted, island-wide and a year (VB-403's report). Measured today: of the grain harvest about a third is seed, 26 to 30% is eaten, about 22% rots, 8% is brewed, 8% is given | Agreed, 12 Nov; measured the same day |
| Food produced | The 600 / 325 / 75 split is deliberate: a smaller farming base than history's, so that a 1,000-person game holds enough specialists and elite to play among. It needs each farmer to produce 1.67 times a farmer's need, and the base yield may have to be higher than that for the island to hold robustly. The review's accounts put the need at 1.7 to 2.15 by how much rots and is feasted, against 1.64 to 1.86 measured counting fish, meat, milk and gathered food | Decided, 12 Nov (deliberate; may exceed 1.67); the numbers Proposed |
| Yield | The foundation under food produced: at high yields farm work leaves hands free, at low yields it takes them all. Measured: seed to yield falls from 3.7 at year 10 to 2.9 at year 50 as soil wears. Noted as a foundational setpoint | Agreed, 12 Nov |
| Idle labour | Idle hours are the labour from which land-owners draw their share: one use of them is work on the land-owning class's farms. Labour and time must be left for a household's own subsistence after that share is taken. Measured: farmers are idle 16 to 33% of working hours in summer and autumn and 59 to 70% in winter, and walking and carrying take 30 to 47% outside winter, so the peaks have little to give | Decided, 12 Nov; measured the same day |
| Fewer dedicated specialists | That farmers' slack-season hours make part of the goods, so that fewer full-time specialists are needed than 325, is welcome | Decided, 12 Nov |
| Servants and retinue | Counted by their own class: servants are peasants; the members of a retinue are specialists (burghers, townspeople) or nobles | Decided, 12 Nov |
| The limit on extraction | Tuned rates (the rent share, a loan's interest, the alderman's keep, as practices the meeting can move) with self-regulation behind them (the squeezed marry later and die more, and those who pay pull the practices down); the floor (the seed and 20 days of need) is the one hard limit. What a tenant can bear: the review puts a full tenant below need at a rent of 0.39 of its grain in a median year and 0.26 in a one-in-four bad year, and the claims designed for the whole game together (rent, tithe, tax, interest, tolls) at 38% of its output. Slice 5's claims on a full tenant are a rent of a quarter, just under the bad year's limit, and nothing else that bites in a bad year: the keep and a patron's due are days of work or a share of a surplus it then does not have. So slice 8's tithe and tax cannot be added to a quarter's rent on a full tenant; its plan (VB-188) fits them under 0.26 together or moves the rent | Proposed, 12 Nov; the tenant's limit stated by VB-404's plan, 15 Nov |
| Pull, then push | What brings a surplus out of farms that aim at their own need: first the wish for goods only trade gives (the fair, slice 4), before any elite exists; later rent, tithe and tax, which hold output up. In the review's model the wish for goods built the town (301 people with it, 103 without). The mocks of 16 November, with nobody calling a market and makers free to move, find that the wish for goods feeds part-farming craftsmen spread over the villages and a yearly market at each host, not a town (Settlements, Towns and regions) | Agreed, 12 Nov; the mocks' finding, 16 Nov |
| Keeping | Mechanisms that lengthen a food's life raise the effective yield late in the game. Nothing changes before facilities: a granary (slice 6) halves `ECON.storeLoss` for the household that builds one, and fired pots keep a store from damp and mice (VB-366); salting and drying stay as built. Their side effect: rot is also what stops hoarding, so better keeping means larger stores, more to feast and more to lend, and the task that builds a granary reports stores, feasts and loans before and after | Decided, 13 Nov (Kim; VB-407) |
| Land use | Not a tunable of the late game: a mature economy uses most of the map (about 95%), and the spread of people between farming and specialised work sets how. Measured: growth slows when land within a day's walk of a village runs out, with 14% of a small map in houses and fields at year 50; the target of 1,000 on a medium map needs about 5% of it as fields and meadows. What fills the rest, and what holds the farmers at 600, is Open | Decided, 12 Nov (not a tunable); Open (the rest) |
| Reach | Reach by water (boats, ice, roads) is not a lever. Proposed reading: reach, on foot and by water, is a condition that sets where the limits sit (the land a village can use, a town's catchment, who can marry whom) | Decided, 12 Nov (not a lever); Proposed (a condition) |
| Population: what holds it | Births and deaths carry growth. Marriage is wanted as a balancing factor, and nursing is to be shown as an important one, the part that regulates itself. Hunger feeds disease, which lowers growth. Measured and modelled: marriage already holds the population by delay, hunger touches under 1% of households, and the means a home must hold before a couple may marry (`MARRIAGE.means`) is the setpoint most late properties follow. How the valve works (the gate, the means as its setpoint, what moves the age and the share never marrying, service, nursing) is in People, Marriage, nursing and growth; which brakes hold 1,000, and at what cost, in The growth budget, below | Decided, 12 Nov (the levers); Decided, 13 Nov (Kim: the gate and its setpoint; the mix of brakes); VB-405's plan, 16 Nov |
| Deaths | Disease rises with crowding in towns and cities, so that towns are sinks of population and the countryside its source; poor health and poor nourishment raise the risk. Disease is not designed (target-v2, 10.3). The review's mock: the town does not slow growth, since its migrants are the village's unmarried; it decides where people live. In the mix chosen the town is the second brake, at about 1.5 times the farms' death rate, and disease is designed for the town only (The growth budget, below); slice 8's plan designs disease (VB-188) and slice 9's the town as a sink (VB-189), each against its line of the budget. **The urban penalty is crowding** (Kim, 16 Nov), wherever people live close, and no rule reads whether a place is a town: a household's crowding is the people living in houses within 2 hex steps of its house, its own among them, and its members' hazard is multiplied by 1 + `CROWD.per` 0.035 for each beyond `CROWD.free` 36, at most `CROWD.max` 2.5; it stands in for disease until disease exists. Measured on populous small: a house has a median of 14 to 17 people within 2 steps and a 99th percentile of 31 to 41, so almost no village house pays it and the densest of a large old village pays a little (seed 7, year 40: 52 people, a factor of 1.56); six plot households close together would pay about 1.5, the town's "about 1.5 times". With no town before the ship, the valve carries the town's line of the budget until then | Decided, 12 Nov (the aim); Decided, 13 Nov (Kim: the town second, at about 1.5 times; disease for the town only); Open (the disease model); Decided, 16 Nov (Kim: crowding), the three numbers Proposed (VB-766) |
| The urban sink | A place's people settle near `M / (d − b)`, its yearly migrants over its deaths less its births per head, and cannot pass `S / c`, the food that reaches it from outside over a person's need; both are reported for every village by decade, with which binds. In the mock neither binds a place before its makers' shared custom does (19 to 36 people at the fullest place), but on a full island, where the farms' grain binds first | Proposed (target-v2, section 6.2; VB-765) |
| Births | Hunger, disease and crowding lower fertility; varied food raises it, through health. From the reading of that day, settled by VB-405's plan (People, Marriage, nursing and growth): an infant's death ends the nursing gap (VB-632); service delays marriage, since a servant has no home to marry into (VB-634); miscarriage and stillbirth are left out; deaths by season wait for disease (VB-188). Disease, crowding and varied food acting on fertility wait for health and disease (slice 8) | Decided, 12 Nov (the aim); Decided, 13 Nov (Kim: nursing, service); VB-405's plan, 16 Nov (the rest) |
| The outside world | Mostly fixed, with some small adaptation. Immigration and emigration are very limited: a merchant may visit, a rich elite may arrive with a retinue, a family of another culture may settle; people leave when an elite recruits young adults for service, or for better chances elsewhere. There is no immigration until boats from the world beyond arrive, late in the mid-game (slice 8), and nobody appears on the island or in a village without having travelled there; until then the island grows by births alone, and settling families are the ship's (VB-636; the number is slice 8's plan's to confirm). The review's mock needed one settling family every 2 years to reach 1,000 in 150 years; founders alone reached about 100 by then | Decided, 12 Nov; Decided, 13 Nov (Kim: none before the ship); the number Proposed (VB-188) |
| Hunger to mobility | The vision: hunger drives the economy of gifts and goodwill, which drives peasants upward into a landed elite through land and standing, and other forces later balance that push. In the design goodwill reaches neither standing nor land; the designed road is debt (slice 5). In the review's mocks debt gave almost no elite at the game's food ratio, office and a tithe gave one of the target's size, and the goodwill road carried almost nothing (VB-404) | Decided, 12 Nov (the vision); the roads Open |
| Patronage | Where kin help falls short a rich household gives (a household of the village above the feast's threshold is asked after kin, before the village store); the goodwill joins the gift and the debt on one scale and gives idle labour a road to the larger farms. A lasting tie, since as first worded the owed work was cleared in a year or two and was negligible in the review's mock: while a household owes a patron more than 30 days of its need (`PATRON.above`), it gives 2 days of work at the patron's harvest each year (`PATRON.days`) and its holder leans to the patron's side in a vote; the days do not pay the balance down. The tie ends when the balance decays or is repaid, so goodwill's halving in 4 years is its brake | Agreed, 12 Nov; the lasting tie Decided (Kim), 13 Nov |
| Goodwill and coin | Two circuits, not one replacing the other: goodwill among people who know each other, coin with strangers. Coin opens trade with strangers; it does not shrink the room for goodwill directly. Silver thins reciprocity by competing for the same surplus: grain sold to pay a tax in silver is grain not feasted | Agreed, 12 Nov |
| Feasts and good-year gifts | A driver of the goodwill economy: a household builds goodwill and standing in a good year by feasting and giving, so that generosity is a strategy and not only an answer to hunger. The rules are in Exchange, goodwill and obligations (The gift: The feast, The harvest home, What a feast books, The good-year gift). In the review's mock feasting mostly replaced rot (with it off, rot took 41% of output at a food ratio of 1.67) and slightly sped land concentration (18 estates against 15): accepted, since generosity is meant to be a road to standing (VB-404, the road of office), and what holds it is goodwill's decay and debtors' politics; the count of estates is reported in slice 5's band | Decided, 12 Nov (the driver); the rules Decided, 13 Nov (VB-407) |
| If the ships stop | No collapse of money is designed: silver's worth is a norm that follows what silver buys, so it weakens by itself. Kim has no strong view | Proposed, 12 Nov |
| Cultures | About two distinct cultures on a map. They differ most from the middle to the late game and are much more alike in the very late game. Some scenarios start with different customs. Hardship pushes on customs directly (a famine on who eats first, land running out on how many heirs). Measured: after 50 years villages differ on about 1 of 14 customs, 29 to 46% of changes undo the one before, and no run split. The review's mock: differences need a seed (a different start, or different land), and the rise and fall appeared only with poorer land on one island and attitudes pulled toward the practice in force, which the game lacks | Decided, 12 Nov (the aims); the mechanisms Open |
| Like seeks like | Works through opinion: pawns whose customs differ think less of each other, and a lower opinion means less contact and fewer marriages across the divide. It replaces the hours added for differing customs in a village's choice of gathering (The gathering). In the review's mock the opinion link is a switch, not a dial: it keeps a difference that exists and does not make one | Decided, 12 Nov; the switch a finding |

### The growth budget (the yardstick; the plan of VB-405, 16 November)

Growth is a budget: what births could give with nothing in the way, and the brakes that take it away. Whatever holds the population
near 1,000 must take away the whole of it, and each way of doing that costs people something different. This is the yardstick a
change to births, marriage, deaths or the town is measured against: it says which line it moves. The points are those of the review's
cohort mock, run with the game's numbers (`docs/balance/findings.md`, Demography mock; Budgets at the target; the figure
`docs/balance/figures/growth-budget.png`): sizes and directions are findings, single figures are not, and the lines are far from
additive (the town takes 0.05 alone and 1.00 added last). The mix is Kim's (13 November).

| Line | Points of yearly growth | What it is in the game |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The ceiling | +2.40 | Every woman married at 18, nobody hungry, deaths by the age curve, a year's nursing after every birth: a doubling in 29 years. An infant's death ending the nursing adds about half a point (VB-632 measures it), and the marriage lines below take it up |
| Marriage, by delay | −0.89 | The wait for a holding, for the means and in service (People, Marriage, nursing and growth). The lever's range: 2.0 points from 18 to 28 |
| Never marrying | −0.66 | Those who never come to a place. The lever's range: 1.5 points at 35% |
| The town | −0.54 | The young and unmarried who move to it, and its deaths at about 1.5 times the farms' (slice 9). It takes nothing from the rate by itself, since its migrants are those the gate already holds: it decides where people live. Its deaths are crowding's (The balance review, Deaths); no town is expected before the ship (Settlements, Towns and regions, 16 Nov), and until one exists the valve carries this line (The mix, below) |
| Disease | −0.28 | Designed for the town only (slice 8 brings it, slice 9 crowds it) |
| Hunger | −0.16 | A rare event of bad years, not a standing condition: hungry households stay near today's 1% |
| Those who arrive, less those who leave | +0.13 | The ship's settling families, the recruited and those who leave (slice 8); nothing before the ship |
| Net, at about 1,000 | 0 | The lines add up to the ceiling. Marriage is the one that answers by itself, so a line that falls short is made up by the two marriage lines |

| Aspect | Design | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The mix | Late marriage and service first, the town's deaths second, hunger rare, disease for the town only. At the target the valve then stands at about women marrying at 23 to 26 and 15 to 25% never marrying, by how much the town and disease take; before the town exists the valve carries the town's line too. These are what the mix implies, reported in the band, and are neither set nor targets | Decided, 13 Nov (Kim: the mix); the reading, VB-405's plan |
| What the mix costs | The cost is paid in years waited and in lives never begun, not in deaths: in the mock about 38% of children die before adulthood and life expectancy at birth is about 33, near today's game (a third die before 15), and a quarter of adults never marry. The alternatives the review weighed: held by hunger alone about half of all children die, at a standing hunger just under the point where marriage stops altogether; held by marriage at 18 alone about half never marry; a town at 3.3 times the farms' death rate drops life expectancy to 23 and takes half the children | Decided, 13 Nov (Kim); the review's numbers |
| The pace | By phase, reported and not a target. **While land is free** within a day's walk of the villages: about 2% a year (1.5 to 2.4; measured on populous small, 1.2 to 2.2% over 50 years, which run into the next phase). **As homes grow scarce:** between 1 and 2%, falling. **Held:** near 0 at about 1,000 on a medium map, in waves. By births alone ten founders reach 100 at about year 115 and 1,000 at about year 230 at 2% (200 years at the ceiling, 310 at 1.5%), so 150 years, which needs 3.1%, is out of reach before the ship; a populous island starts at 800 to 950 and passes 1,000 within its first decades. At 38 minutes a game year at 1×, 230 years are about 49 hours at 3×, inside the run of 40 to 80 hours. The target stays the Growth row's 1% for the median seed | Decided, 13 Nov (Kim: a band of years, reported; births alone until the ship); the restated numbers, VB-405's plan |
| Founders, measured against it | A pool of ten is held by its size before any brake of the budget acts (People, Marriage, nursing and growth: Founders): the nightly band's sixteen seeds had a median of 2.2% a year over 50 years (0.9 to 3.1%; 12 Nov), and seeds 3, 7, 11 and 42 over 80 years gave 0 to 2.6%, the middle near 1.6%, as pools closed. So a founders' island by births alone passes 1,000 between about year 210 and year 300, some seeds stand still, and the ship matters most to the islands whose pool closed. Reported by the band; the levers left are Kim's (how many founders) and slice 8's (when the ship comes) | Measured, 16 Nov; VB-405's plan |
| The overshoot | A balance held by marriage does not return smoothly: after a shock (a famine, a sickness) the marriages put off are made together, their children marry together a generation on, and the population overshoots its level by 3.5 to 4% sixty to seventy years later and echoes for a generation (the mock; a balance held by hunger or disease returns smoothly in about 50 years). It is wanted: a visible wave of a generation, as history had, and small against the 20% between seeds. Nothing damps it; the band reports it | Decided, 13 Nov (Kim) |
| The band | Nightly, reported and never asserted (`tests/lib/bands.js`; VB-633): growth by phase, the year 1,000 is reached, the age at first marriage by sex, the share unmarried at 45, the years waited by what stood in the way, and the widest swing of growth between decades | VB-405's plan |


## 14. Starting scenarios

There are several starting scenarios; the base and default one is the 10 founders, and unless a scenario says otherwise it starts with as few fields and meadows already made as possible, so the first year goes to making them. (Decided) Founders is the reference for the early game and populous for the late game (Decided, 12 Nov).

| Scenario | Start | Purpose | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Founders (base, default) | 10 unrelated founders: four couples, a farmhand and a maid, in two villages: a village of three farms and a lone farm, which is a village of one (Kim, 11 Nov; as built). The two differ in their crops (Goods and the farming year, The scenarios' crops); houses already standing, next to no fields or meadows made; a year's food and seed brought along; the land mass it needs scales with the map size. The marriage limit starts at “first cousins or further”, since the founders' grandchildren are mostly cousins | The normal game | Decided |
| Heirs and empty farms | Two established villages, three generations, adult heirs and younger siblings, an empty farm and a ruin | Exploring inheritance, expansion and customs | Decided |
| Populous Villages | Only villages, in three sizes: small 2–5 farms (about 60% of villages), medium 6–10 (about 30%), large 12–16 (about 10%). Households of 4–9 people, about 6: a couple, their children, sometimes a servant; children 2–3 years apart, the eldest up to 26, so about a third of households have a child of 18 or more (backlog VB-081; Agreed, 30 Oct). At most two generations made, no grandparents on record. 130–170 people on Tiny, 320–380 on Small, 800–950 on Medium and Large; the number is a target, and a map with too few sites starts with the villages it holds (Kim, 15 Nov) | A high population from the start | Decided (scenario, population), Agreed (village and household sizes) |
| The market town (a test bed) | A populous island with one place set dense: plot households close together at the village with most people in reach. It is known to come apart within a generation (the second mock: 4.6 maker households at year 10, 1.4 at year 60), and is **not evidence that a town emerges** | Measuring crowding and the place's label on a dense place; not offered at the start | Proposed, 16 Nov (the orchestrator, a close call; VB-778) |

All scenarios build their villages and households through the same generators; a scenario only chooses what to build and where (Decided).

How a scenario is chosen and shown, and the story archetypes it names, are in The player, commands and views, Starting a game (11 November).

### The founders' first year (Proposed, partly Decided)

- **Spring:** claim and break the first fields in time to sow; a field not ready before the sowing window closes waits a year. A household claims a meadow tile and makes it a field when it needs one (Decided).
- **Summer:** find, claim and clear hay meadows, and make hay for the first winter.
- **Autumn:** a first, probably small harvest; decide how many animals to keep.
- **Stores to bring:** food for a full year plus seed, since the first harvest may be small (Decided), and a hay stock for the first winter (Proposed).
- **Houses:** the founders arrive to houses already standing (Decided).

### Heirs and empty farms

| Village | Farm | Household | What it tests |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A, the culture's centre | 1 | Couple aged 61 and 58; children 29, 25 and 20 | Eldest inherits; younger two want out |
| A | 2 | Widow aged 54; only child a daughter of 22 | Heiress; widow's rights |
| A | 3 | Empty for 6 years after a couple and their baby died | Claims; decay |
| A | 4 | Couple aged 33 and 30; children 8 and 5 | A minor heir and guardian |
| B, remote, ideally across water | 1 | Couple aged 52 and 49; children 27, 24, 20 and 16 | Sons first against any child; landless eldest daughter |
| B | 2 | A ruin, empty for 15 years | Rebuilding; scrub |
| B | 3 | Childless bachelor aged 47 | Inheritance by a younger nephew |

- **Three generations on record**, with the founding grandparents dead.
- **One marriage between the villages** in the parents' generation, so first cousins exist across both and the marriage limit matters.
- **Village B leans differently** on some customs: any child inherits, handover at death, looser claims, a wider voice.
- **Debug tools:** skip one or ten years; hold the meetings now; run the moving round; speed up drift tenfold or a hundredfold; switch natural deaths; kill or retire someone; make someone argue for an option, or rally a fifth of a village's voice.

## 15. The player, commands and views
